v [SOURCE: Page_6]

Summary The Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS) is a critical multi-layered aquifer shared by Namibia, Botswana, and South Africa. It serves as a key water resource for the region, prompting UNESCO and local stakeholders to initiate studies for its sustainable management. This PhD is a continuation of the prior work in STAS with an ultimate goal of developing an integrated hydrological model (IHM). The first objective of this PhD was to develop a hydrogeological conceptual model of STAS. Six hydrostratigraphic units composed of four aquifers, including a new one (the Dwyka), and two aquitards were identified. The analysis revealed that groundwater in STAS flows in two directions, indicating presence of a regional groundwater divide. Hydrostratigraphic and 3D geometry work also led to a 42% increase of the size of STAS compared to the boundary initially delineated by UNESCO. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Since precipitation is the main driving force of IHMs, the second objective involved evaluating the performance of thirteen gridded precipitation products (GPPs) for the whole of Botswana and Namibia in terms of their rainfall detection capability, quantitative accuracy as well as bias decomposition, in order to select the most appropriate for this study. The results showed that Multi-Source Weighted-Ensemble Precipitation (MSWEP) performed best based on all the criteria used. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Groundwater models and IHMs are still commonly evaluated using in situ measurements of groundwater levels and stream discharge. However, such measurements are often scarce and do not represent basin scale groundwater dynamics subjecting the models to parametric uncertainties. Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment (GRACE) provides monthly terrestrial water storage anomalies relative to a long-term mean and its potential value in IHMs lies in its ability to constrain storage changes simulated by IHM. The third objective of this PhD was to utilize GRACE terrestrial water storage (TWS) in addition to groundwater levels to constrain the IHM of STAS through a multi-objective optimization criterion. It was found that change in storage in the unsaturated zone was the major component of subsurface change in water storage of STAS as most of the infiltrated water is stored in there where it eventually evapotranspirates, and thus, unsaturated zone parameters play a major role of controlling subsurface fluxes. However, since most studies do not incorporate state variables for the unsaturated zone, optimising these parameters is quite challenging and therefore the values for these parameters are often obtained from literature and not optimised during calibration. Inclusion of GRACE TWS as an additional state variable of the IHM of STAS showed that not optimizing the UZF parameters could lead to under-estimation of gross and net recharge. It was also observed that using GRACE TWS alone to constrain an IHM poses a risk of over-estimating both gross and net recharge. It is therefore important to use GRACE TWS together with other state variables such as groundwater measurements or actual evapotranspiration to constrain the IHM. [SOURCE: Page_7]

vi [SOURCE: Page_7]

Resume Le système aquifère transfrontalier de Stampriet (STAS) est un aquifère multicouche essentiel partagé par la Namibie, le Botswana et l'Afrique du Sud. Il constitue une ressource en eau essentielle pour la région, ce qui a incité l'UNESCO et les parties prenantes locales à lancer des études en vue de sa gestion durable. Ce doctorat s'inscrit dans la continuité des travaux antérieurs menés dans le cadre de STAS, avec pour objectif final le développement d'un modèle hydrologique intégré (IHM). Le premier objectif de ce doctorat était de développer un modèle conceptuel hydrogéologique de la STAS. Six unités hydrostratigraphiques composées de quatre aquifères, dont un nouveau (le Dwyka), et de deux aquitards ont été identifiées. L'analyse a révélé que les eaux souterraines de la STAS s'écoulent dans deux directions, ce qui indique la présence d'une ligne de partage des eaux souterraines à l'échelle régionale. Les travaux hydrostratigraphiques et de géométrie 3D ont également permis d'augmenter de 42 % la taille de la STAS par rapport à la limite initialement délimitée par l'UNESCO. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Les précipitations étant la principale force motrice des IHM, le deuxième objectif consistait à évaluer les performances de treize produits de précipitations maillés (GPP) pour l'ensemble du Botswana et de la Namibie en termes de capacité de détection des précipitations, de précision quantitative et de décomposition des biais, afin de sélectionner le plus approprié pour cette étude. Les résultats ont montré que le Multi-Source Weighted-Ensemble Precipitation (MSWEP) était le plus performant sur la base de tous les critères utilisés. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Les modèles d'eaux souterraines et les IHM sont encore couramment évalués à l'aide de mesures in situ du niveau des eaux souterraines et du débit des cours d'eau. Cependant, ces mesures sont souvent rares et ne représentent pas la dynamique des eaux souterraines à l'échelle du bassin, ce qui soumet les modèles à des incertitudes paramétriques. L'expérience GRACE (Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment) fournit des anomalies mensuelles de stockage de l'eau terrestre par rapport à une moyenne à long terme et sa valeur potentielle dans les IHM réside dans sa capacité à contraindre les changements de stockage simulés par les IHM. Le troisième objectif de ce doctorat était d'utiliser le stockage d'eau terrestre (TWS) de GRACE en plus des niveaux d'eau souterraine pour contraindre l'IHM de STAS à travers un critère d'optimisation multi-objectif. Il a été constaté que le changement de stockage dans la zone non saturée était la principale composante du changement de stockage d'eau de subsurface de la STAS, car la majeure partie de l'eau infiltrée est stockée dans cette zone où elle finit par s'évapotranspirer, et les paramètres de la zone non saturée jouent donc un rôle majeur dans le contrôle des flux de subsurface. Cependant, comme la plupart des études n'intègrent pas de variables d'état pour la zone non saturée, l'optimisation de ces paramètres est assez difficile et, par conséquent, les valeurs de ces paramètres sont souvent obtenues à partir de la littérature et ne sont pas optimisées lors de l'étalonnage. L'inclusion de GRACE TWS en tant que variable d'état supplémentaire de l'IHM de STAS a montré que la non- optimisation des paramètres UZF pouvait conduire à une sous-estimation de la recharge brute et nette. Il a également été observé que l'utilisation de GRACE TWS seul pour contraindre un [SOURCE: Page_8]

vii [SOURCE: Page_8]

IHM présente un risque de surestimation de la recharge brute et nette. Il est donc important d'utiliser GRACE TWS avec d'autres variables d'état telles que les mesures des eaux souterraines ou l'évapotranspiration réelle pour contraindre l'IHM. [SOURCE: Page_9]

viii [SOURCE: Page_9]

- Summary: Technical section focusing on 1.     general introduction
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

---
### 1.     General Introduction

1.1. Background Groundwater is the most abundant source of fresh water on the globe, and much of it is locked up in transboundary aquifers (TBAs) that straddle the territory of two or more countries (Guo 2018; IGRAC 2021). Sustainable development and management of transboundary aquifers is critical in reducing the risk of conflicts between member countries (Uitto 2004). This calls for an established cooperation among member states which requires a multifaceted approach that improves the knowledge base, develops capacities for cooperation including understanding of legal instruments and frameworks, and supports regional mechanisms to operationalize cooperative arrangements (IGRAC and UNESCO-IHP 2015). However, management of transboundary aquifers is complicated by lack of accurate data and where it exists, it is often not shared among the respective countries (Nijsten et al. 2018). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Recognizing the importance of groundwater as a source of fresh water and the lack of understanding about TBAs, UNESCO International Hydrological Programme (IHP) and the International Association of Hydrogeologists (IAH) launched the Internationally Shared Aquifer Resources Management (ISARM) initiative in 2000 (UNESCO 2001). ISARM has launched a number of global and regional activities since its inception such as delineation and assessment of transboundary aquifer systems (UNESCO-IHP 2009; IGRAC 2021) and advocating for a multidisciplinary methodological approach to transboundary aquifer assessment, encouraging member states to collaborate toward mutually beneficial and sustainable aquifer development. It has also played a significant role in the establishment of legislative frameworks for better transboundary aquifer management (UNESCO-IHP 2009; IGRAC and UNESCO-IHP 2015). [SOURCE: Page_22]

The Governance of Groundwater Resources in Transboundary Aquifers (GGRETA) project (2013- 2022), was one component of the ISARM initiative. It was implemented by the UNESCO International Hydrological Programme (UNESCO-IHP). The GGRETA initiative aimed to strengthen collaboration on water security, prevent transboundary and water-use conflicts, and increase overall environmental sustainability. GGRETA had three pilot studies on Transboundary Aquifer (TBA) systems: the Trifinio aquifer in Central America, the Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS) in Southern Africa, and the Pretashkent aquifer system in Central Asia (UNESCO-IHP 2015). [SOURCE: Page_22]

In the Southern Africa region, phases 1 and 2 of the GGRETA project (Braune et al. 2013; UNESCO 2016) resulted in the creation of a Multi-Country Cooperation Mechanism (MCCM) (Uitto 2004), a unique governance approach agreed upon by the governments of Botswana, Namibia, and South Africa to consolidate the GGRETA program's technical results for the STAS. The MCCM's primary goal is to ensure that STAS countries continue to gather and exchange data and information for use in the STAS Information Management System. It is also envisioned that in the long run, the MCCM will provide STAS countries with collaborative strategic assessments and recommendations on groundwater resource management challenges. In this regard, UNESCO and local stakeholders in STAS have been promoting various studies for assessment and sustainable management of the STAS (UNESCO 2016; Kenabatho et al. 2021; Leblanc et al. 2021). This study is a continuation of the work that has already been conducted in STAS with an [SOURCE: Page_22]

21 [SOURCE: Page_22]

ultimate goal of developing an integrated hydrological model (IHM) of STAS to enable estimation of water resources of the aquifer system. [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.2. Research problem and objectives A few local studies and consultancy studies have been conducted in various parts of STAS but they only provide local understanding of groundwater occurrence and flow system and while the geology and hydrogeology of the aquifer system is comparatively well understood in Namibia, it is not well known how far the aquifer system extends into Botswana and South Africa. It is therefore important to assess the hydrogeology and regional groundwater occurrence in STAS in order to define its boundaries as well as to establish links with the neighboring Central Kalahari Hydrogeological Basin (CKB) (Lekula et al. 2018a). Additionally, no integrated hydrological model has been developed to assess the effect of the thick unsaturated zone on groundwater recharge in the basin and there exists no climatic monitoring network and the groundwater monitoring network is quite scarce and not representative of the basin. [SOURCE: Page_23]

The main aim of this research is to assess groundwater resources of STAS using an integrated hydrological modelling approach and remote sensing data. Specific objectives of this research are to: 1) Develop a hydrogeological conceptual model of STAS to define the aquifer geometry and boundary conditions, through a systematic integration of multi-source data and 3D geological modelling; 2) Evaluate spatial-temporal consistency of various gridded precipitation products and select the most suitable for this study; 3) Translate the hydrogeological conceptual model into an integrated hydrological model (IHM) characterizing all the hydrological and hydrogeological processes in the aquifer system using remote sensing data both as input (rainfall, potential evapotranspiration and other ancillary data). 4) Calibrate the distributed integrated hydrological model using field measurements of groundwater level and GRACE TWS 5) Characterize spatio-temporal water balance dynamics in the STAS [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.3.      Outline of the thesis This thesis consists of six chapters: a general introduction, description of the study area, three core chapters tackling the above outlined objectives, and a synthesis. Each of the core chapters is based on a distinct article submitted or to be submitted for publication to a scientific journal. Chapter 1 provides an overview of the motivation for this study Chapter 2 is a detailed description of the study area Chapter 3 presents the hydrogeological conceptual model of Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (objective 1) [SOURCE: Page_23]

22 [SOURCE: Page_23]

Chapter 4 evaluates performance of various satellite rainfall estimates in Southern Africa (Objective 2) Chapter 5 explores application of remote sensing data both as input (driving forces and parametric data) and calibration target for an integrated hydrological model (IHM) (objectives 3 to 5) Chapter 6 is a synthesis of the application of remote sensing for assessment of groundwater resources in data scarce areas like STAS [SOURCE: Page_24]

23 [SOURCE: Page_24]

### 2.     Description of the study area

2.1.     Geographical and hydrological setting of the study area The aquifer system stretches from central Namibia into western Botswana and South Africa’s Nothern Cape Province and is part of the hydrogeological Kalahari Karoo Basin (KKB). Since one of the objectives of this study was to determine the spatial extent of STAS, the study area consists of the whole Kalahari Karoo sedimentary basin (Figure 2.1), with more focus on the Aranos, South-West Botswana and Kalahari Gemsbok sub-basins. The topography of the area is gently sloping with an elevation of 1500 m to 750 m above mean sea level sloping from the northwest to the south (Figure 2.2). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Hydrologically, STAS is part of Orange Senqu transboundary surface water basin. The drainage courses in the area include Auob and Nossob dry river valleys which originate in the Anas Mountains near Windhoek, Namibia and flow in a south-east direction joining at the southern tip of Kgalagadi Transfontier Park (KTP) and continue on as the Nossob River, which then joins the Molopo and Kuruman rivers at Bokspit (Figure 2.2). There they become the Molopo River which flows southwards to the Orange River. Auob and Nossob are both predominantly dry, only flowing short periods during abnormally high rainfall events and their runoff dissipates within a short distance from the source (ORASECOM 2007, 2009a; Spies 2016). Further south both rivers remain an indication of a wetter era and are often referred to as fossil rivers (Spies 2016). Nossob river section in the KTP and Molopo River up to their confluence at Bokspit form the Botswana- South Africa border. There also exists large shallow endorheic depressions or pans, which hold water periodically during wet seasons (Spies 2016; Chiloane et al. 2020). [SOURCE: Page_25]

2.1. Climate The climate is driven primarily by the seasonal interplay between subtropical high-pressure systems and the migration of easterly flows associated with the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) (Nash 2017). Precipitation occurs as localised showers and thunderstorms and is produced mainly by tropical temperate troughs and their associated cloud bands. During the summer months, the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) draws moisture from the equator to the northern areas of the study area leading to rainfall season between October and April with most of it occurring as a high intensity rain that occurs for a period of 2-4 days. In the far south, the Temperate Zone moves northwards during winter resulting rains which accounts for 50% of annual rainfall. Assessing trends in rainfall distributions and potential effects of climate change is challenging due to the highly unpredictable and variable rainfall patterns in the region both in space and time which brings droughts, floods and other extreme events (Batisani and Yarnal 2010; Eckardt et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_25]

Rainfall normally occurs between October and April predominantly in form of thunderstorms (high intensity and short duration) and ranges between 150 to 250 mm per year (UNESCO 2016). [SOURCE: Page_25]

24 [SOURCE: Page_25]

The highest rainfall months are from January to March whilst the lowest rainfall months are from June to September. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 2-1: Distribution of Karoo sedimentary basins in Southern Africa (after Johnson et al. (1996)). The numbers indicate stratigraphy of the regions described in Table 2-1 [SOURCE: Page_26]

2.2. Land use and Landcover STAS falls within the Kalahari Desert and most of the area is under natural vegetation which mainly consists of savannah grassland, sparse shrubs and sparse acacia trees (Figure 2.3 b). There is no afforestation or large-scale invasion by alien plant species although there is a rise of invasion by prosopis trees (Prosopis spp) including Prosopis chilensis, Prosopis velutina, Prosopis juliflora, Prosopis glandulosa, as well as their hybrids occur along Auob and Nossob Rivers (Strohbach et al. 2015; UNESCO 2016) and south-eastern part of the study area around Bokspit (Muzila et al. 2011; Mosweu et al. 2013). Land use consists of village centres, commercial farms and communal land. Commercial farms are found in the catchment areas of the Upper Auob and Olifants Rivers in Namibia (Figure 2.3 a) practicing mostly livestock farming with a few practising mixed livestock and irrigated crop production. Crops are dominated by fodder with a bit of horticulture. There are two communal lands one under traditional authority (near Aminuis) and the [SOURCE: Page_26]

25 [SOURCE: Page_26]

other owned by the government (near Hoachanas) (JICA 2002; UNESCO 2016) with residents practicing a combination of pastoral and arable farming. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Figure 2-2: Topography and hydrology of the study area and location of the studies conducted previously [SOURCE: Page_27]

Significant portion of STAS in Botswana falls within a wildlife management area (WMA) and the Kgalagadi Transfontier Park (KTP) which covers approximately 38,000 square kilometres. KTP extends into South Africa and is a nature and wildlife conservation park with tourism camping facilities, boreholes and camps used by management of park resources as the only developments (UNESCO 2016; Moswete et al. 2020). The terrain in the park consists of red dunes, sparse vegetation, occasional trees, and the dry riverbeds of the Nossob and Auob rivers. South of KTP in South Africa, livestock farming and game ranching are the two main land use activities within Mier Municipality and the rest of the area falls within District Management Area which is mainly nature reserve (Atkinson and Marais 2007; Taryn et al. 2014). This region is also characterised by a system of endorheic, closed salt pans due to presence of underlying rock and sediment that is susceptible to erosion by the wind (Goudie and Wells 1995). [SOURCE: Page_27]

26 [SOURCE: Page_27]

Figure 2-3: Land use and landcover map [SOURCE: Page_28]

2.3.      Soil STAS' soil is mostly made up of Kalahari sands, an aeolian sand that comes from the basin itself. Soils can be classified as sandy or fine. Sandy soils can be further classified as red, pink, and white sands, whereas fine soils are classified as alluvial soils, which are commonly found in prehistoric rivers and pans. Red soils are usually infertile due to low phosphate, magnesium, potassium, sodium, and carbon contents, as well as a low water holding capacity when less than 2 m deep. Alluvial soils have more clay, making them less water permeable. The difference in fertility and water holding capacity between the two major soil types has a direct impact on plant growth. Beneath the sand is a massive layer of calcareous or silicified sand or sandstone, which contains [SOURCE: Page_28]

27 [SOURCE: Page_28]

grits and small conglomerates (Spies 2016). Based on the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) soil classification system, the soils of STAS can be classified into four textural classes, based on sand, silt and clay contents, which include sandy loam, loamy sand, sand clay loam and sand Figure 2.4 (Jones et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_29]

Figure 2-4: Soil map of STAS (Source : (Jones et al. 2013)) [SOURCE: Page_29]

2.4.   Geology and groundwater occurrence The stratigraphy consists of Karoo Super Group, with its associated intrusions of dolerite, underlain by Pre-Karoo rocks and covered by a layer of Tertiary and Quaternary Kalahari sands. The pre-Kalahari geology of the study area is shown in Figure 2.5 as compiled from various geology maps which include pre-Kalahari geology of Botswana (Key and Ayres 2000), pre- Kalahari geology of the Aranos sub-basin (Stampriet Artesian Basin) (JICA 2002), geology of [SOURCE: Page_29]

28 [SOURCE: Page_29]

Namibia (Miller et al. 2008), simplified geology of South Africa (Johnson and Wolmarans 2016), geology of Nossob map sheet 2419 (van Wyk 1987a, b; Rose and Van Wyk 2006) and geology of Molopo-Nossob Orange River Surface Water Catchment (ORASECOM 2009b) [SOURCE: Page_30]

2.5.1. Pre-Karoo group The study area is bounded to the west, south, north and south-east by Pre-Karoo rocks which also form the basement rocks (Figure 2.5). To the west and south lies the Nama Group, which comprises mostly of red, brown, and purple sandstones, quartzites, and flagstones with thin micaceous shale horizons and clay-pellet conglomerate, and is underlain by the Proterozoic Damara Sequence (JICA 2002; Miller et al. 2008). The Nama Group occurs predominantly in Namibia and underlies the Karoo succession throughout most of Aranos sub-basin. In the south-west, it extends a short distance eastwards into South Africa (Rose and Van Wyk 2006), outcropping in a small region northwest of Rietfontein. To the north is the Ghanzi Group, which consists of weakly metamorphosed purple-red, arkosic sandstones, siltstones, mudstones and rhythmites (Aldiss and Carney 1992; Carney et al. 1994). The south-eastern margin is marked by quartzite crags of the Olifantshoek Group (Carney et al. 1994; Key et al. 1998). [SOURCE: Page_30]

2.5.2. The Karoo super group The Karoo Supergroup is an extensive volcano-sedimentary sequence that spans most of Southern Africa, deposited in the Paleozoic (Carboniferous to Cretaceous) era (SACS 1980; Smith et al. 1984; Johnson et al. 1996) (Figure 2.1). It occurs in the Main Karoo Basin in South Africa, Kalahari Karoo Basin as well as a number of minor basins in South Africa, Namibia, Botswana, Zimbabwe, and Mozambique, with the various basins being separated from one another by zones of non-deposition and of erosion (Catuneanu et al. 2005). The lithostratigraphy of Karoo Supergroup is subdivided into groups, formations and members based on general sedimentological traits. Different lithostratigraphic nomenclature is adopted in various Karoo basins and sub-basins. Johnson et al. (1996) compiled various stratigraphical classifications in each of the Karoo basins and correlated the lithostratigraphies in various basins to each other with the groups established in the main Karoo Basin in South Africa as the reference. Table 2-1 attempts to integrate the stratigraphy and hydrostratigraphy of Aranos, Kalahari Gemsbok and South-West Botswana sub- basins, while description of each group is given in the subsequent sections. [SOURCE: Page_30]

Dwyka group The Dwyka Group forms the base of the Karoo Supergroup and consists of a sequence of tillite, diamictite, pebbly mudrock, and ferruginous sandstone that were deposited in the late Carboniferous to the early Permian period (Smith et al. 1984; Thomas et al. 1988; Key et al. 1998; JICA 2002). It is sub-divided into two formations with the lower Malogong Formation consisting mainly of tillite and the upper heterolithic Khuis Formation consisting of diamictites and blue to black sandy shales with interbedded sandstone lenses (Levin 1980; Thomas et al. 1988; Key et al. 1998; ORASECOM 2009b). The Khuis Formation outcrops south-west of the study area at [SOURCE: Page_30]

29 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Rietfontein where it lies unconformably on the Nama Group, as well as in localised areas in the south-east along Molopo River channel (Levin 1980; Thomas et al. 1988; Rose and Van Wyk 2006) where it lies on top of the Olifantshoek Group. From the outcrop at Rientfontein, its thickness increases eastwards and away from the Nama Group where it is overlain by Ecca or Kalahari Group and extends to the north up to south of Matamata Camp and Kameelsleep area on Nossob river where it underlies, Ecca, Dolerite sills and Kalahari Group (van Wyk 1987a). South of Kuruman River, the Dwyka group is overlain by the Kalahari Group (Levin 1980). In Namibia and Botswana, Dwyka Group does not constitute an important aquifer within the study area and serves as an impermeable layer, except from the south-eastern area north of Molopo where they supply Gakhibane village (DGS 1994a, b; ORASECOM 2009b). However, in South Africa, the Dwyka formation is classified as a fractured aquifer and forms an important source of water to villages in this region in form of springs and boreholes (Levin 1980; Rose and Van Wyk 2006; ORASECOM 2009b). [SOURCE: Page_31]

Ecca Group Ecca group overlies the Dwyka Group and in the Aranos sub-basin, it corresponds with the Prince Albert Formation of the main Karoo Basin in South Africa (Johnson et al. 1996; Key et al. 1998; DGS 2000; JICA 2002). It comprises of lacustrine grey to green shales, mudstones, limestones, sandstones and coal-bearing shales. Its thickness increases eastwards with a reported thickness of over 380 m in the north-eastern part of the study area at Mashelheng Pan (Stoakes and McMaster 1990) as cited by Key et al. (1998). In the Aranos sub-basin in Namibia and Kalahari Gemsbok in South Africa, the Ecca Group is subdivided into Nossob, Mukorob and Auob Members. Nossob member forms the base of the Ecca Group and consists of white siltstone at the bottom, with a coarsening upward trend into fine-grained sandstones. The overlying Mukorob Member consists of dark grey shale interlayered with sandstone and a grey micaceous siltstone with thin coal bands at the top. Overlying Mukorob Member is the Auob Member which comprises a sequence of five units consisting of three sandstone layers separated by two thin carbonaceous shale members associated with coal (JICA 2002; Zieger et al. 2021). The Auob Member thins out towards the southern part of STAS with the most south-westerly evidence of its occurrence a few kilometers south of Union’s End (van Wyk 1987a). Nossob, Mukorob and Auob Members correspond to Lower Kobe (Ncojane Sandstone), Upper Kobe and Otshe Formations of the South-West Botswana Sub-basin (Fig.1) respectively, following Smith et al. (1984) nomenclature. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Rietmond Member Within the study area, the Rietmond Member occurs only in the Aranos sub-basin where it overlies the Auob Member. It consists of two units: The Lower Rietmond Member is composed primarily of shale, whereas the Upper Rietmond Member, of reddish-brown coarse to medium-grained sandstones interbedded with very fine-grained, highly micaceous sandstone or siltstone and dark grey to black shale. The Lower Rietmond shale was deposited throughout the basin but was partly or entirely eroded in many locations by two phases of erosion, one preceding the deposition of the [SOURCE: Page_31]

30 [SOURCE: Page_31]

Upper Rietmond Member and the other preceding the deposition of the Kalahari sequence. It is over 100 m thick where it is best developed, but due of the aforesaid erosion, its thickness varies erratically in the regions where it is found (JICA 2002). While WRC (2008) correlated the Lower Rietmond Member to the Kule Formation of South West Botswana, according to Johnson et al. (1996), they are different formations with the Rietmond Member forming the upper part of the Prince Albert Formation (van Wyk 1987a; JICA 2002). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Beaufort Group The Beaufort Group overlies the Otshe Formation in the north eastern part of the study area with its base distinguished by an abrupt transition in lithology from the underlying carbonaceous/coaly mudstone of the Otshe Formation to grey, purple-brown non carbonaceous mudstone (WRC 2008). In Southern part of the study area, the Beaufort Group occurs in a region of limited extent (Figure 2.5) which could be due to erosion at the end of the Beaufort times and after eruption of the Karoo Basalts (Key et al. 1998). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Lebung Group The Lebung Group lies on top of the Beaufort Group in the north-eastern part of the study area and extends into CKB. It is composed of Dondong and Nakalatlou Formations (WRC 2008). The Dondong Formation consists mainly of reddish-brown mudstone, with inconsistent basal reddish- brown conglomeritic sandstone or siltstone occurrences. Its thickness increases west to east and was not intercepted in the southern part of Ncojane block (Figure 2.2) during the WRC (2008) project. The Nakalatlou Formation constitutes the youngest sedimentary unit of Karoo Super Group and consists of reddish fine to medium grained sandstone. It pinches out against the Ecca Group west of Matlho-a-Phuduhudu Block (Figure 2.2) and against the Dondong Formation to the south (WRC 2008). [SOURCE: Page_32]

Karoo Basalts The Basalt lava forms the uppermost volcanic member of the Karoo Super Group. It consists of stacked basalt flows and is completely eroded in most of the study area except of the north-western and north-eastern parts (Figure 2.5). In the Aranos sub-basin it is referred to as the Kalkrand Basalt where JICA (2002) reported a thickness of up to 370 m, and is equivalent to the Stomberg Basalts of the Central Kalahari Basin (WRC 2008; Lekula et al. 2018a). The Kalkrand Basalts are well developed, and most water boreholes dug in this area encountered water inside the formation, therefore just a handful were drilled through to the underlying rocks (JICA 2002). [SOURCE: Page_32]

2.5.3. Post-Karoo Geology Dolerite sills Post Karoo dolerite dykes and sills intrude the Karoo sequence, mostly favouring the Ecca- Rietmond and Ecca-Beaufort Group contact and in places cut out the whole of the Rietmond Member and part of the Auob Member (JICA 2002; ORASECOM 2007). The dolerites are [SOURCE: Page_32]

31 [SOURCE: Page_32]

greenish grey to dark grey in colour, fine to medium grained and are weathered in some instances (JICA 2002; WRC 2008). [SOURCE: Page_33]

Kalahari Group Post-Karoo superficial deposits of the Kalahari Group cover the whole of the study area. From the bottom, it comprises of clayey gravel (Wessels Formation) and red to brown calcareous clay (Budin Formation) sandstone probably formed from Karoo Sequence; grit and conglomerate (Eden Formation) deposited under fluviatile conditions; calcrete (Mokalanen Formation) and sand (Gordonia Formation); clayey diatomaceous limestone (Lonely Formation) deposited in a lacustrine environment; and clay and sand (Goeboe Goeboe Formation) found in pans and rivers (Malherbe 1984; Smith et al. 1984; Thomas et al. 1988). [SOURCE: Page_33]

Figure 2-5: Simplified Pre-Kalahari geology of STAS (Data sources: Namibia (JICA 2002; Miller et al. 2008), Botswana (Key and Ayres 2000) and South Africa (van Wyk 1987a; ORASECOM 2009b; Johnson and Wolmarans 2016). The numbers indicate stratigraphy of the regions described in Table 2-1. [SOURCE: Page_33]

32 [SOURCE: Page_33]

Table 2-1: Stratigraphy and hydrostratigraphy of the study area modified after (Johnson et al. 1996) Smith et al. (1984); JICA (2002); WRC (2008); UNESCO (2016). Note that dolerites sills and dykes cross-cut the Karoo lithologies in many regions but are not included in the table because they are not considered as a group and they occur at different stratigraphic levels. However, they play a significant role in the hydrogeology of the study area. [SOURCE: Page_34]

33 [SOURCE: Page_34]

2.5.    Geological structures Geological features, including faults, lineaments and dolerite sills have been defined in the study area in previous studies through aeromagnetic, seismic and gravity data interpretation as well as through borehole analysis (JICA 2002; WRC 2008; Corner and Durrheim 2018). The main features include the NE-SW trending Makgadikgadi Line; the east-west trending Zoetfontein Fault; and the north-south trending Kalahari Line (Aldiss and Carney 1992; Key and Ayres 2000) (Figure 2.3). The Makgadigadi Line coincides with a zone of increased seismic activity; it separates regions of strongly contrasting magnetic and gravity signatures (Corner and Durrheim 2018). The Kalahari Line forms the border between the Gemsbok Sub-basin and the CKB (Smith et al. 1984; Key et al. 1998). The Zoetfontein fault is an early fault with post-Karoo faulting as its youngest manifestation (Corner and Durrheim 2018). There are other minor post-Karoo faults in the study area some of which have been intruded by dolerite dykes (JICA 2002; WRC 2008). All the faults were digitised from Corner and Durrheim (2018). [SOURCE: Page_35]

2.6.     Hydrogeology and regional groundwater flow system Kalahari, Auob and Nossob form the main aquifers of the study area with Rietmond and Mukorob Members as the main aquitards, separating the Kalahari, Auob and Nossob Aquifers respectively. The groundwater flow is mainly influenced by both, topography and geology and is directed from north-west (~1500 m a.s.l.) towards south-east (~750 m a.s.l.) and east (~1150 m a.s.l.). During years with average rainfall, long-term mean of gross recharge to the Kalahari aquifers is estimated at 0.5% of rainfall indicating that a substantial proportion of rainfall directly evapotranspirates and consequently does not recharge the aquifers (JICA 2002). This is due to the high potential evapotranspiration rates resulted by large vapour pressure deficit (Lekula et al. 2018a), the thick sandy unsaturated zone ranging from less than 10 m in the west, to more than 100 m in the central STAS, from where the stored moisture (or even groundwater from water table or capillary fringe) can be uptaken by deep rooted vegetation or directly evaporated by vapor transport (Lubczynski 2009). Remarkable in STAS is not only presence deep rooted acacia tree species (with record depth at 60 m for Acacia erioloba) but also alien plants such as Prosopis spp (with root system of up to 53 m for Prosopis.julifora) (Schachtschneider and February 2013; Canadell et al. 2014) particularly abundant along the river channels. [SOURCE: Page_35]

2.1. Water use and requirements Water use in STAS can mainly be categorised into domestic, livestock and irrigation. Since there are no permanent surface water bodies, the main water supply is groundwater through boreholes drilled in various aquifers, with very minor supply from pans and fossil riverbeds holding water for short periods during the rainy season which is used by livestock and wildlife (UNESCO 2016). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Water resources of STAS are mainly utilised in Namibia where irrigation accounts for 47%, followed by stock watering at 37.5%, and domestic use at 15%, while a small proportion of less than 1% is used for tourism. In the area there are over 7,000 abstraction boreholes for various [SOURCE: Page_35]

34 [SOURCE: Page_35]

purposes and only 1% of the total annual groundwater abstraction comes from Nossob aquifer, whereas 33% comes from the Auob aquifer and 66% comes from the Kalahari aquifers. In the last two decades, two hydrocensus have been conducted in this part of STAS (JICA 2002; UNESCO 2016) (Table 2-2). UNESCO (2016) estimated that groundwater abstraction in the STAS had increased by up to ~30% from 2002 to 2015, primarily due to an increase in the amount of irrigated land that had been identified and recorded as well as cattle watering. However, due to illegal over- irrigation on some farms (irrigating more land than is allowed), low permit compliance, and irrigation farmers with farm areas smaller than 1 ha who do not need water abstraction permits, a thorough assessment of the evolution of water abstraction is very difficult (UNESCO 2016). Groundwater for domestic purposes in larger settlements in the area is mainly extracted from the Kalahari and Auob aquifers apart from Koes which uses water from the Nossob aquifer. Efficient use of domestic water supply encounters major problems as around 49% of all municipal water is lost or unaccounted (e.g., Aranos, Koes, Gochas and Leonardville). Water loss and water unaccounted for is higher than 60% of water supply in Stampriet (UNESCO 2016). [SOURCE: Page_36]

Sectors                   Water Usage (million m^3/year)                Proportion (%) 2001                 2015                    2001       2015 1)Domestic water use •Village centres       0.6350                 0.8865                4.26        4.37 •Commercial farms      1.5940                 2.0000                10.69       9.87 •Communal farms        0.1270                 0.1316                0.85        0.65 2) Irrigation             6.8760                 9.5450                46.10       47.10 3) Livestock              5.6780                 7.6877                38.07       37.93 4) Tourism                0.0040                 0.0156                0.03        0.08 Total                     14.9140                20.2664               100.00      100.00 [SOURCE: Page_36]

In Botswana, Ncojane wellfiled consisting of six boreholes tapping the Auob Aquifer was established in 2007 during the Matsheng Groundwater Development Project to meet the water demand of the village centres in the Kgalagadi North District (WRC 2008). Table 2-3 outlines the recommended amount of abstraction from each borehole. Water supply in the Kgalagadi Transfontier Park comes from boreholes sunk into Kalahari Aquifer in the lower part of Auob and Nossob riverbeds where Nossob river forms the south-western boundary between Botswana and South Africa down to its confluence with the Molopo River. These boreholes supply water to the wildlife animals, tourists and staff working within the park and camping sites. There are also temporary pans/ water ponds within the park which largely and directly depend on rainfall availability and minor flows in parts of Molopo River which can be used as water supply mainly for livestock and wildlife (Spies 2016). [SOURCE: Page_36]

BH Number         Easting      Northing       Abstraction m^3d-1 BH[PHONE_REDACTED] BH[PHONE_REDACTED] BH[PHONE_REDACTED] BH[PHONE_REDACTED] BH[PHONE_REDACTED] BH[PHONE_REDACTED] Total                                         9637 [SOURCE: Page_37]

The Bokspit-Khawa settlements, located in the Kgalagadi South District to the south of KFT, combined pastoralism with hunting, gathering, and cattle rearing as a means of subsistence. Crop production is not practised in this area due to the unfavourable soil and climatic conditions. Most settlements in the district are situated near pans or fossil river valleys, or on rock outcrops that serve as sources of water through ground water supplies. There is no significant wellfield in this region as the only abstractions are for local settlements and for watering of livestock (mainly sheep and goats) (ORASECOM 2009b). Exploitation of groundwater resources in the southern part is limited by salinity and the abstraction is mostly limited to the Kalahari Aquifer (Rose and Van Wyk 2006; ORASECOM 2007). [SOURCE: Page_37]

2.2.    Groundwater level monitoring Groundwater level has been monitored on monthly basis in the Namibian part of STAS at selected towns and villages since 1980s but, out of those, only the monitoring wells at Tugela and Olifantswater West (Figure 2.6) are still operational. In 1999-2000, JICA (2002) drilled 19 observation boreholes at 9 different sites (Cristiana (1), Okonyama (3), Olifantswater West (2), Klein Swatmodder (1), Steynrus (3), Jackalsdraai (2), Cobra (3) and Tweerivier (3)) to monitor the hydraulic characteristics and water level of various aquifers in the area (Kalahari, Auob and Nossob aquifers). In 2004-2005, 7 additional boreholes were drilled at Spesbona(1), Boomplas, (2), Muritz (2) and Sandverhaar (2). In Ncojane area - Botswana, 6 wells monitoring the Auob Aquifer were established by WRC (2008) with monthly data available from 2008 to 2013. There is only one monitoring well in South African part of the study area with data from 2002 to 2014. [SOURCE: Page_37]

### 3.     Hydrogeological Conceptual Model of Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System

Abstract Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS) is shared between Botswana, Namibia and South Africa with massive utilization especially in Namibia. However, the understanding of the comprehensive hydrogeological framework of the aquifer system is limited. This study aimed to develop a comprehensive hydrogeological conceptual model of the aquifer system which mainly entailed 3D hydrostratigraphic modelling using Rockworks 3D geological modelling software, analysis of regional groundwater occurrence and flow system, sources and sinks, hydraulic parameters and definition of STAS boundaries. Six hydrostratigraphic units composed of four aquifers, including a new one (the Dwyka), and two aquitards were identified. Analysis of the regional groundwater flow system revealed that groundwater flows in two directions, indicating presence of a regional groundwater divide. 3D hydrostratigraphic geometry revealed that groundwater flow in STAS is influenced by topography but also by faults such as the Zoetfontein Fault, which displaces the pre-Kalahari rocks vertically creating a barrier to groundwater flow and. Hydrostratigraphic and 3D geometry work also led to a 42% increase of the size of STAS compared to the boundary initially delineated by UNESCO. The proposed hydrogeological conceptual model forms a basis for development of numerical integrated hydrological model to simulate surface-groundwater interactions, regional groundwater flow and aquifer development potential. [SOURCE: Page_40]

Keywords: 3D Geological Modelling, Hydrogeological Conceptual Model, Multi-layered Sedimentary Hydrogeological Basin, Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System [SOURCE: Page_40]

This chapter is based on Kinoti I, Leblanc M, Lekula M, et al (2024) Hydrogeological conceptual model of Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System in Southern Africa. Groundw Sustain Dev 101301. https://doi.org/https://doi.org/10.1016/j.gsd.2024.101301 [SOURCE: Page_40]

39 [SOURCE: Page_40]

3.1.   Introduction Groundwater is the main water resource in arid and semi-arid areas where often surface water is scarce or polluted. Understanding aquifer replenishments, groundwater flow dynamics and the impact of exploitation of groundwater resources is thus vital for groundwater management. These processes are usually evaluated through numerical models that include all features characterizing the system (Anderson et al. 2015). However, the ability of numerical models to reproduce these processes to evaluate possible utilization of management scenarios is highly dependent on reliability and accuracy of a hydrogeological conceptual model (HCM). An HCM is a synthesis of what is known about the area of interest (Krešić and Alex 2012) and provides a systematic overview of the hydrogeological system, properties and dominant processes relevant to the modelling objective. For quantitative groundwater assessment, an HCM may include information on hydrostratigraphy and spatial variability of hydraulic properties; groundwater flow pattern and direction, sources and sinks; boundary conditions; and field based water budget components (Anderson et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_41]

An HCM bridges the gap between hydrogeological characterization and groundwater modelling and is a major source of uncertainty in numerical modelling (Gupta et al. 2012; Krešić and Alex 2012; Anderson et al. 2015). Conceptual model uncertainty arises from incorrect hydrogeological characterization due to data inadequacy and insufficient knowledge of a groundwater system (Gupta et al. 2012; Enemark et al. 2019). Propagation of that uncertainty into the numerical modelling may lead to biased parameter estimation through calibration, hence uncertainty in model predictions (e.g. simulated heads and flow) (Doherty and Welter 2010; Ye et al. 2010). However, since a groundwater model is a simplification of reality, it is not possible to represent all hydrogeologic features, heterogeneities in subsurface properties and groundwater processes. All HCMs, so also numerical models, are therefore characterized by a certain level of uncertainty, which can only be reduced, but never eliminated (Anderson et al. 2015). The conceptual model uncertainty can be reduced either through a systematic integration of multi-source data that characterizes the system in question or by adopting the multi-model approach where alternative conceptualizations are developed and tested through the modelling process in parallel rather than sequentially (Enemark et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_41]

Development of an HCM typically entails the integration of surface and subsurface information such as topographic, geological, hydrometeorological, geomorphological, and hydrochemistry data. This is mostly done with an aid of geospatial analysis tools such as Geographical Information System (GIS), a combination of GIS and 3D geological modelling software or hydrogeological conceptualization tools built-in numerical modelling environments such as Groundwater Modelling System (GMS) (AQUAVEO 2023) as used, for instance, by Gurwin and Lubczynski (2005), Vijai Singhal (2011) and Tam et al. (2014) among others. 3D hydrostratigraphic modelling in a standard GIS environment (e.g. ArcGIS) is based on definition of lithological classes and extrapolating these data from boreholes. However, while this approach provides some basic two- [SOURCE: Page_41]

40 [SOURCE: Page_41]

dimensional and three-dimensional visualization functions, there are many deficiencies in the ability to display and analyze complex multi-source spatial data especially in areas with high heterogeneity (Tam et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_42]

An alternative solution can be achieved by combining 3D geological modelling software such as Rockworks (2020) with standard GIS as used by Lekula et al. (2018), Shishaye et al. (2020a), Shishaye et al. (2020b) and Zahran (2020), to mention a few. Lekula et al. (2018) noted that 3D modelling software such as Rockworks are easier to use and require less time to learn while offering comparable capability to GMS. 3D geological modelling enables reliable delineation of the hydrostratigraphic framework to ensure reliable predictions of groundwater flow. 3D geometry also provides insights into the variability of the volume of each hydrostratigraphic unit, which in turn can be useful to estimate the available groundwater development potential (Shishaye et al. 2020b, a). Rockworks also offers capability for exportation of results into ArcGIS for visualization and usage in subsequent numerical model. Some of the other 3D geological modelling software commonly applied in hydrogeological studies include Geomodeller (2023), GOCAD (2023), EarthVision (2023), GSI3D (2012) and Petrel (2023), to mention a few. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS), the focus of this study, is a multi-layered sedimentary aquifer system shared between Namibia, Botswana and South Africa. It is a major source of groundwater, particularly in Namibia where it is predominantly used for irrigation, domestic use and livestock watering, and is a key component of human and economic development in the region (ORASECOM 2007). A few local consultancy studies have been conducted in various parts of STAS (Figure 2.2) including JICA (2002), who conducted a detailed groundwater resources assessment in the western part of the STAS (Aranos sub-basin) in Namibia. Stone and Edmunds (2012) estimated groundwater recharge rates using the chloride mass balance method for the same spatial extent as JICA (2002). They estimated a long-term average direct rainfall recharge rate, ranging from 7 mm.y-1 to 46 mm.y-1 . WRC (2008) conducted a study in the north eastern part of the STAS in Botswana to assess the aquifers’ potential to provide sufficient portable water for supply to villages in the region, referred to as the Matsheng Villages project, and to locate suitable areas to site boreholes for monitoring, exploration and production purposes; they found that Ecca Aquifer was most productive in this region. A number of studies have also been conducted in the south-eastern part of STAS to identify zones of greatest potential suitable for livestock and domestic use in the Bokspits and Middlepits Tribal Grazing Land Policy (TGLP) Ranch Blocks (DGS 1994b, 2000; ORASECOM 2022). They found that the Auob Aquifer had the highest potential but its utilization was limited by groundwater salinity. Four desalination plants have since then been installed in the region to mitigate shortage of water for domestic use and livestock watering (ORASECOM 2022). Other studies include East et al. (2009), Kamundu et al. (2019) and Kisendi (2016) who developed conceptual and numerical models for Aranos, Auob and upper SW Botswana sub-basins respectively. [SOURCE: Page_42]

41 [SOURCE: Page_42]

The above-listed studies provide only local understanding of groundwater occurrence and flow system and do not take into consideration the transboundary nature of the aquifer system. Also, while the geology and hydrogeology of the aquifer system is comparatively well understood in Namibia, where it is referred to as Stampriet Artesian Basin, it is not well known in Botswana and South Africa. UNESCO (2016) made an attempt to delineate the boundary of STAS based on the occurrence of geological formations belonging to the ECCA Group within Auob and Nossob drainage basins which was later extended ‘arbitrarily’ eastwards into Botswana by UNESCO (2017a) (Figure 2.2). However, based on the map of the distribution of Karoo sedimentary basins in Southern Africa (Figure 2.1), as well as on the compiled in this study geological map (Figure 2.5), it is possible that the aquifer system extends beyond this boundary. It is therefore important to integrate data from all the previous studies in order to give a better understanding of regional groundwater occurrence and flow in the STAS, as well as to establish links with the neighboring regional hydrogeological study of Central Kalahari Basin (CKB) (Lekula et al. 2018a). [SOURCE: Page_43]

This study, therefore, aims to develop a hydrogeological conceptual model of the whole of STAS through a systematic integration of multi-source data and 3D geological modelling. Specifically, this study aims to (1) assess the spatial distribution and geometry of the hydrostratigraphic units; (2) improve understanding of regional groundwater flow system, sources and sinks, boundaries and interaction between different hydro-stratigraphic units; and (3) define the spatial extent of STAS and propose a new boundary building on the study by UNESCO (2016). [SOURCE: Page_43]

This STAS study is part of Governance of Groundwater Resources in Transboundary Aquifers (GGRETA) international project implemented by UNESCO International Hydrological Programme (UNESCO-IHP), which aimed at gaining experience in Governance of Groundwater Resources in Transboundary Aquifers. GGRETA had three pilot studies of Transboundary Aquifer (TBA) systems: the Trifinio aquifer in Central America, the Stampriet aquifer system in Southern Africa and the Pretashkent aquifer system in Central Asia. As part of the GGRETA project, UNESCO and local stakeholders have been promoting the development of a groundwater model for the assessment and the sustainable management of the STAS (UNESCO 2016; Kenabatho et al. 2021; Leblanc et al. 2021). In this paper, a new conceptual hydrogeological model of the STAS is proposed, which finally will be converted into numerical model in a further study. [SOURCE: Page_43]

3.2.    Methods Data collected by UNESCO-IHP through the GGRETA project (UNESCO 2016) was used in this study. This data includes borehole logs, geological maps and hydrogeological reports done by government agencies and private consultants from the member countries. The SRTM 30 m digital elevation model obtained from https://earthexplorer.usgs.gov/ was used to define the topography. To establish the link between STAS and CKB, the hydrogeological data of the CKB was obtained from Lekula et al. (2018). [SOURCE: Page_43]

42 [SOURCE: Page_43]

3.2.1. Harmonization of the hydrostratigraphy across the basin and hydrogeological characterization Different nomenclature in different literature sources is used to describe geology in various sub- basins of STAS. For instance, the nomenclature adopted in the SW Botswana sub-basin follows Smith et al. (1984) while in Aranos and Kalahari Gemsbok in South Africa (Figure 2.1), by SACS (1980). It was therefore important to harmonize the nomenclature and definition of the hydrostratigraphic units across the basin. This process involved an identification of the lithology in every borehole log and a subsequent classification into the appropriate hydrostratigraphic units. A total of 431 boreholes were used (Figure 3.1). [SOURCE: Page_44]

3.2.2. 3D modelling of hydrostratigraphic units After verification and classification of lithologic units into respective hydrostratigraphic units, the borehole data which included XY coordinates, depth, elevation and thickness of each hydrostratigraphic unit and also XY coordinates representing the location of the regional structures (Makgadikgadi Line, Kalahari Line and Zoetfontein Fault Zone) was imported into Rockworks (2020) for interpolation. Both, kriging with the automatic variography offered by Rockworks and inverse distance weighting (IDW) with power two interpolation methods, were tested. In contrast to the findings of Bamisaiye (2018), the inverse distance weighting was found to give a better correlation with existing geological records for the study area as also reported by Lekula et al. (2018). A grid spacing of 5 km was used and a 3-D hydrostratigraphic model was generated through 3D interpolation iteratively until satisfactory results were obtained. That model was used to generate other outputs including 2-D cross-sections along selected transects across the study area. For visualization of the model layers and subsequent use in the numerical model, 3-D data points detailing thickness of each hydrostratigraphic unit were exported from Rockworks as XYZ files, then imported and interpolated in ArcGIS to produce spatial extent of each unit. This enabled estimation of the geometry and thickness of each hydrostratigraphic unit. [SOURCE: Page_44]

43 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure 3-1 Location of boreholes used to construct the 3D hydrostratigraphic model in Rockworks. Cross section boreholes are those along the transects selected for visualization of the hydrostratigraphic cross-sections presented in Figure 5. [SOURCE: Page_45]

3.2.3. Regional groundwater flow paths and boundary conditions To determine the flow system in the Namibian part of the STAS, water level data based on a survey conducted on both monitoring and production wells during the JICA (2002) study in 2000 was obtained. In Botswana, data from monitoring, exploration and production boreholes drilled in the north-east of STAS was obtained from WRC (2008) report, while for the south-eastern area around Middlepits, Bokspits, Khawa and Tsabong villages, was obtained from DGS (1994b) and ORASECOM (2022). For the South African STAS area, the data was requested and downloaded from https://www.dws.gov.za/groundwater/NGA.aspx, which is the national groundwater archive. For each aquifer, the water levels were converted to hydraulic heads and interpolated in ArcGIS using the IDW method with power two. Potentiometric maps were then derived from the interpolated heads to get the flow direction for each aquifer. Hydrogeological boundary [SOURCE: Page_45]

44 [SOURCE: Page_45]

conditions were defined by, first examining the spatial extents of each aquifer trying to identify physical boundaries, followed by the regional potentiometric maps, to deduce the regional flow system. Where no physical boundaries exist along the margin of the study area, the external boundary conditions were defined along marginal streamlines defined as perpendicular to the potentiometric lines. Potential interaction between aquifers was assessed by analysing their corresponding heads in respect to each other. [SOURCE: Page_46]

### 3.2.4. Estimation of aquifer hydraulic properties

STAS aquifer transmissivity and storage coefficient data were extracted from 58 pumping tests (8 for Kalahari, 43 for Auob and 7 for Nossob) documented in groundwater consultancy reports (JICA 2002; WRC 2008). However, this data was deemed insufficient to represent the hydraulic parameters of the whole of STAS especially for the Kalahari and Nossob aquifers. Therefore, ranges of values were obtained from the literature based on lithological units which will be optimised through zonation in the numerical model in further research. [SOURCE: Page_46]

3.2.5. Sources and sinks The groundwater input to the aquifer system is through diffuse rainfall recharge in STAS which is limited, given the thick unsaturated zone and remains difficult to quantify. Rainfall recharge, via local depression and the drainage network, is often referred as the dominant recharge process in arid areas (Wheater et al. 2010). However, it has seldom been documented and quantified in the STAS. [SOURCE: Page_46]

The groundwater output from the aquifer system is through evapotranspiration, abstraction and groundwater outflow through the north-eastern boundary. The potential recharge and discharge areas based on environmental tracer data were identified from studies conducted by JICA (2002) Kirchner et al. (2002) and Stone and Edmunds (2012) and a review by Tweed (2021) in Namibia, and Levin (1980),Verhagen (1983),Toens & Partners (1997) as reviewed by Rose and Van Wyk (2006) in South Africa. Groundwater abstraction estimates were derived from various reports (JICA 2002; WRC 2008; ORASECOM 2009a, 2022; UNESCO 2016), for different parts of STAS where the aquifer is utilised. [SOURCE: Page_46]

### 3.3.    Results

3.3.1. Harmonization of the stratigraphy across the basin and hydrogeological characterization Eight hydrostratigraphic units underlain by impermeable basement, were defined in this study; they include (counting from the top): Kalahari Aquifer, Kalkrand Basalt Aquifer, Lebung Aquifer, Inter-Karoo Aquitard, Auob Aquifer, Mukorob Aquitard, Nossob Aquifer and Dwyka Aquifer (Table 2-1). The Lebung Group was split into two Formations with the Nakatlalou [SOURCE: Page_46]

45 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Formation (Ntane Sandstone), which occurs only at the north-eastern tip of the study area and extends into the CKB forming the Lebung Aquifer. The Dondong Formation of Lebung Group was combined with the underlying Beaufort Group and the Rietmond Member of the Aranos sub-basin to form the Inter-Karoo Aquitard. Karoo dolerite sills were found to occur at different hydrostratigraphic units within the study area. From a hydrogeological point of view, the sills are aquitards except where they are weathered or fractured and therefore, where they occur above, in between or below the Rietmond and Beaufort formations, they were considered to be part of the Inter-Karoo Aquitard. The Ecca Group was split into Auob Aquifer, Mukorob Aquitard and Nossob Aquifer. The Dwyka Group represented by fractured aquifer (Figure 5, E-E’ and G-G’), occurs in the south. [SOURCE: Page_47]

Seven hydrostratigraphic cross-sections across the study area were generated (Figure 3.1 and Figure 3.2), from the 3D hydrostratigraphic model, to visualize key findings. These cross- sections enabled visualization of the geometric interrelations between the hydrostratigraphic units relative to each other, influence of geological structures on the layers and the hydrogeological relationships between the sub-basins. For instance, cross-section AA’, CC’, and DD’ show the limited extent of the Kalkrand Basalts and Lebung Aquifers within the study area. The Inter-Karoo Aquitard and Ecca layers pinch out against the Kalkrand Basalt to the west and against the Pre-Karoo rocks to the north west and south-east as depicted by cross-sections AA’ and EE’ respectively. BB’ shows that at the central part of the Aranos sub-basin, the Inter Karoo Aquitard has been eroded and therefore the Kalahari and Auob Aquifers are hydraulically connected. The north-south cross-section DD’, which cuts across the South-West Botswana sub- basin shows the absence of the upper Karoo Formations south of the Zoetfontein Fault Zone (ZFZ) while EE’ and FF’ show the absence of the Auob Aquifer in the south-western part of the study area. In that region, the Inter-Karoo Aquitard is represented only locally by the dolerite sills. Cross-section DD’ shows the displacement of the Karoo Group vertically by the Zoetfontein Fault zone, while at the intersection between Zoetfontein Fault and Makgadikgadi line, the Karoo sequence is cut of completely as portrayed by cross-section GG’. That area is also characterised by presence of Karoo dolerites and sills and dykes (Figure 2.5). The west-east hydrostratigraphic cross-section HH’ (Figure 3.3) extending from the western edge of STAS to the eastern edge of CKB was generated to establish the link between the two basins. [SOURCE: Page_47]

46 [SOURCE: Page_47]

Figure 3-2: Hydrostratigraphic cross-sections from the transects in Figure 3-1 – vertical dashed lines represent faults [SOURCE: Page_48]

Figure 3-3: West-north-east cross-section cutting across northern STAS into CKB. Auob, Mukorob and Nossob hydrostratigraphic units of STAS have been combined into Ecca Group for better interpolation. The red vertical line is an approximation of the boundary between STAS and CKB based on hydrostratigraphy [SOURCE: Page_48]

47 [SOURCE: Page_48]

3.3.2. Regional groundwater flow pattern As shown in Figure 3.4, the general groundwater flow in the Kalkrand Basalt Aquifer (Figure 3) is from north to south-west into the neighbouring Fish Orange Basin. This agrees with the findings of JICA (2002) who applied an outflow boundary on the southern part of the Kalkrand Basalt Aquifer. Within the Auob River sub-catchment (Figure 2), groundwater flow is similar to surface water flow, i.e., from north-west to south-east and eventually southwards while in the Nossob River sub-catchment, groundwater flow exhibits a split into a north-eastward flow towards Central Kalahari Basin in Botswana and ultimately into Makgadikgadi Pans and south- eastward and then southward towards Orange River. The flow in the south-east of STAS, along the Molopo sub-catchment, is from the north east to south west and then southwards, similar to surface water flow system. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Figure 3-4 : Regional surface water and groundwater flow pattern in STAS and CKB and the proposed new boundary of STAS. Surface water flow is represented by the rivers and follows topography while groundwater flow is represented by the piezometric contours. The arrows show the general groundwater flow direction [SOURCE: Page_49]

48 [SOURCE: Page_49]

3.3.3. Hydrostratigraphic units and their geometry Figure 3-5 shows the spatial extent and thickness of the modelled hydrostratigraphic units within STAS, for each 5 km grid and the average thickness, spatial coverage and total volume of each unit is shown in Table 3-1. Kalahari Aquifer is absent in the south west where the Dwyka Group outcrops and its thickness varies from less than 10 m in the north-west and south, to more than 200 m in the central part of the Aranos sub-basin (Figure 3-5 a) where the Inter-Karoo Aquitard has been partially or completely eroded and the Kalahari aquifer is hydraulically connected with the Auob Aquifer (Figure 3-2 BB’). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Kalahari Aquifer is separated from Auob Aquifer by the underlying Inter-Karoo Aquitard which is spatially discontinuous (Figure 3-5 b). Inter-Karoo Aquitard is the thickest in the north and north-east (>150 m) and it wedges into basement rocks to the south and north-west of STAS. The locations where Inter-Karoo Aquitard is missing are potential ‘hydraulic windows’ for the groundwater recharge from the Kalahari into the Auob Aquifer. [SOURCE: Page_50]

The Auob Aquifer constitutes the main aquifer of the STAS. Its thickness varies from 0 m in the north-west and south where it wedges out, probably due to erosion, and increases north- eastwards to >200 m (Figure 3-5 c) towards CKB. Depth to the top of Auob is spatially variable and is largely determined by the deepening of the basin towards the east (Figure 3-5 CC’) and the reduction of the Karoo Super Group thickness southwards. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Mukorob Aquitard underlies the Auob Aquifer and is a confining unit to the underlying Nossob Aquifer. Similar to the Auob aquifer, it wedges out to the north-west and south and its thickness increases eastwards to ~180 m (Figure 3-5 d). It confines the Nossob Aquifer in most of the study area, apart from a small region south of Union’s End (Figure 3-6 a) where the Nossob aquifer is overlain either by dolerites or Kalahari layer. [SOURCE: Page_50]

The Nossob Aquifer extends between the overlying Mukorob aquitard and underlying Dwyka Group. Similarly, to Auob and Mukorob, it wedges out to the north-west and south. It has an average thickness of ~30 m (Figure 3-5 e), although that estimate is not precise, as not many boreholes reach and pass through the Nossob Aquifer and for the same reason, its spatial extent to the north-east is uncertain. The Dwyka Aquifer which is part of Dwyka Group, underlies the Nossob Aquifer in the south and its thickness increases from ~50 m in the west to more than 500 m in the east (Figure 3-5 f). [SOURCE: Page_50]

49 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Figure 3-5: Spatial extent and thickness of the hydrostratigraphic units: a) Kalahari, b) Inter-Karoo Aquitard, c) Auob Aquifer, d) Mukorob Aquitard, e) Nossob Aquifer and f) Dwyka Aquifer Table 3-1: Geometries, volumes and spatial coverages of the hydrostratigraphic units of STAS [SOURCE: Page_51]

3.3.4. New boundary of STAS and proposed boundary conditions Based on the hydrostratigraphy and the general groundwater flow system, a new boundary of STAS is proposed. It consists of the Aranos, South-West Botswana and Kalahari Gemsbok sub- basins of KKB (Figure 2-1) with slight modifications. To the north-west at Kalkrand Basalt (Figure 2-5), the boundary was adjusted along the groundwater divide (Figure 3-4), to exclude areas where the flow is towards the Fish-Orange River sub-catchment located south of STAS, which excluded the Kalkrand Aquifer initially considered to be part of STAS by JICA (2002) and UNESCO (2016). The boundary to the north-east in Botswana, was defined based on hydrostratigraphy following cross-section AA’ (Figure 3-1 and Figure 3-2), especially at borehole W$_3$ where all the layers start dipping north-eastward towards CKB and Lebung Aquifer begins, hence excluding both Lebung and Stomberg Basalt from the STAS. To the South, the boundary was extended to include the whole of Kalahari Gemsbok sub-basin to the end of the Kalahari Karoo Basin. [SOURCE: Page_52]

The boundary conditions were defined based on hydrostratigraphy, groundwater flow system and saturated thickness in the case of the upper phreatic Kalahari Aquifer. The groundwater flow system in the Kalahari Aquifer is mainly from the north-west to south east (Figure 3-6 a) and its saturated thickness varies both in space and time, increasing after significant recharge and decreasing afterwards due to downward leakage and groundwater evapotranspiration. Groundwater level is shallowest west of Auob River at ~10 m and deepest in the central part with at >100 m below the ground surface. In Botswana, the Kalahari is mostly unsaturated and groundwater occurs as highly localised perched aquifers mainly associated with calcrete pans and fossil river valleys (DGS 2000; WRC 2008; ORASECOM 2022). A lateral head dependent inflow boundary was defined to the north-west while all the other boundaries were defined as no flow boundaries as they are parallel to the flow lines and there exists a shallow groundwater divide between STAS and CKB in this aquifer to the north-east and in the south Kalahari is completely unsaturated. [SOURCE: Page_52]

The flow system in the Auob and Nossob Aquifers is bidirectional i.e. from north-west to north- east and south-east. Although the Karoo Super Group wedges out against Pre-Karoo Formations in the north-west (Figure 2-5), it is hypothesised that there could be inflow into Auob and Nossob Aquifers through this boundary depending on whether the Pre-Karoo Formations hold water or not and if there is a flow hydraulic gradient. This boundary was, thus, defined as a potential head dependent inflow boundary (Figure 3-4 a and b) and a head dependent lateral outflow, into the neighbouring CKB, was defined to the north-east for both aquifers. All the other external boundaries were defined as no flow boundaries as they are parallel to the groundwater flowlines. In the south, both Auob and Nossob are wedging so the groundwater could be evapotranspired or leaking upward into the Kalahari Aquifer or downward into Dwyka, depending on the hydraulic window contacts and vertical hydraulic gradient. [SOURCE: Page_52]

51 [SOURCE: Page_52]

There is no sufficient data to analyze the flow system in the Dwyka aquifer but it is conceptualized to be from the north east to south-west, similar to surface water flow. STAS is bounded to the south by Pre-Karoo rocks and therefore there is possibly no outflow to the south and all the outflow is through evaporation through the pans that are abundant in the southern part of STAS (Figure 2-2). [SOURCE: Page_53]

Figure 3-6: Groundwater flow system and boundary conditions per aquifer: a) Kalahari Aquifer; b) Auob Aquifer; and c) Nossob Aquifer [SOURCE: Page_53]

3.3.5.         Sources and sinks Generally, STAS is characterised by low rainfall and high potential evapotranspiration resulting in soil moisture deficits hence low groundwater recharge potential under normal rainfall conditions. Groundwater recharge is highly variable in space and time, as depicted by research [SOURCE: Page_53]

52 [SOURCE: Page_53]

conducted in various parts of STAS. In the western part in Namibia, groundwater recharge mechanism has been investigated through isotope analysis (JICA 2002; Kirchner et al. 2002; Tweed 2021) and chloride mass balance method (Stone and Edmunds 2012). The environmental tracer (δ18O, 3H and 14C) data indicates that sinkholes and river channels are potential recharge areas during the wet season. The sinkholes, mostly located in the western STAS area, are the result of the karstification of the Kalahari calcrete (Figure 3-7). Slight depressions in the landscape drain runoff towards these sinkhole areas, which then act as points of focused recharge to the Kalahari aquifer. However, delineation of the extent of these areas, as analysed by Tweed (2021), was limited by the availability of hydrochemical sampling data, as such showing a strong bias towards the western and central areas of Kalahari and Auob aquifers in Namibia. [SOURCE: Page_54]

In the southern part of the study area in the vicinity of Molopo, Nossob and Kuruman Rivers in South Africa, three potential recharge areas, including the south-western Dwyka sub-outcrop area (Figure 2-5), the Kuruman River and the pan system (Figure 2-2) were identified by Levin (1980). They found out that recharge occurs as collection of water in fissures and contact zones of outcrop surfaces and where the Kalahari layer is thin (Levin 1980). This was affirmed by Toens & Partners (1997) in Rose and Van Wyk (2006),who reported high 14C and 3H concentrations at the Dwyka outcrop (i.e., south-western parts of the study area which indicates rapid recharge, while much lower 14C and declining 3H concentrations were observed in areas covered by thick Kalahari Layer (i.e., area north of Matamata (Figure 2-2), which indicated slower recharge rate. Active recharge occurs on the Kuruman River bed during flooding and is dependent on heavy rainfall in the river catchment area. The effect of the recharge on the water quality and water level is restricted to the river bed and decreases sharply away from the river channel (Levin 1980), as depicted by a freshwater lens, representing relatively young groundwater, that is restricted both laterally and vertically possibly due to presence of an impervious or semi-impervious layer (Verhagen 1983). The water level beneath most of the pans in the southern part of STAS is shallow and rapidly deepens away from the pans. During rainy seasons, the salt on the pans dissolves and the salt solution moves downwards to the water table (Levin 1980). Downward leakage from Kalahari Aquifer into Auob Aquifer occurs at the central part of STAS where there is a large hydraulic window. Environmental isotope investigations in the south-east show no evidence of recent recharge into the Auob and Nossob aquifers (DGS 1994b). [SOURCE: Page_54]

The dominant discharge mechanism from the unconfined Kalahari aquifer is by evapotranspiration which occurs mainly from the unsaturated zone, especially along the dry river channels and from the salt pans in the south. However, the deep-rooted plants such as Prosopis spp (Figure 3-8 a) can also withdraw groundwater (Braune et al. 2013; UNESCO 2016), although the stable isotope data (δ18O and δ2H) indicate greater impact of evaporation than transpiration (Kirchner et al., 2002). Lateral groundwater outflow through Auob and Nossob Aquifers across the north-eastern [SOURCE: Page_54]

53 [SOURCE: Page_54]

boundary towards Central Kalahari sub-basin (CKB) and ultimately into Makgadikgadi Pans (Lekula et al. 2018a), constitute the other discharge of groundwater of the STAS aquifer system. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Water resources of STAS are mainly utilised in Namibia where irrigation accounts for 47% of groundwater abstraction, followed by stock watering at 37.5%, and domestic use at 15%, while a small portion of less than 1% is used for tourism. In the area there are over 7,000 abstraction boreholes for various purposes and only 1% of the total annual groundwater abstraction comes from Nossob aquifer, whereas 33% comes from the Auob aquifer and 66% from the Kalahari aquifers. A hydro census conducted by JICA (2002) estimated the total abstraction from Namibian part of STAS to be ~15 million cubic metres per annum. Another hydrocensus by UNESCO (2016) estimated that the abstraction had increased by ~30% to 20 million cubic metres per annum. In Botswana, Ncojane wellfiled consisting of six boreholes tapping the Auob Aquifer was established in 2007 during the Matsheng Groundwater Development Project to meet the water demand of the village centres in the Kgalagadi North District (WRC 2008) referred to as the Matsheng Villlages. The total recommended amount of abstraction from the wellfield is ~3.5 million cubic metres per annum. In the south-east three production boreholes supplying the Bokspits Ranches were reported by ORASECOM (2022) with an annual abstraction of 85,249 m^3 reported in 2020. More groundwater utilisation in this area is limited by very high salinities in the Ecca aquifers and parts of Kalahari Aquifer and also by low yield from the latter (DGS 1994b). Nevertheless, four desalination plants have since been installed in the region to mitigate water shortage (ORASECOM 2022). In South Africa, the aquifer system is utilised within the Kgalagadi Transfrontier Park abstracting mostly the Kalahari Aquifer and for domestic and livestock watering in the Mier settlement with an estimate of ~1.5 million cubic metres per annum (ORASECOM 2009a). This totals to ~35.85 m^3 per annum although it could be more due to unaccounted, illegal groundwater abstractions (UNESCO 2016). [SOURCE: Page_55]

a) b) [SOURCE: Page_55]

Figure 3-7: a) Google Earth Pro image (25/2/2005) of the landscape in the west with sinkholes (dark areas) and the site where groundwater has a 3H value of 0.6 TU and b) Locations of sampling [SOURCE: Page_55]

54 [SOURCE: Page_55]

sites where there is 3H present and relatively high 14C concentrations (red circles) and low 18O values (yellow circles), and therefore represent sites of relatively rapid recharge (Tweed 2021). [SOURCE: Page_56]

Figure 3-8: a) Google Earth Pro image (14/12/2015) of a section of Auob river showing encroachment by deep rooted alien Prosopis vegetation; and b) Production boreholes and discharge pans overlayed on ESRI world imagery [SOURCE: Page_56]

3.3.6. Hydraulic parameters Hydraulic properties reflect spatial heterogeneity of an aquifer system and determine aquifer flows and connectivity across aquitards. The parameter values are usually obtained from field tests such as pumping tests or slug tests. However, most boreholes in the STAS were drilled for production but without pumping test; as such the available data is not representative of the whole system. Therefore, ranges of values for hydraulic conductivities, specific yield and specific storage were adopted from literature, based on lithology (sand, sandstone and siltstone for Kalahari, Auob and Nossob aquifers respectively and shale for both aquitards), to enable estimation of water resources of STAS. These parameters will be optimised in the numerical model in a follow-up study. The adopted values of hydraulic conductivity range between four to five orders of magnitude with the ones for sand ranging between 10-3 m d-1 to 10 m d-1, sandstone between 10-6 m d-1 to 10-1 m d-1, siltstone between 10-7 m d-1 to 10-3 m d-1 and shale between 10-9 m d-1 to 10-4 m d-1 (Freeze and Cherry 1979; Domenico and Schwartz 1990; Kuang et al. 2020). Values of specific storage were obtained from recent review studies conducted by Kuang et al. (2020) and Chowdhury et al. (2022). Based on the reviewed field tests, specific storage of sandstone and shale range between 10-5 m-1 to 10-7 m-1and 10-6 m-1 to 10-7 m-1orders of magnitude respectively. The values of specific yield for the phreatic Kalahari Aquifer which range between 0.21 to 0.35 were obtained from Johnson (1967). [SOURCE: Page_56]

55 [SOURCE: Page_56]
