### 3.4.1. Hydrogeological characterization and regional groundwater flow system

A 3D hydrogeological conceptual model of STAS was developed, through systematic integration of data from various sources, to assess the physical, process and spatial variability structures of the aquifer system. The physical structure, which includes the extent and geometry of hydrostratigraphic units (Gupta et al. 2012; Enemark et al. 2019) was defined through an iterative process using Rockworks 3D geological modelling code. The first and most important step was to harmonize the hydrostratigraphic units across the basin. The hydrostratigraphic units were identified through a comprehensive analysis of lithological information from borehole logs and potential groundwater occurrences following a similar study by Lekula et al. (2018) in the neighbouring CKB. Rockworks offered various capabilities not available in a GIS environment such as flexibility in data management and processing, a range of ways to visualize the results such as through 2D cross-sections, and exportation of results into ArcGIS for visualization and usage in subsequent numerical model. Rockworks also provides flexibility to update the 3D model and therefore as more drill logs become available, the conceptual model can be updated and re-computed for more accurate hydrogeological representation. [SOURCE: Page_57]

The 3-D hydrostratigraphic model showed that the overall lithological and hydrostratigraphic setting of the Kalahari Karoo Basin varied based on the geological and deformation processes that had occurred in the basin over time. For instance, absence of Upper Karoo Formations i.e., Karoo Basalts and Lebung Group, and part of Lower Karoo i.e. Beaufort and upper part of Ecca Group in the western and southern parts of STAS, could be due to non-deposition or as a result of erosion after Karoo deposition and during the post-Gondwanan period (Catuneanu et al. 2005). Within STAS, thick Kalahari sands are found where the Rietmond Member has been eroded partially or completely. Part of this area has been intruded by post-Karoo dolerite sills and where there are no dolerite sills, the Kalahari and Auob Aquifers are hydraulically connected. The Karoo Basalts are absent in most of the studied area except the western edge (known as Kalkrand Basalts) where they are quite fractured and weathered and several production boreholes have been drilled through the formation (JICA 2002). That formation exists in most of CKB (referred to as Stomberg Basalt) where it forms a confining unit to the Lebung Aquifer (Lekula et al. 2018a), which pinches out at a short distance west of the Kalahari line (Figure 2-5). [SOURCE: Page_57]

The Ecca Group of STAS is subdivided into three formations/members, each forming a single hydrostratigraphic unit. In contrast, in CKB, Mukorob Member/Upper Kobe Formation and Nossob Member/Lower Kobe Formation/Ncojane Sandstone are considered as one formation, the Bori Formation (Johnson et al. 1996; Catuneanu et al. 2005), which together with overlying Kweneng and Boritse Formations, form the Ecca Aquifer of CKB (Lekula et al. 2018a). The Kweneng Formation is equivalent to the Auob of STAS (Johnson et al. 1996; Catuneanu et al. [SOURCE: Page_57]

56 [SOURCE: Page_57]

2005). The whole of KKB, apart from a small portion in the south-western tip where the Dwyka Group outcrops, is overlain by sediments of Kalahari Group. The Dwyka Group forms the lowest Karoo Group across the KKB and apart from the southernmost part of STAS in South Africa and part of south-western Botswana where it is considered a fractured aquifer, it is generally considered as an aquiclude. [SOURCE: Page_58]

STAS boundary as defined by UNESCO (2016) was initially delineated based on the occurrence of Ecca geological group within Auob and Nossob River basins. That boundary was later extended eastwards by extending the contour lines arbitrarily trying to identify a possible no- flow boundary (UNESCO 2017). This was based on the assumption that groundwater flow was a replica of surface topography. While this is a useful simplification, it may introduce bias in instances when topography is not the primary determining factor. Thus, when assessing the groundwater flow system, the combined effect of topography, climate, and geology on groundwater flow patterns should be examined (Zhang et al. 2022a). Shishaye et al. (2020b) recommends comparing potentiometric surface to surface water flow in order to identify other aspects such as geological structures that may influence groundwater flow. Application of this method in this study revealed that Ecca groundwater flow in the northern part of STAS was not entirely controlled by topography, as surface water flows south-eastwards while groundwater flows both south-eastwards and north-eastwards indicating presence of a groundwater divide. Similar results were observed by ORASECOM (2009b) who analysed groundwater flow system in the Molopo-Nossob surface water sub-catchment of Orange River Catchment. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Inclusion of regional faults in 3D geological modelling revealed that at the intersection between Zoetfontein Fault and Makgakgadi line, the Karoo sequence is displaced vertically (Figure 3-2 GG’) hence altering the groundwater flow direction. This area is also characterised by intrusion of dolerite dykes and sills which could also form a barrier to groundwater flow. This is in agreement with Reeves and Hutchins (1982) who stated that “in southern Botswana, the Zoetfontein Fault appears to have a post-Karoo vertical displacement of several hundred metres and therefore cuts out the Karoo rocks and brings Archaean granite-greenstone terrain, overlain by Proterozoic platform sediments, close to the surface (below only Kalahari cover)”. This divides the SW Botswana sub-basin into two, with groundwater in the northern part (Ncojane sub-basin) flowing north-eastwards into CKB and ultimately into Makgakgadi pans and the southern part (Nossob sub-basin) flowing south-wards towards Molopo River. Similar results were observed by Lekula et al. (2018) in the south-eastern part of CKB where the Zoetfontein Fault displaced the Karoo Group vertically upthrown to the north and cuts off the Ecca Aquifer. The zone south of the Zoetfontein fault, is characterised by absence of Upper Karoo Formations. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Proper conceptualisation of groundwater flow system was crucial as it enabled external boundary conditions to be defined realistically. The new proposed boundary of STAS covers an area of approximately 154,000 km^2 of which 41% in Botswana, 39% is in Namibia and 20% in South [SOURCE: Page_58]

57 [SOURCE: Page_58]

Africa. A head dependent outflow boundary in the Ecca aquifers was identified at the north- eastern boundary of STAS at the contact with CKB which is in contrast to UNESCO (2017a) and Lekula et al. (2018) who applied a no flow boundary. This means that CKB is part of a larger regional groundwater flow system and not a laterally closed groundwater flow system like was hypothesised by Lekula et al. (2018) and both CKB and STAS are sub-basins of the larger hydrogeological KKB. STAS is bounded by Pre-Karoo rocks to the south and Rose and Van Wyk (2006) describe the flow in the southern part of the STAS as retarded, indicative of a stagnant system. DGS (1994a) reports similar flow system north of Molopo River within Botswana and attributes the slow flow system to be the cause of the high salinities. The discharge mechanism in the south could thus be only through evaporation through the salt pans. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Six hydrostratigraphic units including, from the top, Kalahari Aquifer, Inter-Karoo Aquitard, Auob Aquifer, Mukorob Aquitard, Nossob Aquifer and Dwyka Aquifer were identified in the STAS. The unconfined Kalahari Aquifer plays a major role in the rainfall storage and spatio- temporal distribution of sub-surface fluxes (Lekula et al. 2018a). The plant interception and thick unsaturated zone in combination with large vapour pressure deficit due to aridity, causes evaporation of infiltrated water after a rain event and consequently most of the rain water does not reach the water table. Presence of deep-rooted vegetation, such as Acacia and Prosopis spp, withdrawing water not only from the unsaturated zone but potentially also from groundwater, also implies large interception and transpiration processes. [SOURCE: Page_59]

Environmental tracer data, highlights areas of water exchange between the Kalahari and Auob aquifers; these coincide with the location of faults and areas where the Inter-Karoo Aquitard thickness is less than 3 m (Tweed 2021). The Nossob Aquifer is characterised by low groundwater yields, although the piezometric level is very shallow and exhibits sub-artesian to artesian conditions in some areas in the Aranos sub-basin in Namibia, hence the basin is commonly referred to as the Stampriet Artesian Basin. The Nossob Aquifer is confined by the Mukorob Aquitard throughout nearly the whole STAS and thus it is assumed that in majority of its extent, it is not hydraulically connected with the overlying aquifers. According to Kirchner et al. (2002) the Nossob groundwater is 17500 – 40000 years old and as such they classified it as fossil water. The Dwyka Aquifer is hydraulically connected to Nossob and the Kalahari Aquifers [SOURCE: Page_59]

3.4.2. Implication for groundwater management and future work This study provides a systematic overview of the 3D hydrogeological system, properties and dominant processes as well as delineation of a new boundary of STAS. Use of 3-D geological modelling enabled reliable delineation of the hydrostratigraphic framework to improve understanding of the hydrogeology of STAS as well as to ensure reliable predictions of groundwater flow. These provided insights into the spatial extent and variability of the thickness of each hydrostratigraphic unit, which is useful for estimation of groundwater development potential. The aquifer thicknesses from Rockworks are also easily transferrable into the groundwater model to define the aquifer geometry. Reliable estimation of the aquifer geometry [SOURCE: Page_59]

58 [SOURCE: Page_59]

will reduce uncertainties propagated into the numerical groundwater flow model, hence more accurate quantitative estimation of water resources of STAS. The new boundary of STAS is ~67000 km^2 (i.e., 42%) more than the area previously reported in Phase 1 of the GGRETA project (UNESCO 2016), implying that more settlements, inhabitants and industrial developments could be reliant on this strategic aquifer system than previously thought, and therefore proper groundwater management is key in this area. [SOURCE: Page_60]

A schematic diagram summarizing the important elements of the conceptual model of STAS, including the hydrostratigraphic units, sources and sinks, boundary conditions, groundwater flow system is presented in Figure 3-9. Six hydrostratigraphic units (four aquifers and two aquitards) are proposed. The hydrogeological conceptual model of STAS, which is mainly qualitative, forms the basis for a quantitative numerical model. 3D integrated hydrological model (IHM) is recommended, as it allows for most realistic integration of surface and groundwater fluxes (Zhang et al. 2022b; Balugani et al. 2023; Daoud et al. 2024; Lubczynski et al. 2024), simulation of the role of the unsaturated zone on the water balance dynamics, simulation of groundwater flow in all three spatial dimensions, realistic simulation of pinching out of aquifers and provides opportunity for particle tracking option to determine groundwater residence time (Anderson et al. 2015). [SOURCE: Page_60]

3.4.3. Uncertainty and limitations Hydrogeological conceptual models are usually developed using different data sources often with inherent uncertainties (Anderson et al. 2015). The uncertainties also arise because the models are created from point observations which are not always representative of the whole sub-surface. The major sources of uncertainties as identified by other authors include data density, data quality, geological complexity and geological interpretations (Raiber et al. 2012; Moya et al. 2014; Lekula et al. 2018a; Shishaye et al. 2020b, a). In respect to 3D hydrostratigraphic modelling the main area of low confidence was within and around Kgalagadi Transfrontier Park in Botswana, where no borehole logs were available. The pre-Kalahari geological map of Botswana (Smith et al. 1984; Carney et al. 1994; Key and Ayres 2000) was used to assess and adjust the Rockworks model output through an iterative process in order to improve the accuracy in this region. Most of the wells used to produce the 3D hydrostratigraphic model did not penetrate the whole Ecca Group, particularly in Botswana and South Africa leading to higher uncertainty in the modelled thickness of the deeper layers in these areas. This uncertainty is less in Namibia where ~18% of the boreholes penetrate into the Nossob aquifer and they are well spatially distributed. The uncertainty increases eastwards into Botswana where only a handful of the boreholes penetrate into the Kobe Formation and only 3 within the study area intercepts the whole Ecca Group. Nonetheless, since real geology is complex, it is not necessary to bring all the complexity of geology into a descriptive groundwater model (Krešić and Alex 2012; Anderson et al. 2015) and thus the results attained were considered satisfactory. Another source of uncertainty in STAS is due to lack of sufficient data to define the groundwater flow system per aquifer in the north-eastern part of the STAS, especially for Kalahari and [SOURCE: Page_60]

59 [SOURCE: Page_60]

Nossob Aquifers and to map the saturated thickness of Kalahari Layer. The flow system of the Kalahari is dependent on whether the Kalahari is saturated at the boundary or not and also on spatial variability of the Kalahari saturation, but the data to define it is scarce. Other sources of uncertainty include, the potential hydraulic connection with the underlying aquifers, especially in the north-east and south where the available data is scarce, as well as mapping potentiometric surface of the Nossob Aquifer to define location of artesian conditions and eventual hydraulic contacts with the overlying aquifers. [SOURCE: Page_61]

3.5.   Conclusion This study characterises the 3D hydrogeological settings of STAS, the connectivity of the aquifers, the regional groundwater flow system, boundary conditions, hydraulic properties and the sources and sinks. Use of 3-D geological modelling code such as Rockworks proved to be vital as it allowed integration of various data types from different sources such as drill logs and regional faults. Two regional groundwater flow systems, one to the north east into CKB and ultimately into the Makgadikgadi Pans and another one to the south towards the Orange River were identified from analysis of groundwater level data, indicating presence of a groundwater divide. Comparison of the groundwater flow system to the surface water flow system and surface topography showed that groundwater flow in Kalahari was influenced by topography but in Ecca Aquifers (Auob and Nossob) also by geology and geological structures. Inclusion of regional faults in 3D modelling showed that the Zoetfontein Fault Zone (ZFZ) cut and displaced the Ecca Group vertically. Also, the intersection between the ZFZ and the Makgakgadi Line is characterised by a dolerite dyke which also could also create a barrier to groundwater flow, hence creating a water divide. [SOURCE: Page_61]

Six hydrostratigraphic units, including four aquifers and two aquitards were identified in STAS. The study also proposes a new boundary of STAS, highlights the distinctiveness of the geometries and extents of each hydrostratigraphic unit, and defines external boundaries which include inflow boundaries at the north-west and outflow boundary at the north-east. The environmental tracer data, as reviewed from different literature, highlights river channels, karstic sinkholes located in the western part of STAS in Namibia, outcrops, where the Kalahari is thin and the pan system in the southern part of STAS as potential recharge zones. River channels and the pan system also act as discharge areas through evapotranspiration. The environmental tracer data also highlights the faults and areas where the Inter Karoo Aquitard is less than 3 m as potential areas of interaction between the Kalahari and Auob Aquifers. The results from this study are fundamental inputs into the follow up numerical modelling studies for the assessment of groundwater potential estimation, recharge estimates and scenario simulation for sustainable groundwater management in STAS. [SOURCE: Page_61]

60 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Figure 3-9: Three-dimensional schematization of the hydrogeological conceptual model showing the geometry, sources and sinks, groundwater flow system and boundary conditions of the study area (not to scale): Abs – abstraction; ETgw - groundwater evapotranspiration; ETuz - unsaturated zone evapotranspiration; Exfgw - groundwater exfiltration; N - north; ZFZ - Zoetfontein Fault Zone [SOURCE: Page_62]

61 [SOURCE: Page_62]

### 4.      Evaluation and comparison of 12 gridded precipitation products over Namibia and Botswana

Abstract Precipitation, as the main driving force of the Earth’s hydrological cycle, plays a major role in the ecosystem and, therefore, high-quality precipitation data at high spatial and temporal resolution is paramount. Several grided precipitation products (GPPs) based on satellite data, re-analysis, gauge measurements or a combination of two or all of these sources, have been developed and widely applied in various studies. However, these products are characterized by inherent errors resulting from the primary data sources, retrieval algorithms or both and, therefore, the GPPs need to be evaluated for these errors before they are applied in any study to avoid propagation of the errors. This study evaluated thirteen GPPs using in -situ measurements from 37 rain gauges throughout Botswana and Namibia as the reference data at daily and seasonal scales. Categorical and continuous metrics as well as bias decomposition into hit, miss or false bias and decomposition of mean squared error into systematic and random errors, were used to evaluate each product's precipitation detection capability, quantitative accuracy, and dominant error source. All the metrics supported the superior performance of MSWEP at daily scale with a critical success index (CSI) = 0.43 correlation coefficient (r) = 0.63, root mean square error (RMSE) = 3.0 mm d-1 and total bias = 7.7%. MSWEP also performed best consistently at rain gauge station level and at seasonal scale. This study provides valuable guidance on selection of appropriate GPPs for various applications such as hydrological, agriculture, flood and drought monitoring. [SOURCE: Page_63]

Keywords: Botswana, gridded precipitation products, gauge rainfall, Namibia, remote sensing [SOURCE: Page_63]

62 [SOURCE: Page_63]

4.1.     Introduction Precipitation dynamics play a key role in Africa, where agriculture is the main source of income and the quality of pastures in arid and semi-arid areas mainly depends on water availability (Meyer et al. 2009). Therefore, high-quality precipitation data at high spatial and temporal resolution are of great importance for weather monitoring, climate analysis and hydrological studies (Seyama et al. 2019). Rain gauges are considered the reference data source for precipitation and the most accurate data for hydrological modelling. However, they give point-level precipitation readings that are then spatialized via interpolation to depict the spatial-temporal distribution of precipitation for various purposes such as hydrological modelling. Several gridded precipitation data sets based entirely on gauge information have been created at different spatial-temporal scales and are widely used. Examples include data from the Climate Research Unit (CRU), the Global Precipitation Climatology Centre (GPCC), University of Delaware (UDEL), Climate Prediction Centre (CPC): CPC Unified (CPCU), CPC Merged Analysis (CMAP) and Global Precipitation Climatology Project (GPCP). However, in most cases, the gauge network is too sparse and unevenly distributed to characterize highly variable spatial dynamics of precipitation correctly. This is especially true for remote areas such as mountainous and arid areas and developing countries where inadequate installation and maintenance operations do not allow for realistic monitoring of temporal and spatial climate characteristics. The available global gauge-based datasets have poor spatial and temporal resolution ranging from 0.5° to 2.5° mostly at monthly scale although there could be local datasets with better resolutions for various applications but to the authors knowledge none is available for the area of interest in this study. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Ground-based radar networks are an alternative to rain gauges and are well established in some parts of the world. Radar networks monitor vast areas in a distributed manner, unlike measurements made using conventional gauges, making it possible to track precipitation over far- off places. However, these measures are not available on a global or regional scale and are constrained in the presence of complicated topography that obstructs the radar signal. Moreover, radar systems are not widely deployed in Africa (Meyer et al. 2009). [SOURCE: Page_64]

The idea of predicting precipitation from wireless networks like commercial cellular phone microwave links has been assessed by a number of researchers during the past decade. These calculations are based on the electromagnetic signals between communication antennas' attenuation during precipitation events (Uijlenhoet et al. 2018). However, accurate precipitation predictions are difficult in remote locations with fewer towers and the technique is also limited by the private cellular phone companies' policies on data sharing. Moreover, since these microwave links were originally designed for communication needs, they are not optimal for rainfall monitoring due to less than ideal operating frequencies, geometric deployment and the coarse magnitude resolution of the microwave system (David et al. 2019). Therefore, this approach is not reliable as a standalone method of rainfall estimation but can be used as a complement to existing tools like rain gauges, radars and satellites. Additionally, so far only a handful e.g., (Hoedjes et al. 2014; Gosset et al. 2016) of researchers have focused on applying the method in Africa. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Another source of precipitation estimates is based on re-analysis systems. Reanalysis systems aim to combine irregular data and models that cover a wide range of physical and dynamical processes to produce a synthesis estimate of the system's state over a uniform grid with spatial homogeneity, temporal continuity, and a multidimensional hierarchy (Satgé et al. 2019). Many essential climate [SOURCE: Page_64]

63 [SOURCE: Page_64]

variables output from reanalysis systems maintain a physically consistent framework and can be obtained after only a short time delay. A reanalysis system includes a background forecast model and a data assimilation routine. The observations assimilated into the reanalysis system, the model parameterizations, and the complex interactions between the model and the observations all influence the subsequent precipitation forecast generated by the system (Trenberth et al. 2001; Lorenz and Kunstmann 2012). Some of the re-analysis precipitation products include the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts’ (ECMWF) ERA$_5$ (Hersbach et al. 2020) and ERA$_5$-Land (Muñoz-Sabater et al. 2021) and their predecessors, National Centers for Environmental Prediction (NCEP) Climate Forecast System Reanalysis (CFSR) (Kistler et al. 2001) , the Japanese 55 year re-analysis (JRA-55) (Kobayashi et al. 2015; Harada et al. 2016), the Modern Era Retrospective analysis for Research and Applications, version 2 (MERRA-2) (Gelaro et al. 2017). Although some re-analysis products have very high temporal resolution providing data hourly, they are characterized by poor spatial resolution ranging from 0.5° to 2.5°, with the exception of ERA$_5$-Land with a spatial resolution of 0.10. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Satellite Rainfall Estimates (SREs) are another alternative to improve spatial and temporal precipitation measurements. A number of global, quasi-global and continental SREs have been developed in the recent decades and are being widely used for different applications such as weather forecasting, hydrology and water resource management. SREs are indirect measurements made from satellite/sensor constellations which utilize thermal infrared (TIR) and passive microwave (PMW) parts of the electromagnetic spectrum for satellite rainfall estimation. There are currently over 20 SREs available. The first-generation SRE was the Tropical Rainfall Measuring Mission (TRMM) (Theon 1994), which was launched in 1997 by the National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA) and the Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency (JAXA). Some other SREs which took advantage of previous SREs and missions to provide rainfall estimates at high spatial and temporal resolution and coverage include the Climate Hazards Group Infrared Precipitation with and without station data (CHIRP and CHIRPS) (Funk et al. 2015), Africa Rainfall Climatology (ARC) (Novella and Thiaw 2013), Precipitation Estimation from Remotely Sensed Information using Artificial Neural Networks Climate Data Record (PERSIANN-CDR) (Ashouri et al. 2015), Multi-Source Weighted Ensemble Precipitation (MSWEP) (Beck et al. 2017, 2019), Tropical Applications of Meteorology using Satellite and ground-based observations (TAMSAT) (Maidment et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_65]

Although most SREs are similar in that most of them combine data from PMW and TIR sensors, each of them has its unique approach in cross-calibrating, weighting, and blending various data sources. Furthermore, since satellite measurements are indirect, they suffer from biases which may be due to indirect nature of the relationship between the observations and precipitation, inadequate sampling, tuning of the instrument or retrieval algorithm (Thiemig et al. 2012). Several studies have validated SREs through comparisons with surface observations, both in terms of mean errors and detection capabilities across different regions of the world, in order to select the most appropriate product for various applications and circumstances. Gauge measurements are the main indicators used to assess the ability of SREs to reproduce precipitation patterns with a few studies reporting on hydrological sensitivity to SREs (Satgé et al. 2019). Also there have been numerous published bias correction methods for satellite-derived rainfall estimates. Therefore, it is difficult to conclude on the respective advantages and limitations of various indicators and bias correction schemes to assess the ability of SREs to reproduce precipitation patterns as SREs could rank [SOURCE: Page_65]

64 [SOURCE: Page_65]

differently depending on the indicator and correction scheme used (Satgé et al. 2019). Generally, performance of SREs is shown to be dependent on topography, seasonality and climatology, especially in terms of detection probability and bias. For instance, regions with complex terrain exhibit poor rain detection and magnitude-dependent errors while semi-arid exhibit low probability of detection (Maggioni et al. 2016). This means that evaluation results for one area are not transferable to another similar area and, therefore, it is necessary to assess whether a certain product is appropriate for a particular environment or region. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Most studies conducted in Southern Africa evaluated a limited sample of SREs. Some of these include: a study by Liechti et al. (2012) who compared TRMM-3B42 v^6, FEWS-NET RFE 2.0, and CMORPH 0.250 against daily, decadal, and monthly rainfall gauge data in order to determine which may be utilized as an input for the hydrological model of the Zambezi Basin. In that investigation, FEWS-Net RFE 2.0 and TRMM-3B42 outperformed CMORPH. Toté et al. (2015) evaluated the performance of TAMSAT African Rainfall Climatology and Time-series (TARCAT 2.0), FEWS-NET RFE 2.0, and Climate Hazards Group Infrared Precipitation with Stations (CHIRPS) for drought and flood monitoring in Mozambique. They compared their decadal estimates with decadal rain gauge data and found that FEWS-Net RFE 2.0 and CHIRPS performed better than TARCAT 2.0 because both are inherently bias corrected using the Global Telecommunication Station (GTS) rain gauge data network. Valdés-Pineda et al. (2016) evaluated CMORPH, PERSIANN and TRMM-3B42 for use in real-time forecasting of daily stream flows, in Upper Zambezi Basin, using Quantile Mapping and Principal Component based bias correction techniques with CHIRPS as the reference data and found that TRMM Multi-Satellite Precipitation Analysis (TMPA) outperformed both CMORPH and PERSIANN as it produces more realistic rainfall estimates while the latter overestimate daily rainfall for the basin. [SOURCE: Page_66]

In the Notwane catchment area in south-eastern Botswana, Kenabatho et al. (2017) used quantitative (descriptive) and categorical statistics to assess the daily performance of TRMM and spatial rainfall from the Generalized Linear Models (GLMs) on rainfall detection. Their findings demonstrated the potential of SREs to supplement the scant rainfall data in semi-arid regions. Lekula et al. (2018) evaluated FEWSNET-RFE∼11 km, TRMM-3B42∼27 km, CMOPRH∼27 km and CMORPH∼8 km using scatter plot analysis, descriptive statistics, categorical statistics and bias decomposition and found that FEWS-RFE∼11 km performed best, providing better results of descriptive and categorical statistics than the other three SREs, although bias decomposition showed that all four SREs underestimated rainfall. For bias correction, they considered the time variant space fixed method due to lack of sufficient rain gauges to represent the whole catchment. [SOURCE: Page_66]

All these studies have provided useful information related to their specific objectives but to give comprehensive feedback on the performance of SREs in the poorly gauged arid to semi-arid Namibia and Botswana a large sample of GPPs need to be evaluated. This study aims to evaluate performance of several gridded precipitation products in space and time. [SOURCE: Page_66]

65 [SOURCE: Page_66]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High

### 4.2.     Study area and data

4.2.1. Study area The study area lies approximately between latitudes 11.70° S to 29.4° S and from longitudes 16.90° to 26.75° E covering approximately 1.4 million km^2, with 823,680 km^2 and 582,000 km^2 in Namibia and Botswana respectively. Hydrologically, most of the area is characterized as water scarce due to sparse and highly variable rainfall combined with high evaporation rate and absence of permanent surface water over large parts. The mean annual rainfall ranges from ~650 mm in the north to less than 250 mm in the southern area (Kalahari Desert) and less than 50 mm in the south west and along the coastal areas (Namib desert). [SOURCE: Page_67]

The climate is driven primarily by the seasonal interplay between subtropical high-pressure systems and the migration of easterly flows associated with the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) (Nash 2017) (Figure 4-1). Precipitation occurs as localized showers and thunderstorms and is produced mainly by tropical temperate troughs and their associated cloud bands. During the summer months, the Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) draws moisture from the equator to the northern areas of the study area leading to rainfall season between October and April with most of it occurring as a high intensity rain that occurs for a period of 2-4 days. On the other hand, the northward flowing Benguela current brings cold air to the western coastal shores driven by high pressure system and this combination suppresses rainfall. In the Namib desert, coastal fog is five times greater than rain and forms an important source of water for the desert flora and fauna (Eckardt et al. 2013). Assessing trends in rainfall distributions and potential effects of climate change is challenging due to the highly unpredictable and variable rainfall patterns in the region both in space and time which brings droughts, floods and other extreme events (Batisani and Yarnal 2010; Eckardt et al. 2013). [SOURCE: Page_67]

4.2.2. Gridded Precipitation Products The GPPs that were assessed in this study are briefly described in this section. These products were chosen because they are readily available for lengthy time series (more than 20 years), temporal resolutions (daily) that make them particularly ideal for hydrological applications, spatial resolutions (<= 0.25 degrees) are accessible in near real time, and are available in the public domain. [SOURCE: Page_67]

TMPA TRMM is a cooperative NASA-JAXA space mission created to track and study tropical and subtropical precipitation as well as the energy release that goes along with it. TRMM was designed to measure rain rates from space using a combination of high resolution Precipitation Radar (PR), TRMM Microwave Imager (TMI) and a visible and infrared scanner (VIRS) (Theon 1994). Several SREs have been created based on TRMM, such as TRMM Multi-Satellite Precipitation Analysis (TMPA), which provides rainfall estimates using TRMM radar and radiometer observations (along with other satellite inputs) until the end of 2014 and observations from the Global Precipitation Measurement (GPM) mission since its launch in 2014. (Huffman et al. 2007). Estimates from the TMPA are created in five steps: First, the Goddard profiling algorithm is used to calculate the PMW rain rates for each sensor. Next, the rain-rate data are cross-calibrated and combined. Third, the IR precipitation estimates are made using the corrected PMW estimates. [SOURCE: Page_67]

66 [SOURCE: Page_67]

Fourth, the PMW and IR estimates are combined. Finally, a bias correction is applied, and the final result is produced (Huffman et al. 2007, 2010). Several versions of TMPA precipitation products such as 3B40RT, 3B41RT, 3B42RT and 3B42 adjusted have been produced over time. The latest version of TMPA i.e., TMPA 3B42 adjusted version 7 with a spatial resolution of 0.250 at daily scale was used in this study. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Figure 4-1: Southern African synoptics which influence rainfall patterns in the region: Angola Low (AL), Coastal Low (CL), Cold Front (CF), Intertropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), Tropical Temperate Trough (TTT), West Coast Trough (WCT), Westerly Wave Low (WWL), Zaire Air Boundary (ZAB) and other major pressure system such as the Continental High Pressure (CHP), South Atlantic High Pressure (SAHP), South Indian Ocean High Pressure (SIHP) (adopted from Eckardt et al. (2013)) [SOURCE: Page_68]

IMERG The Integrated Multi-Satellite Retrievals for GPM (IMERG) merged precipitation product was introduced by the US GPM science team following the launch of the Global Precipitation Measurement (GPM) mission Core Observatory (GPM-CO) in February 2014. The IMERG algorithm is designed to inter-calibrate, merge, and interpolate all available satellite PMW precipitation estimates, along with estimates from microwave calibrated infrared (IR) satellites, precipitation gauge analyses, and other precipitation estimators, in order to produce rainfall [SOURCE: Page_68]

67 [SOURCE: Page_68]

estimates with fine spatial and temporal resolution spanning the TRMM and GPM eras for the entire planet. [SOURCE: Page_69]

CMORPH CDR CMORPH CDR (Xie et al. 2020) is made up of bias-corrected CMORPH high resolution global precipitation estimates that have been reprocessed. The precipitation estimates are generated in two steps. First, the purely satellite-based global precipitation fields are constructed by integrating level 2 retrievals of instantaneous precipitation rates from all available passive microwave measurements aboard Low Earth Orbiting platforms. Following that, bias is removed by comparing the data to the CPC daily gauge analysis over land and adjusting the data to the Global Precipitation Climatology Project (GPCP) integrated analysis of pentad precipitation over ocean. The bias corrected CMORPH satellite precipitation estimates are produced once a month with a 3–4-month latency on an 8 km by 8 km grid over the global domain from 600 S to 600 N and in 30-minute intervals beginning January 1, 1998. The bias estimates are then upscaled to provide two additional sets of data files with varied time and space resolutions, including the hourly and daily CMORPH at 0.250. Both the 30 min at 8 km grid and daily at 0.250 versions hereafter referred to as ‘CMORPH 30 min 8 km’, and ‘CMORPH daily 0.250’ are used in this study. FEWSNET-RFE version 2 FEWSNET-RFE, hereafter referred to as RFE$_2$, is developed by National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) Climate Prediction Centre (CPC) primarily for the Famine Early Warning Systems Network (FEWSNET) to support initiatives for disaster monitoring in Africa. RFE$_2$ has a spatial resolution of 0.10 at daily scale and spans from January 2001 to present in near real time. Estimates from Advanced Microwave Sounding Unit (AMSU), Special Sensor Microwave Imager (SSM/I), the Geostationary Operational Environmental Satellite (GOES) precipitation index (GPI) calculated from cloud-top infrared (IR) temperatures on a half-hourly basis and daily Global Telecommunications System (GTS) rain gauge data are the main input data for the RFE$_2$ algorithm. [SOURCE: Page_69]

African Rainfall Climatology version 2 (ARC$_2$) ARC$_2$ (Novella and Thiaw 2013) is based on the same algorithm as RFE$_2$ but unlike RFE which includes PMW estimates, ARC only uses geostationary IR data from the European Organisation for the Exploitation of Meteorological Satellites (EUMETSAT) collected every three hours over Africa. A second input data for ARC is quality-controlled 24-hourly GTS gauge observations over Africa. [SOURCE: Page_69]

CHIRP and CHIRPS The Climate Hazards Group (CHG) Infrared Precipitation (CHIRP) and CHIRP combined with station data (CHIRPS) were created by the United States Geological Survey (USGS) and the University of California, Santa Barbara's Climate Hazards Group. They are quasi-global (500 S - 500 N) products with a spatial resolution of 0.050 at daily scale and pentadal, decadal and monthly accumulations, starting in 1981 to near present. The data sources used to create CHIRP include: (1) the Climate Hazards Precipitation Climatology (CHPClim) (Funk et al. 2015b); (2) quasi- global geostationary TIR satellite observations from two NOAA sources, the CPC and the National Climatic Data Center (NCDC); (3) atmospheric model rainfall fields from the NOAA Climate [SOURCE: Page_69]

68 [SOURCE: Page_69]

Forecast System, version 2 (CFSv$_2$); and (4) the TRMM 3B42 product from NASA. The CHIRP product is then merged with in situ precipitation observations obtained from a number of sources such as Global History Climatology Network (GHCN), Global Summary of the Day (GSOD), Global Telecommunications System (GTS) and Southern African Science Service Centre for Climate Change and Adaptive Management (SASSCAL), to produce CHIRPS [SOURCE: Page_70]

PERSIANN-CCS-CDR Precipitation Estimation from Remotely Sensed Information using Artificial Neural Networks- Cloud Classification System-Climate Data Record (PERSIANN-CCS-CDR) (Sadeghi et al. 2021) was created to provide high spatial and temporal resolution dataset for monitoring heavy precipitation rates. It utilizes Gridded satellite infrared data (GridSat-B$_1$), NOAA Climate Prediction Center (CPC-4km) IR product, Global Precipitation Climatology Project (GPCP V$_2$.3) as the input data and CPC Global unified gauge-based analysis of daily precipitation, NCEP Stage IV Quantitative Precipitation Estimate (QPE) Data and PERSIANN-CDR (Ashouri et al. 2015) as the reference dataset. PERSIANN-CCS-CDR is generated beginning with rain rate outputs from the PERSIANN-CCS (Hong et al. 2004) model which utilizes one global IR image from Gridsat- V$_1$ from 1983 to February 200 and CPC-4km from March 2000 to present, resampled to 0.040, to estimate the rainfall for that corresponding time step. Next, a threshold of 0.1 mm/3hr is applied to the 0.040 rain rate (RR) grids which are then aggregated to monthly temporal resolution and 2.50 spatial resolution for comparison to monthly GPCP v^2.3 precipitation. Bias adjustment weights for each monthly 2.50 grid cell are then calculated, bilinearly interpolated back to 0.040 and applied to each original RR grid. Finally, the bias-adjusted rain rate files are combined to create the final PERSIANN-CCS-CDR product, which includes an accumulation at 3-hourly resolution and 0.04° spatial resolution. [SOURCE: Page_70]

TAMSAT The TAMSAT algorithm (Maidment et al. 2014, 2017; Tarnavsky et al. 2014), which is geared towards drought monitoring, is based on two main data sources: Meteosat TIR imagery from the European Organisation for the Exploitation of Meteorological Satellites (EUMETSAT) and rain gauge measurements for calibration. It measures the length of cloud temperature below a specific threshold during a 5-day period for version 3 and a 10-day period for version 2. Rainfall is then approximated as a function of Cold Cloud Duration (CCD) using calibration coefficients derived from linear regression of CCD totals for the chosen temperature threshold against historical rain gauge accumulations. The correlation function varies across Africa, as the continent is divided into many homogenous climate zones that change with each calendar month. The decadal (v^2.0) and pentadal (v^3.0) rainfall estimates are then disaggregated to daily values proportional to the quantity of CCD observed for each day between 06:00 and 06.00 the next day at a 0.0375◦ resolution. TAMSAT v^3.0 (Maidment et al. 2017) whose calibration was modified to better capture local variations in the rainfall climate and to reduce the dry bias observed in v^2.0 was used in this study. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Multi-Source Weighted-Ensemble Precipitation (MSWEP) version 2.8 MSWEP combines the complementary strengths of gauge, satellite, and re-analysis data to offer reliable precipitation estimates over the globe. It includes six non-gauge-corrected gridded satellite precipitation datasets (CMORPH, TMPA 3B42RT, GSMaP, and GridSat (pre-2000) and IMERG (post 2000) as well as re-analysis (ERA-Interim, JRA-55) and gauge-based (WorldClim, GHCN- [SOURCE: Page_70]

69 [SOURCE: Page_70]

D, GSOD, GPCC, and others). Using a multiplicative gauge correction scheme, daily interpolated gauge data from 76,747 quality-controlled gauge observations are used to correct the merged 3- hourly satellite-reanalysis-based precipitation estimations. The corrected precipitation estimate accumulation is then calculated daily for the 24-hour period ending at the interpolated reporting time, and a blended estimate is calculated by weighted-mean averaging of the corrected precipitation estimate accumulation and the daily interpolated gauge-based estimate (Beck et al. 2019). MSWEP is available in three different variants: ‘past_nogauge’, ‘past’ and ‘NRT’ at a spatial resolution of 0.10 and latency of 3 hours with daily and monthly accumulations and covers the period between 1979 to 2000 for the ‘past_nogauge’, ‘past’ variants and to present for the ‘NRT’ variant. The ‘past_nogauge’ and ‘past’, hereafter referred to as MSWEP-NG and MSWEP- G were used in this study. [SOURCE: Page_71]

ERA$_5$-Land ERA$_5$-Land (Muñoz-Sabater et al. 2021) is the enhanced global dataset for the land component of the fifth generation of European Re-analysis (ERA) It employs the 4D-Var data assimilation method and was developed utilizing the enhanced integrated forecast system (IFS), model cycle CY41R$_2$, and the ECMWF Tiled Scheme for Surface Exchanges over Land (H-TESSEL) to provide hourly data. Surface fluxes include downward shortwave and longwave radiation, as well as liquid and solid total precipitation. These fields are interpolated from the ERA$_5$ resolution of ~31km to the ERA$_5$-Land resolution of ~9km using a triangular mesh-based linear interpolation approach. The atmospheric forcing developed for ERA$_5$-Land is hourly and consistent throughout the production period, and it is the result of the assimilation of a large number of conventional meteorological and satellite observations through a four-dimensional variational assimilation system (4D-Var) and simplified extended Kalman filter (SEKF) systems (Hersbach et al. 2020). Unlike previous land re-analyses (e.g., (Beck et al. 2019b; Nogueira 2020; Tarek et al. 2020) ) ERA-5 Land is not subjected to any corrections to the precipitation forcings to address limitations of the precipitation fields of the atmospheric reanalysis. This is mostly due to the (1) improved quality of ERA$_5$ precipitation compared to earlier atmospheric reanalysis and (2) reduced reliance on external data, which would limit near-real-time data availability. However, air temperature, humidity, and pressure are compensated for altitude changes between the ERA$_5$ and ERA$_5$-Land grids. [SOURCE: Page_71]

70 [SOURCE: Page_71]

4.2.3. In situ data This study was carried out using four hydrological year daily data, from 01/09/2015 to 31/08/2019. The study period was selected based on availability of in situ data with less data gaps. Daily in situ rainfall data was sourced from Global Summary of the Day (GSOD) (https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/) and Southern African Science Service Centre for Climate Change and       Adaptive      Land       Management         (SASSCAL)        www.sasscal.org     and https://sasscalweathernet.org/, a joint effort of Angola, Botswana, Namibia, Zambia, South Africa in close collaboration with Germany. However, most of the selected satellite and re- analysis precipitation products directly or indirectly use some of the GSOD data to correct the bias in their rainfall estimates. CHIRPS and MSWEP ‘past’, for example, use GSOD data directly; ARC and ERA$_5$ use World Meteorological Organization – Global Telecommunication System (WMO-GTS) station data, which has a high overlap with GSOD and IMERG, TAMSAT, PERSIANN-CCS-CDR and RFE use GPCC data obtained from GSOD station data. Only CHIRPS utilizes data from SASSCAL weathernet, although there is overlap of 7 stations between GSOD and SASSCAL datasets. Stations that are already included in precipitation products should ideally not be utilized for validation since they may mislead users about the genuine accuracy of the rainfall products (Mekonnen et al. 2023). Therefore, only the SASSCAL stations were used in this study, including the stations overlapping with GSOD for spatial representation since, with the exception of CHIRPS, there is no information on which GSOD stations have been merged with various GPPs and for which time period. A total of fifty- seven rain gauges providing data at daily scale were available for Namibia and Botswana but after screening, only thirty-seven were deemed fit for use in this study and only those with <10% data gaps throughout the four years were used (Table 4-1). The rain gauges are quite well distributed spatially (Figure 4-2) and therefore were considered to be enough to give a spatial representation of the rainfall pattern in the study area. [SOURCE: Page_73]

The study area was classified into four climate zones based on the level of aridity as defined by Holzapfel (2008). Regions receiving <100 mm annual precipitation were defined as extremely arid, 100 to 250 mm as arid, 250 to 500 mm as semi-arid and >500 mm as non-arid (Figure 4-2). [SOURCE: Page_73]

72 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Table 4-1: Rain gauge stations and data gaps for the period 2015–2019 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Figure 4-2: Location of the rain gauges used in this study and four climatic regions defined based on the level of aridity and daily precipitation based on a sample of rain gauges for each climatic zone for a period of four years. [SOURCE: Page_74]

4.3.     Methodology It is important to incorporate a variety of metrics, such as categorical and continuous metrics focusing on precipitation occurrence and intensity, in order to evaluate performance of GPPs from different and complementary perspectives because no one statistic can accurately reflect the performance of GPPs and no single perspective can meet the needs of diverse applications. [SOURCE: Page_74]

73 [SOURCE: Page_74]

For example, Camici et al. (2020) investigated the importance of continuous and categorical metrics for GPPs' evaluation on river discharge simulation and found that categorical metrics and continuous metrics of correlation coefficient did not provide relevant information, whereas RMSE was reliable for selecting a GPP with superior performance for discharge simulation. In contrast, categorical metrics might be more helpful for other applications where frequency of precipitation occurrence i.e., precipitations/no precipitations, is an influential factor (Zhao 2023). Moreover, since the total biases come from different error components, including decomposition of the error components in GPP evaluation could provide significant information for customizing retrieval algorithms and mitigating errors (Chen et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_75]

In this regard, to evaluate performance and space-time consistency of GPPs, point gauge measurements were compared to the respective GPP pixel measurement using four descriptive and six categorical statistics and finally decomposition of the total bias into hit, miss and false bias and mean squared error into systematic and random components. Because of the uncertainties associated with the upscaling/downscaling procedures, the original spatial resolution of the selected precipitation products was preserved rather than matching them to a common resolution. All analysis were carried out at daily scale, first over the whole study period and then over different seasons. [SOURCE: Page_75]

4.3.1. Categorical statistics SREs can be affected by three different types of errors, i.e., missed rain events (events that are recorded on the ground but not detected by the SRE), false alarms (rainfall that is detected by the SRE algorithm but not observed on the ground), and hit bias (mistakes in the rain-rate magnitude of accurately detected rainfall events). Categorical statistics were used to evaluate and compare performance of SREs in terms of rain detection, at daily scale based on 37 rain gauges for a period of 4 hydrological years. Six categorical statistics, including Probability of Detection (POD), False Alarm Ratio (FAR), Critical Success Index (CSI), Frequency Bias (FBS), Fraction of Miss (FM) and Fraction of Detection (FD), were calculated following equations 4.1 to 4.6: [SOURCE: Page_75]

## 𝐹𝐷 = (𝐻 + 𝐶𝑁)/(𝐻 + 𝑀 + 𝐶𝑁)                                                                   4.6

74 [SOURCE: Page_75]

Where 𝐻 (hit): number of successfully detected rain events; 𝑀 (miss) number of non-detected rainy events; 𝐹 (false alarm) means that satellite algorithm detected rain on a non-rainy day; and CN (correct negative) means that satellite algorithm successfully detected a no rain case. Calculating the four components of Hit, False, Miss, and Correct Negative is the initial step in calculating the aforementioned categorical metrics. The four factors were calculated following contingency Table 3-2 (Roebber 2009). [SOURCE: Page_76]

Table 4-2: 2x^2 Contingency table                     Reference rain gauge Yes              No Gridded Precipitation Product Yes                    Hit              False (GPP)                         No                     Miss             Correct Negative [SOURCE: Page_76]

The POD indicates the ability of SRE to capture actual precipitation occurrence with an optimal of 1. FAR with an optimal value of 0 indicates the incorrect detection of actual precipitation occurrence. CSI comprehensively illustrates the overall fraction of precipitation events correctly detected by GPPs, with an optimal value of 1. FM is similar to POD but, in contrast, uses M in the numerator instead of H and has an optimal value of 0. FD uses H, M and CN to evaluate the overall detection capability of GPPs with an optimal value of 1. FBS shows the ratio of rainy days detected by GPPs against the ones recorded by the rain gauges with an optimal value of 1. In summary, the GPP with the best rainfall detection capability should have a value close to 1 for POD, CSI, FBS and FD and a value close to 0 for FAR and FM. [SOURCE: Page_76]

The best indicator to assess rainfall detection capability is the miss bias as it refers to true rainfall recorded on the ground by the rain gauge but not detected by the satellite algorithm. False bias might not be an adequate indicator because, due to the coarse spatial resolution of the SREs, it is possible for a rain cloud to pass over an analyzed pixel but not pass the site of the in-situ observation. In that case, the gauge would not register rain even although there might be rain in that pixel that the satellite would properly record. Therefore, by using standard categorical statistics, such an event would then be interpreted as a false alarm, despite the fact that the satellite would have correctly detected a rain event (Lekula et al. 2018b; Gebremedhin et al. 2021). FM and FD were proposed by Gebremedhin et al. (2021) as better indicators because unlike the other categorical statistics, except POD, they are not dependent on false alarm. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Due to large uncertainty for light precipitations, a defined threshold value is often used to identify precipitation/no precipitation days (Mantas et al. 2015; Kenabatho et al. 2017; Lekula et al. 2018b; Zhou et al. 2020). A threshold of 0.5 mm d-1 was adopted in this study which is consistent with the threshold adopted by Kenabatho et al. (2017) and Lekula et al. (2018b) in Notwane catchment and Central Kalahari Basin in Botswana respectively. [SOURCE: Page_76]

75 [SOURCE: Page_76]

4.3.2. Continuous metrics Continuous metrics (equations 4.7 to 4.10) were used to assess the accuracy of the GPPs with respect to the reference rain gauge data quantitatively. Continuous metrics used in this study to compare gauge observations with GPPs at pixel level include mean error (ME), mean absolute error (MAE), root mean square error (RMSE) and Pearson correlation coefficient (r). ME is an average of the difference between the gauge rainfall and SRE. ME shows whether a GPP overestimates or underestimates rainfall. MAE evaluates the average size of the difference between GPP and gauge measurements without taking into account their direction. The RMSE is a quadratic scoring rule that gauges the average magnitude of an error as it gives high weight to large errors. ME values range from -∞ to ∞ while MAE and RMSE values range from 0 to ∞ and in all three cases, values closer to 0 indicate good performance. Pearson correlation coefficient (r) is a measure of the linear correlation between the reference daily gauge data and the daily GPPs with values ranging from +1 to -1. A value of 0 indicate that there is no linear correlation between in-situ observations of rainfall and satellite-derived rainfall, 1 implies perfect positive linear correlation, and -1 infers perfect negative linear correlation. 𝑀𝐸 = 1⁄𝑁 ∑𝑁                                                                               4.7 𝑖=1 𝑆𝑖 − 𝐺𝑖 [SOURCE: Page_77]

𝑀𝐴𝐸 = 1⁄𝑁 ∑𝑁                                                                          4.8 𝑖=1 |𝑆𝑖 − 𝐺𝑖 | [SOURCE: Page_77]

4.9 𝑅𝑀𝑆𝐸 = √1⁄𝑁 ∑𝑁 𝑖=1(𝑆𝑖 − 𝐺𝑖 )² [SOURCE: Page_77]

4.3.3. Bias decomposition Total Bias (TB), which is the cumulative difference between the SREs and gauge data, is based on aggregation of differences in rainfall volumes over the entire sample and therefore does not provide information on the sources of such differences. Tian et al. (2009) proposed decomposition of the total rainfall bias reported into three components, which include Hit Bias (HB), Miss Bias (MB) and False Bias (FB). This bias analysis gives an insight in the possible sources of SREs' algorithm errors hence providing valuable information on how to customize retrieval algorithms and error mitigation (Chen et al. 2021). The four bias components are as defined by equations 4.11 to 4.15: [SOURCE: Page_77]

𝐻𝐵 = (∑𝑁                𝑁 𝐼=1( 𝑆𝑖 − 𝐺𝑖 )/ ∑𝐼=1 𝐺𝑖 ): (𝑆 > 0 𝑎𝑛𝑑 𝐺 > 0)                                   4.12 [SOURCE: Page_77]

The value of HB can either be positive or negative, MB is always negative while FB is always positive. Assuming that the rain gauge represents the correct rainfall measurement over the pixel coinciding with the pixel for the SRE, then a negative HB signifies that the satellite algorithm underestimated rainfall while a positive value indicates an overestimation. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Additionally, the total mean squared error was decomposed into systematic and random components using a technique (equations 4.16 and 4.17) that was first applied by Willmott (1981) to decompose the error in numerical weather prediction models. [SOURCE: Page_78]

1 𝑁 1 𝑁 1 𝑁                                    4.16 2                  2 (∑(𝑆𝑖 − 𝐺𝑖 )2 ) = (∑(𝑆𝑖 ^ − 𝐺𝑖 ) ) + (∑(𝑆𝑖 − 𝑆𝑖 ^ ) ) 𝑁                  𝑁                  𝑁 𝑖=1                       𝑖=1                     𝑖=1 [SOURCE: Page_78]

𝑆𝑖 ^ = 𝑎𝐺𝑖 + 𝑏                                                                              4.17 [SOURCE: Page_78]

Where a and b are the slope and y-intercept and can be calculated using the least squares method. The first component of the equation represents the systematic error (𝐸𝑆 ) while the second component represent the random error (𝐸𝑟 ) and when expressed as a percentage of mean squared error, they add up to 100%. Systematic and random errors are impacted by several key impact factors such as seasonality, rainfall intensity and topography. [SOURCE: Page_78]

### 4.4.     Results

4.4.1. Overall performance of precipitation across the study area Table 4-3 shows a general overview of the performance of GPPs without considering spatial or temporal characteristics at daily scale. Both MSWEP with and without gauge performed similarly and therefore only the one without gauge, recommended by the developers to be used when evaluation is based on station data, is discussed in subsequent sections. In terms of rainfall detection capability, POD, FM and FD were all consistent where MSWEP performed best with average POD, FD and FM values of 0.8, 0.98, 0.2 respectively. This means it had the least missed rainfall events and the most hit events which translates to the best actual rainfall detection capability. This was followed by ERA$_5$-Land then IMERG while TAMSAT, ARC and CHIRPS, had the weakest performance. Both CMORPH had the lowest FAR of 0.45 and 0.47 for CMORPH Daily 0.250 and 30 min 8 km respectively, followed by TMPA (0.48) and ARC (0.51) while ERA$_5$-Land, CHIRP and IMERG had the highest FAR values of 0.72, 0.71 and 0.6. CMORPH Daily 0.250, CMORPH 30 min 8 km and MSWEP had the highest CSI of 0.44, 0.42 and 0.43 respectively, which translates to low miss bias and false alarm combined [SOURCE: Page_78]

77 [SOURCE: Page_78]

while IMERG, TAMSAT, CHIRP and ERA$_5$-Land ranked lowest with values of 0.35,0.29 and 0.27 and 0.26 respectively. [SOURCE: Page_79]

FBS values lower than 1 translate to more missed rain events while values higher than 1 translate to more false alarm. However, values close to 1 do not necessarily mean better detection capability because it could be due to missed events equaling false alarms as is the case with ARC with FBS value of 1.03 but FM and FAR of 0.49 and 0.51 respectively. Same case with CHIRPS and TAMSAT average FBS value of 1.21 and 0.84 and FM and FAR values of 0.52 and 0.43 and 0.53 and 0.59 respectively. FBS is therefore not an adequate indicator of precipitation detection capability on its own. [SOURCE: Page_79]

Continuous metrics (Table 4-3) echoed categorical statistics where MSWEP had the lowest ME, MAE and RMSE with values of 0.02-, 0.82- and 3.02-mm d-1 respectively. Conversely, despite the relatively weak performance of CHIRPS in terms of precipitation detection capability, it ranked second after MSWEP on the accuracy of precipitation intensity with a ME of 0.02 mm d-1, MAE of 0.91 mm d-1 and RMSE of 3 mm d-1. ERA$_5$-Land had the weakest performance based on continuous metrics with ME, MAE and RMSE values of 9-, 9- and 35-mm d-1 respectively. MSWEP and CMORPH had the highest correlation coefficient of 0.63 and 0.58 respectively while TAMSAT, CHIRP and ARC showed the lowest correlation with values of 0.33, 0.36 and 0.36 respectively. [SOURCE: Page_79]

The daily comparisons were also calculated at each reference station individually. The box plots of six categorical metrics and four continuous metrics are presented in Figure 4-3 and Figure 4-4 respectively. The categorical results show that ERA$_5$ Land, MSWEP and RFE$_2$ reported the highest number of correctly detected rainfall events and lowest number of missed rainfall events by generally obtaining higher Probability of Detection (POD) and Frequency of Detection (FD) values and lower Frequency of Miss (FM) at each station, while TAMSAT, ARC and CHIRPS reported the lowest number of correctly detected rainfall events and the highest number of missed rainfall events. False Alarm Ratio (FAR) box plots show rather close performance patterns for most GPPs except with the best performance from CMORPH and worst from CHIRP and ERA$_5$ which reported a high number of false events. Critical Success Index (CSI) metric, which is a measure of the overall precipitation detection capability of the GPPs, demonstrated that CMORPH and MSWEP had the best performances while ERA$_5$ Land, TAMSAT and CHIRP had the weakest performance. On the other hand, the continuous metrics (Figure 4-4) show that MSWEP and CHIRPS obtained the lowest values of ME, MAE and RMSE in all stations which translates to the most accuracy of estimated rainfall amounts among the GPPs while ERA$_5$, CMORPH 30 min 8 km and IMERG obtained the highest values which indicate the lowest accuracy. The highest correlation coefficient was obtained by MSWEP, both CMORPH and TMPA in all stations which is an indication of the highest correlation between reference gauge and GPPs while TAMSAT, ARC and CHIRP had the lowest correlation [SOURCE: Page_79]

78 [SOURCE: Page_79]

Figure 4-3: Box and whisker plots of categorical metrics at daily scale between Sep 2015 and August 2019 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Figure 4-4: Box and whisker plots of continuous metrics [SOURCE: Page_81]

In addition to presenting the statistical metrics results at the station level through boxplots, they are also illustrated based on geographical coordinates. This analysis enables an investigation of the spatial variability of the GPPs performance in the study area. Spatial variation of rainfall detection capability was illustrated using the CSI (Figure 4-5). The findings indicate weakest performance of all the GPPs in the south-western arid areas of the Namib desert, with north- eastwards increase in the performance. Similar results were exhibited by the correlation [SOURCE: Page_81]

80 [SOURCE: Page_81]

coefficient (Figure 4-6), with the exception of IMERG which depicts a reverse trend and TMPA, CMORPH and MSWEP which show relative spatial consistency. Based on RMSE, all the GPPs depict a similar SW-NE gradient with the lowest values in the driest part of the study area which increases north-eastwards to areas experiencing higher rainfall (Figure 4-7). Systematic error (Figure 4-7) was the most leading error component of the mean squared error in CHIRP in all stations while for CHIRPS, the systematic error was more dominant in the extremely arid and arid zones and decreased north-eastwards into the semi-arid to non-arid areas. Random error was the most dominant in all stations for IMERG, CMORPH, ERA$_5$ Land and PERSIANN while for TMPA and MSWEP, the random error was most dominant in the extremely arid and arid zones and decreased north-eastwards into the semi-arid and non-arid regions. [SOURCE: Page_82]

4.4.2. Performance of GPPs over different seasons As the seasonal dependency of the errors of GPPs has been documented (Tian et al. 2009; Chen et al. 2021; Ghorbanian et al. 2022), performance of the GPPs was also evaluated at seasonal scale based on both categorical and continuous metrics, using daily observations. Four seasons including September to November (SON), December to February (DJF), March to May (MAM) and June to August (JJA) were considered. Most of the rainfall occurs in the DJF season while JJA is the driest season. The results are presented using Performance Diagram and (Roebber 2009) Taylor Diagram (Taylor 2001) which enable multi-metric comparison of the GPPs. Results of categorical statistics, i.e., POD, success ratio (1-FAR), CSI and FBS, used to evaluate the GPPs in terms of precipitation detection capability are presented in Figure 4-8 for each season. In these diagrams, the GPPs closer to the right top corner had better detection capability of precipitation occurrence i.e., they had high POD and CSI values and low FAR and FBS values. The results show that ERA$_5$-Land, CHIRP, MSWEP and IMERG had the highest POD and CSI values while TAMSAT, ARC$_2$ and CHIRPS had the lowest values in all seasons. ERA$_5$-Land, CHIRP, IMERG and MSWEP had the lowest success ratio in three seasons (SON, DJF and MAM) while in JJA, MSWEP and RFE had the highest values. CHIRP and ERA$_5$- Land had the highest FBS in all seasons while TAMSAT, ARC$_2$ and CHIRPS had the lowest. [SOURCE: Page_84]

83 [SOURCE: Page_84]

Generally, the performance of the all the GPPs declined in the dry season (JJA) compared to other seasons with the best performance in DJF which is the wettest season. Taking all the categorical metrics presented in the Performance Diagram into account, CMORPH-DLY, RFE$_2$, and MSWEP performed best consistently in all seasons except from JJA for CMORPH Daily 0.250 while TAMSAT, ARC$_2$ and CHIRPS performed poorest. [SOURCE: Page_85]

Figure 4-8: Performance diagrams for comparing the GPPs using daily observations between September 2015 and August 2019 at seasonal scale: a) SON; b) DJF; c) MAM; d) JJA [SOURCE: Page_85]

The results of continous metrics i.e., RMSE, correlation coefficient and standard deviation, used to assess the GPPs in terms of the ability to provide accurate precipitation estimates, were presented using the Taylor’s diagram for each season Figure 4-9. In these diagrams, the GPPs closer to the reference station point illustrted in the horizontal axis, show better performances i.e., they had lower RMSE, higher CC values and SD values closer to the reference precipitation. ERA$_5$-Land had exremely high RMSE and standard deviation and therefore was excluded in the diagrams for better readability. In general, Figure 4-9 shows that all the GPPs obtained lowest RMSE and SD in the driest season (JJA) indicating better performance and highest values during the wettest season (DJF) indicationg weaker performance. In all the seasons, MSWEP was closest to the reference point, indicating best performance in terms of the the three prese nted metrics while CMORPH 30 min 8 km was the furthest in SON, DJF, MAM and IMERG in JJA indicating the poorest performance. [SOURCE: Page_85]

84 [SOURCE: Page_85]

4.4.3. Bias decomposition To further evaluate the SREs quantitatively, the biases were decomposed into HB, MB and FB (Table 3-3). Performance of SREs varied from one station to another but generally, based on total bias, all SREs over-estimated rainfall with CHIRPS exhibiting the lowest bias of 1% followed by MSWEP with a value of 8 % ERA 5-Land performed the poorest as it over- estimated rainfall by a very big margin with a total bias of >1000%. This was followed by IMERG and CMORPH 30 min 8 km with total biases of 198% and 144 % respectively. RFE, CHIRPS, MSWEP and CHIRP showed an under estimation of correctly detected rainfall (negative hit bias) while the rest of the SREs showed an over-estimation with ERA$_5$-Land, CMORPH 30 min 8 km and IMERG depicting the highest over-estimation at 560%, 99% and 59% respectively. [SOURCE: Page_86]

MSWEP had the lowest miss bias at 9% followed by RFE and ERA$_5$-Land at 12% and 14% respectively. On the other hand, TAMSAT, ARC and CHIRPS had the highest miss bias at 45%, 36% and 28% respectively. MSWEP had the lowest false bias, followed by CMORPH Daily and CHIRPS at 33%, 36% and 38% respectively while ERA$_5$-Land, IMERG, and CHIRP and RFE had the highest value of false bias at 515%, 156% and 79% respectively. [SOURCE: Page_86]

85 [SOURCE: Page_86]

Figure 4-10: Total and false bias scores at the rain gauge station level for the thirteen GPPs at daily temporal resolution for a period of four hydrological years (Sep 2015 to Aug 2019) The error components were also illustrated per station and based on geographical coordinates (Figure 4-10 and Figure 4-11). The results show that the total bias was generally higher in the Namib desert and north-eastern areas associated with high precipitation rates. ERA$_5$-Land and CMORPH-30min largely over-estimated precipitation in all stations with total bias greater than 100% and high false bias and positive hit bias, followed closely by IMERG. Miss bias was relatively low for all the products and depicted a general SW-NE increasing trend in most of the GPPs with the lowest values for MSWEP, ERA$_5$-Land, RFE and the highest for TAMSAT and ARC$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_87]

The error components were also analyzed for the four seasons and generally the GPPs performed the weakest during the JJA dry season. ERA$_5$-Land over-estimated precipitation in all seasons with high total, hit and false biases followed by CMORPH-30min with lower false bias but high total and hit bias. CHIRPS, ARC$_2$, TAMSAT and PERSIANN-CCS-CDR under- estimated precipitation during the JJA dry season as depicted by the negative total and hit biases and relatively high miss bias. MSWEP under-estimated (negative hit bias) correctly detected rainfall CHIRPS had the lowest total and hit bias in all seasons except the JJA season. [SOURCE: Page_87]

86 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Additionally, MSE was decomposed into systematic (𝐸𝑠 ) and random (𝐸𝑟 ) errors (Table 3-3). Systematic error ( 𝐸𝑠 ) was most dominant for CHIRP with an average of 71% and 52% respectively while IMERG and CMORPH had the lowest values of 15% and 19% respectively. MSWEP had an average standard error value of 45% with a noticeable increasing eastwards trend which is in contrast with CHIRPS with a decreasing eastwards trend. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Analysis at rain gauge station level (Figure 3.13) showed that CHIRP had systematic bias as the leading error component of the mean squared error in all stations followed by TMPA, MSWEP and CHIRPS while ERA$_5$-Land, CMORPH and IMERG had random error as the highest component. [SOURCE: Page_88]

87 [SOURCE: Page_88]
