4.5.    Discussion Several GPPs have been developed and employed for different purposes worldwide. However, their accuracy varies from one region to another and lack of quantitative confidence in GPPs remains a key concern that should be addressed appropriately. This is because any existing inaccuracy in precipitation predictions, such as magnitude inaccuracies, will propagate into models into which they are incorporated and their final outcomes. For example, errors in precipitation estimates may have an impact on optimized parameters in hydrological modelling, and the introduced error may be amplified through parameter optimization, resulting in higher uncertainties and making the final modelling results less reliable, whereas under-estimation or over-estimation may have an impact on flood and drought monitoring (Ghorbanian et al. 2022). Therefore, it is important to evaluate GPPs in order to quantify the magnitudes of errors and identify the error characteristics adequately. This enables one to select the best product for a certain application as well as simulate error propagation and hence provide results with related uncertainties as supplementary information, enabling informed decision making. Furthermore, the evaluation results in any specific region could be viewed as a useful proxy to foster improvements in GPPs internationally and regionally. [SOURCE: Page_89]

This study evaluated thirteen GPPs using in situ observations from 37 rain gauge stations from September 2015 to August 2019 throughout Namibia and Botswana as the reference. Both categorical continuous and metrics as well as error component analysis through bias decomposition were considered for the evaluation process. Generally, the findings showed notable differences in the performance of the GPPs even though with the exception of CHIRP and MSWEP-no gauge, they have been directly or indirectly bias corrected with station data. This discrepancy could be attributed to the different data sources, and different approaches and [SOURCE: Page_89]

88 [SOURCE: Page_89]

algorithm principles for blending and merging the datasets and different bias adjustment procedures adopted by each GPP as well as the number of gauge datasets assimilated into each GPP. [SOURCE: Page_90]

In general, the results showed that with the exception of MSWEP, which portrayed superior performance i.e., best precipitation detection capability and quantitative accuracy of the precipitation with respect to the reference data, the performance of the GPPs was highly dependent on the evaluation metrics used. For instance, ERA$_5$-Land performed well in terms of rainfall detection as portrayed by the POD and FM but had a high FAR and largely over- estimated correctly detected rainfall events, and also performed the weakest based on continuous metrics. CHIRPS performed poorly in terms of precipitation detection capability but performed second best after MSWEP in terms of quantitative accuracy as depicted by continuous metrics and bias decomposition. All GPPs over-estimated precipitation values with the least over-estimation by CHIRPS and MSWEP and the highest by ERA$_5$-Land, CMORPH- 30min 8 km and IMERG. Over-estimation of precipitation by ERA$_5$ on monthly scale were also reported by Terblanche et al. (2022) in South Africa and Ollivier et al. (2023) in Madagascar. [SOURCE: Page_90]

A discrepancy was observed in the performance of CHIRP and CHIRPS where CHIRP performed better in terms of detection of precipitation occurrence with a POD of 0.72 in contrast to a value of 0.57 by CHIRPS. This could be due to the station blending procedure used or the use of the SASSCAL data some of which has already been merged with CHIRPS data for evaluation. This shows that using data that has been already integrated with the GPPs as validation datasets could give misleading information of the actual accuracy of the GPPs (Mekonnen et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_90]

TAMSAT and PERSIANN CCS-CDR products, which provides relatively high-resolution data compared to the other GPPS, showed relatively weak performance especially in terms of rainfall detection capability. This indicates that a higher spatial resolution does not always result in more accurate estimates. Similar results for, TAMSAT, were reported by Mekonnen et al., (2023) who evaluated performance of eight GPPS over Africa. The poor performance of these products could be attributed to the sources of the input data as they both utilize only IR and gauge measurements for calibration, rainfall retrieval algorithms and data merging techniques used in their development (Mekonnen et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_90]

Furthermore, the station-levels analysis revealed that the performance of the GPPs was also significantly impacted by climate with over-estimation/under-estimation with larger magnitudes occurred in regions with higher precipitation rates. Similar results have been reported by other studies such as Ghorbanian et al., (2022), Mekonnen et al., (2023) and Ollivier et al., (2023) in Iran, Continental Africa and Madagascar respectively. [SOURCE: Page_90]

89 [SOURCE: Page_90]

The results obtained from this study will aid researchers in selecting the most suitable daily GPP for specific application in Namibia and Botswana. For instance, some applications such as hydrological water balance assessment at a certain time scale require precipitation estimates to be accurate. CHIRPS and MSWEP would be appropriate for this application as depicted by RMSE and the total bias (TB) which are an indication of their ability to give accurate precipitation estimates quantitatively. On the other hand, for some type of calendar related applications such as agricultural operations, the timing of rainfall events is more important than accurate estimation. For this application, MSWEP and CMORPH would be more appropriate as depicted by the Critical Success Index (CSI) and Pearson correlation coefficient (r). MSWEP would most appropriate for applications such as drought detection and flood monitoring for which both timing of the rainfall events and accuracy of the estimates are important for developing early warning systems and disaster management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_91]

4.5.1. Uncertainty and limitations of the study In this study, the point to pixel comparison method was implemented to assess the performance of GPPs in Namibia and Botswana. Accordingly, the results may under value the exact accuracies of the GPPs due to mismatches between point measurements of the reference datasets and pixel measurements of the GPPs. The different spatial resolution of GPPs could also be a source of uncertainty and the limited representativeness of the point measurements could affect the results. The temporal inconsistency between accumulation of GPPs and local time of recording in situ could also introduce some uncertainties into the daily validations. Therefore, the actual accuracy of GPPs could probably be higher than the results of this study. Given the nature of precipitation in the region, where rainfall is extremely localised, the spatial resolution of the GPPs and the number of reference rain gauges used in this study, the analysis undertaken may only provide a broad picture of the accuracy of the GPPs and may not depict true rainfall variability at the local scale. [SOURCE: Page_91]

4.5.2. Current State and Future Perspectives The study area is dominated by arid and semi-arid climate zones, which are characterised by low precipitation and high evapotranspiration rates. The region is prone to climate hazards such as droughts and floods (Bailey et al. 2014a, b). Meanwhile, the events are coupled with over- abstraction of groundwater leading to declining groundwater levels (JICA 2002; Lekula and Lubczynski 2019). Proper water management requires access to high quality, reliable long-term precipitation data for better monitoring of water resources as well as more accurate monitoring of relevant phenomena such as drought and floods. Generally, while most GPPS combine inputs from many sources (visible, infrared, passive/active microwave sensors, re-analysis and in situ data), each product is limited by the individual sources, precipitation retrieval algorithms and merging approaches and, thus, no one GPP is capable of simulating actual surface precipitation (Mekonnen et al. 2023). For example, the imprecisions of infrared sensors in distinguishing clouds and clouds temperature might result in false alarms and poor detection capability, particularly in subtropics and semi-arid locations with light rain as evidenced by TAMSAT and PERSIANN-CCS-CDR in this study, but may perform best in tropical climates. Microwave sensors have had some success compared to infrared sensors but are limited by data resolution both spatial and temporal (Kidd and Levizzani 2019). The reanalysis rainfall product (ERA$_5$ Land) derived from numerical weather [SOURCE: Page_91]

90 [SOURCE: Page_91]

models and observations from various sources has limitations in detecting and predicting tropical rainfall, which could be linked to the 4-Dimensional Variation data assimilation method (Mekonnen et al. 2023). On the other hand, rain gauges are usually sparsely distributed and therefore are only representative of the microclimate of the area where they are located but not the surrounding areas. [SOURCE: Page_92]

The findings of this research indicate that MSWEP had the superior performance over the other GPPs based on the assessment criteria used, although it has a relatively high false bias. The superior performance of MSWEP could be attributed to the combination of multiple datasets from different sources as well as the efficacy of the applied data merging and blending techniques. Moreover, studies have reported that GPPs merged with station data perform better than their counterparts not merged with in situ data (Satgé et al. 2019; Mekonnen et al. 2023; Ollivier et al. 2023). Therefore, the GPPs could be further modified and enhanced in order to provide more precise estimates of precipitation in the region, either through bias correction at basin/country or regional scale, or applying an appropriate GPP ensemble-merging approach (Ollivier et al. 2023; Zhao 2023; Zhang et al. 2023), or both. For instance, ERA$_5$ and IMERG, both of which are incorporated in MSWEP have relatively high precipitation detection capability as depicted by the high POD values of 0.79 and 0.75, low miss bias with fraction of miss (FM) of 0.21 and 0.25 but high false alarm ratio (FAR) of 0.72 and 0.6 respectively. This is reflected in MSWEP which ranks best among the evaluated GPPS in terms of POD (0.8) and FM (0.2) but has a relatively high false alarm of 0.5. The false bias in MSWEP could thus be reduced by using it together with another product with low false bias such as CMORPH-CDR at daily scale (CMORPH Daily 0.250) as well as gauge observations from national databases which are not already available in GSOD and GHCN-D. [SOURCE: Page_92]

4.6.     Conclusion Several GPPs, based on satellite data, re-analysis, gauge data or a combination of two or all of these have been developed in the recent decades, making precipitation data available even in regions with low-density rain gauge networks, such as Namibia and Botswana. However, these products are characterised by inherent errors and their accuracy varies in space and time. It is therefore essential to evaluate their performance in order to choose the most robust product for a certain application. In this study, thirteen GPPS (12 SREs and 1 re-analysis) were evaluated over a period of four years in Botswana and Namibia, using rain gauge measurements from 37 rain gauges as reference. The evaluation was done on daily and seasonal scale using categorical and continuous metrics as well as bias decomposition to identify the dominant type of error i.e., either hit, miss or false bias and systematic and random errors. The daily scale evaluation revealed that, with the exception of MSWEP, which demonstrated superior performance (best precipitation detection capability and quantitative accuracy of precipitation with respect to reference data), the performance of the GPPs was highly dependent on the evaluation metrics used. MSWEP also performed best consistently at gauge level and seasonal scale. The results of this study provide useful information to guide researchers in selecting the most suitable product for hydrological, agriculture, drought and flood monitoring within the region. [SOURCE: Page_92]

91 [SOURCE: Page_92]

5.      Multi-objective calibration of an IHM of Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System using groundwater levels and GRACE TWS [SOURCE: Page_93]

Abstract Distributed integrated hydrological models (IHMs) are considered to be the most effective tools for studying and analyzing water balance dynamics and predicting aquifer responses to groundwater abstraction and climate and land use change. However, these models are subject to structural and parametric uncertainties due to lack of or insufficient field measurements. In recent years, the Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment (GRACE) satellite mission has demonstrated great potential for tracking total water storage changes over large basins. Motivated by the demonstrated success of GRACE for monitoring groundwater storage changes, as well as calibration of hydrological and standalone groundwater models, this study explored the combined use of in situ groundwater level measurements and GRACE TWS for calibration of an IHM of Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS). The IHM was achieved by coupling MODFLOW 6 with UZF Package and was mainly constructed using remote sensing data as model forcings. The study demonstrated the effectiveness of the UZF- MODFLOW$_6$ IHM in modelling a multi-layered aquifer system, providing a quantitative assessment of subsurface water balance components and changes in storage. The findings revealed that the UZF-MODFLOW$_6$ model, unlike the standalone MODFLOW model, effectively simulates the fluxes between land surface models and groundwater, thus enhancing the understanding of the aquifer's water budget. The incorporation of GRACE total water storage (TWS) data as a calibration target allowed for better constraining of UZF parameters, improving the accuracy of simulated subsurface water fluxes. Moreover,the results indicated that for the IHM simulated storage changes to align with GRACE TWS, there must be no lag between simulated infiltration and evapotranspiration processes in the unsaturated zone. Notably, the unsaturated zone was found to account for the majority of subsurface storage within STAS, emphasizing its role in storing infiltrated water, which may evaporate before reaching the water table, a characteristic that may extend to similar aquifer systems. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Keywords: GRACE TWS, integrated hydrological modelling, multi-objective calibration, remote sensing, Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System, [SOURCE: Page_93]

92 [SOURCE: Page_93]

5.1. Introduction Groundwater recharge in arid and semi-arid areas is highly dependent on infiltration rate, permeability of unsaturated and saturated zones (Dandekar et al. 2018) and the thickness of the unsaturated zone (Cao et al. 2016). A thick layer of vadose zone stores a substantial amount of infiltrated water, which is lost by evapotranspiration so does not reach water table. Vadose zone processes also affect the travel time through the unsaturated zone and the time lag of actual recharge (Hunt et al. 2008; Cao et al. 2016). Estimation of groundwater recharge and groundwater evapotranspiration required as input into standalone groundwater models is challenging in such areas (Gee and Hillel 1998; Scalon et al. 2006), and groundwater exfiltration is not considered at all in standalone models. Therefore, distributed integrated hydrological models are the most effective tools for studying and analyzing water balance dynamics and predicting aquifer responses to groundwater abstraction and climate and land use change. (Keese et al. 2005). [SOURCE: Page_94]

IHMs of various complexities and capabilities have been developed and applied in the recent past. The most complex IHMs are those coupling atmospheric, surface and sub-surface by integrating three-dimensional physically based hydrological models with land surface models such as the Watershed Environmental Hydrology Hydro-Climate Model (WEHCY-HCM) (Kavvas et al. 2013; Kure et al. 2013) and the CATHY/NoahMP which couples physically based CATchment Hydrology (CATHY), which describes three-dimensional sub-surface flow in variably saturated media, with Noah LSM with multiple parameterization schemes (NoahMP), which accounts for energy, water, and carbon flux exchanges between various land surface elements and the atmosphere, among others. Although this complex approach honors the natural systems and allows an accurate characterization of mass and energy couplings between the groundwater component and other stores of the hydrologic cycle, implementation of such sophisticated models is challenging (Sun et al. 2012). [SOURCE: Page_94]

Another approach involves coupling only surface water and groundwater flow equations, either fully or ‘loosely’, to simultaneously simulate vertical water exchange between surface water bodies and the aquifers through the unsaturated zone, without considering the atmospheric processes. Fully coupled IHMs simulate the unsaturated zone by solving three-dimensional Richards’ equation. Examples include CATHY (Camporese et al. 2010), Hydrogeosphere (Therrien et al. 2004), ParFlow (Kollet and Maxwell 2006), InHM (VanderKwaak 1999), OpenGeoSys (Kolditz et al. 2012) HYDRUS-3D (Šimůnek et al. 2011), WASH123D (Cheng et al. 1998) and MODHMS (Panday and Huyakorn 2004). All these IHMs are computationally demanding and require fine spatial and temporal discretization due to large nonlinearity of Richards’ equation. [SOURCE: Page_94]

The ‘loosely’ coupled models simplify the Richards’ equation to simulate the vertical flow through the unsaturated zone and are more robust. Examples include MIKE SHE (DHI 2017), SWATMOD (Bailey et al. 2016) which links Soil and Water Assessment Tool (SWAT) with MODFLOW, HYDRUS Package (Seo et al. 2007) for MODFLOW 2000 (Harbaugh et al. 2000) and Unsaturated Flow (UZF) Package (Niswonger et al. 2006) for MODFLOW 2005 (Harbaugh 2005) and MODFLOW 6 (Langevin et al. 2017) which simplifies the 3D Richard’s equation to 1D. The UZF Package further simplifies the vertical, variably unsaturated flow by assuming [SOURCE: Page_94]

93 [SOURCE: Page_94]

that it is only driven by gravity potential gradient ignoring the negative potential gradients (Niswonger et al. 2006). The vertical flow is approximated with kinematic waves (Smith et al. 1983) and solved by the method of characteristics. Such a simplified solution is advantageous for basin scale applications because it requires less input data and computational power (Hassan et al. 2014; Lekula and Lubczynski 2019) and is comparatively accurate since the direction of unsaturated flow averaged over large grid cells is usually vertical (Mantoglou 1992; Chen et al. 1994). [SOURCE: Page_95]

The ability of an IHM to simulate hydrological processes and capture spatial-temporal aquifer dynamics accurately is highly dependent on the reliability and accuracy of input and forcing data. To assess groundwater flow system and flux (recharge and discharge) dynamics in both space and time, real-time or near-real-time data which include driving forces and state variables to constrain the model is required. However, such in situ data is unavailable in STAS as there is no up to date climatic monitoring network. In recent years, remote sensing has played an important role in providing spatio-temporal information such as rainfall, evapotranspiration and estimates of monthly terrestrial storage anomaly from the Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment (GRACE) mission, for evaluation and management of water resources, and its contribution to IHMs is rapidly increasing (Lubczynski et al. 2024). However, remote sensing is mostly used to provide input data and it is still a common practice to evaluate groundwater models and IHMs using in situ measurement of aquifer water levels from monitoring wells and stream discharge. However, these measurements are often scarce and do not represent basin scale groundwater dynamics subjecting the models to parametric uncertainties. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Since its launch in 2002, GRACE has provided continuous measurements of change in TWS, including groundwater, surface water, soil moisture and snow water. However, partitioning the total signal into the aforementioned components has been one of the main challenges of GRACE studies. Different approaches have been applied to estimate changes in groundwater storage from GRACE-based TWS. The most common is the direct approach which involves removing contributions of the different hydrological reservoirs i.e. surface water, soil moisture, snow and groundwater storage from the TWS (Frappart and Ramillien 2018). The contributions from the individual hydrological reservoirs are obtained from external sources, e.g., hydrological model outputs, in-situ data, or other remotely sensed observations. In arid and semi-arid areas where surface waters are negligible and snow non-existent such as STAS, changes in groundwater storage can be inferred by removing the anomaly of soil moisture from GRACE-TWS. Soil moisture data can be obtained from in situ measurements or from outputs of hydrological and land surface models. The main limitation of this approach is that the soil moisture estimates are usually from the top few meters and therefore may not be representative of the thick unsaturated zone which characterises arid and semi-arid areas. To provide more accurate estimates, outputs of integrated hydrological models (IHM) which simulate flow in the unsaturated zone and saturated zone should be used (Seyoum and Milewski 2016). [SOURCE: Page_95]

GRACE TWS has also been used to constrain both global and regional-scale land surface and surface hydrology models e.g., (Werth and Güntner 2009; Lo et al. 2010; Zhu et al. 2017; Demirel et al. 2019; Khaki 2023) but only few studies have incorporated GRACE data to calibrate groundwater models. The study by Sun et al. (2012) was the first to incorporate monthly terrestrial water storage anomaly from GRACE mission combined with in-situ water level measurements, to calibrate a regional groundwater model in Texas. Their results indicate that GRACE data helped further constrain model parameters. Hu and Jiao (2015) proposed a [SOURCE: Page_95]

94 [SOURCE: Page_95]

method to calibrate a large-scale groundwater model in data scarce areas using only GRACE data. They used monthly GRACE-derived groundwater storage anomaly converted into hydraulic head at each grid as the only model calibration target and compared the simulated heads to observed heads at three observation wells. Their results showed a reasonable match demonstrating that a model calibrated against GRACE data can catch the dynamic behaviour of observed hydraulic heads. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Seyoum and Milewski (2016) used an integrated hydrological model to quantify storage in the various terrestrial water compartments using MIKE-SHE IHM and in situ data (groundwater levels, soil moisture and stream discharge) and compared the results to GRACE derived terrestrial anomaly. The results showed that GRACE-derived TWS anomaly and the anomaly simulated from the model were generally in agreement on a monthly scale with few discrepancies and they concluded that GRACE data and IHMs are capable of reproducing observed trends in TWS. However, to the authors’ knowledge, no study has incorporated GRACE-TWS as an additional state variable for an IHM. The main goal of this study is to apply a multi-objective approach to calibrate an IHM of STAS using GRACE TWS as an additional calibration target in order to compensate for the insufficient head measurements and to quantify and analyse the spatial-temporal aquifer dynamics by using real-time or near-real-time data [SOURCE: Page_96]

### 5.2.   Data and Methods

5.2.1. Model set-up MODFLOW$_6$ coupled with Unsaturated Zone Flow (UZF) Package was used to simulate the water fluxes in the saturated and unsaturated zones, respectively because: (1) it is a relatively simple integrated modelling solution; (2) the study area is relatively dry with no permanent surface water bodies hence no need for sophisticated IHM solution; (3) it allows for computation of groundwater fluxes (gross recharge, exfiltration and subsurface evapotranspiration) internally while taking into account the delay between infiltration at the land surface and recharge at the water table, rather than assigning arbitrary recharge as is the case in standalone ground water models; (4) the Observation Package in MODFLOW 6 is flexible and allows definition of many different types of continuous in time or point in time observations which makes it easier to incorporate additional calibration targets; (5) supports pinching out of layers; (6) provides an option to use Newton-Raphson formulation which has the ability to solve problems involving drying and rewetting nonlinearities of the unconfined groundwater flow equation; (7) the UZF Package in MODFLOW 6 supports multi-layer option, which allows for vertical heterogeneity in unsaturated zone properties (8) it is public domain software. [SOURCE: Page_96]

ModelMuse graphical user interface (Winston 2019) was used for processing because (1) it is a public domain software; (2) it is easy and straightforward software with great technical support. Post-processing of cell by cell water budgets for each model layers was analysed using the zone budget (Harbaugh and Survey 1990) while the time series was evaluated using the GW charts (Winston and Virginia 2000). [SOURCE: Page_96]

95 [SOURCE: Page_96]

The study area was discretised into block-centred, uniform rectangular grids of 5 km by 5 km and six vertical layers, four aquifers and two aquitards, following the conceptual model described in chapter 3. The model top boundary representing the topography was defined using 30 m spatial resolution SRTM 1 Arc-Second Global digital elevation model obtained from https://earthexplorer.usgs.gov/ resampled to 5 km. Each layer was defined in the model by subtracting its interpolated thickness from model top for the topmost aquifer and from the bottom of the layer above for subsequent layers. In the top layer, 1D-vertical, variably saturated flow between land surface and water table, was simulated by the UZF Package. All the six model layers were defined as convertible to allow for conversion from confined to unconfined condition depending on the head position. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Inflow and outflow across the north west and north east boundaries per layer were simulated using the General Head Boundary (GHB) Package (Harbaugh et al. 2000) following the boundaries defined in the conceptual model (Figure 3.7). Conceptually, a GHB is a fixed head far from the model and it is assumed that it is not affected by the model stresses with time (GMS 2017). The flux across the boundary is proportional to the head difference between the water table and the specified boundary head and the constant of proportionality is the conductance (USGS 2020). The conductance is described as equal to 𝐾𝐿𝑊/𝐷; where K is the average hydraulic conductivity, W is the thickness of the saturated aquifer perpendicular to the flow direction, L is the boundary length perpendicular to the flow direction, and D is the distance from the general head boundary to the model boundary. The inflow and outflow boundary cells were defined using a polyline. The conductance interpretation was set as calculated to allow MODFLOW$_6$ to calculate an appropriate conductance for each cell based on the length of intersection between the polyline and the cell and the hydraulic conductivity was assumed to be equal to horizontal hydraulic conductivity. All the other boundaries were defined as no flow boundaries. The salt pans located in the south were simulated using the Drain Package which works similar to the GHB, except that with GHB there can be flow into and out of the model domain depending on the head while the Drain Package only simulates head dependent outflow from the model domain. [SOURCE: Page_97]

To quantify and analyse the spatial-temporal aquifer dynamics by using real-time or near-real- time data, a transient solution was required. Since initial time steps of transient models are strongly influenced by initial conditions, it is a common practice to use heads generated by a steady-state model as the initial conditions for a transient groundwater model. Furthermore, since steady-state models are easier to develop and use, developing one before a transient simulation could provide preliminary information about the distribution of horizontal hydraulic conductivity as well as the behaviour and responsiveness of the system that could be helpful in the implementation of the more complicated transient solution (Anderson and Woessner 1992). However, the UZF Package does not simulate evapotranspiration from the unsaturated zone in steady state (Niswonger et al. 2006) which results in unrealistically large gross recharge and does not provide satisfactory initial heads and initial water contents to initialize transient simulation (Niswonger et al. 2006; El-Zehairy et al. 2018; Lekula and Lubczynski 2019). Thus, a transient model was set-up and initialised with a warm up period of one year (September 2001 to August 2002) and followed by the normal simulation period of 14 years (September 2002 to August 2016). [SOURCE: Page_97]

96 [SOURCE: Page_97]

5.2.2. Driving forces Effective precipitation (Pe = P-Ei) (Niswonger et al. 2006) and effective potential evapotranspiration (PETe= PET-Ei) (Daoud et al. 2024) which are rainfall and potential evapotranspiration reduced by rainfall interception loss (Ei) further referred as interception, are the driving forces for the UZF Package, with Pe as the main driving force. In this study, as daily P, spatially variable satellite rainfall data from Multi-Source Weighted Ensemble Precipitation (MSWEP) (Beck et al. 2019a) was used. This data was used because it incorporates satellite imagery, re-analysis and-situ station data, and it accounts for gauge reporting times. Additionally, evaluation of several gridded precipitation products was conducted for Botswana and Namibia and MSWEP was found to perform best in terms of rainfall detection capabilities and quantitative estimations (see chapter 4). Spatial-temporal variable interception losses for STAS were estimated using the Revised Gash Model by Kagusi et al. (2023) who followed method proposed by Gebremedhin et al. (2023). Finally, for each time step, spatial-temporally variable effective precipitation (𝑃𝑒 ), was calculated as precipitation less interception rate. [SOURCE: Page_98]

PETe is required as the second driving force to set an upper limit of evapotranspiration in order to allow the IHM to compute the actual evapotranspiration. However, PET is considered to be the most confusing of the evaporation terms as there exists several definitions. In this study, the definition of McMahon et al. (2016) which summarizes PET as “the upper limit of evapotranspiration under constant meteorological and surface temperature conditions from a surface (vegetation, bare soil, or open water) that is saturated and of such extent to negate effects of local advection” was adopted. Since there is no sufficient in situ data within the study area to calculate PET, various remote sensing PET products were assessed in order to select the most suitable product to use in the IHM. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Some of the RS PET products with global or quasi-global spatial coverage and their spatial and temporal resolutions include: (i) CRU PET (Harris et al. 2020) and (ii) PT-JPL (Fisher 2018) at spatial resolution of 0.50 at monthly scale; (iii) FEWSNET PET at 0.10 and daily scale; (iv) MOD16 (Running et al. 2019) at 500 m every 8 days; (v) GLEAM (Martens et al. 2017) at 0.250 and daily scale; and hPET (Singer et al. 2021) at 0.10 every hour. CRU-PET, MOD16, FEWSNET and hPET are calculated based on the Penman-Monteith equation while GLEAM and PT-JPL are based on the Priestley-Taylor equation. Daily data was required for the IHM and, thus, to avoid the uncertainties associated with downscaling, only FEWSNET, GLEAM and hPET were applicable in this study. Since IHMs are less sensitive to PET than to rainfall (Lekula and Lubczynski 2019), the performance of PET data was not evaluated in detail. The hPET product, with the highest spatial (0.10) and temporal (hourly) resolutions, was selected. [SOURCE: Page_98]

hPET is based on the output of ERA$_5$-Land re-analysis meteorological variables dataset and is also provided as daily aggregation. hPET is calculated based on the FAO Penman-Monteith equation for reference evapotranspiration using seven meteorological variables which include (i) 10 m u-component (zonal) of wind speed [m s-1]; (ii) 10 m v-component (meridional) of wind speed [m s-1]; (iii) 2 m dew point temperature [K]; (iv) 2 m air temperature [K]; (v) surface net solar radiation [Jm-2]; (vi) surface net thermal radiation [Jm-2]and (vii) atmospheric surface pressure [Pa]. Since surface net solar radiation was used as a meteorological forcing instead of the 0.23 constant albedo applied in reference evapotranspiration in the FAO method, this product is considered as PET and not ETo (Singer et al. 2021), hence the land use land cover (LULC) factor (Kc) as proposed by studies such as Gebremedhin et al. (2022) was not applied to the data. [SOURCE: Page_98]

97 [SOURCE: Page_98]

5.2.3. Parameterization of the UZF Package UZF Package simulates flow in the unsaturated zone by approximating one dimensional form of Richard’s equation (Richards 1931) by kinematic wave equation that is solved using a method of characteristics (Smith et al. 1983). Steady state parameters used by the UZF Package to simulate unsaturated flow include initial (θi), residual (θr) and saturated (θs) water content, saturated vertical hydraulic conductivity (Ksat) and an exponent in the Brooks-Corey function (ε) while the time-variable parameters include the extinction depth (Dext) and the extinction water content (θext). The Brooks-Corey function (Brooks and Corey 1966) is used to evaluate unsaturated hydraulic conductivity as a function of water content following equation 5.1 . [SOURCE: Page_99]

𝜃 − 𝜃𝑟 𝜀                                     5.1 𝐾(𝜃) = 𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 [         ] 𝜃𝑠 − 𝜃𝑟 Where 𝐾(𝜃) is the unsaturated hydraulic conductivity as a function of water content (m d-1), 𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 is the saturated hydraulic conductivity (md-1), 𝜃 is the current soil water content, 𝜃𝑟 is the residual water content 𝜃𝑠 is the saturated water content and 𝜀 is the Brooks-Corey exponent. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Figure 5.1 summarises the processes within the unsaturated zone and the unconfined aquifer. The maximum infiltration rate (flow per unit area) (𝑞𝑎 ) across the land surface is limited to the vertical saturated hydraulic conductivity (𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 ). The infiltration rate at the surface is converted to water content in order to apply the characteristic solutions for unsaturated flow following equation 5.2. [SOURCE: Page_99]

𝑞𝑎 1⁄ 𝜀                                                                            5.2 𝜃𝑞𝑎 = (𝐾 )            (𝜃𝑠 − 𝜃𝑟 ) + 𝜃𝑟                     0 < 𝑞𝑎 ≤ 𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 𝑠𝑎𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_99]

where 𝜃𝑞𝑎 is the water content of a wave generated from infiltration and 𝑞𝑎 is the volumetric infiltration rate per unit area. The water content (𝜃𝑞𝑎 ) is set to 𝜃𝑠 when the user-specified infiltration rate is equal to, or exceeds, 𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 . If the applied infiltration rate is higher than 𝐾𝑠𝑎𝑡 or the water table is too shallow, some of the infiltrating water may be rejected. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Evapotranspiration is modelled by assuming that evaporation and uptake by roots can be grouped together as ET, and they occur as an instantaneous loss of water over a depth interval equal to extinction depth (Niswonger et al. 2006). ET is specified as a rate (length per time) within the UZF Package (input variable PETe) and is converted internally to a rate per unit depth by dividing the specified ET rate by the extinction depth (Dext) (equation 5.3). The volumetric rate of evapotranspiration is dependent on the volume of water stored in the unsaturated zone (θ) above the specified extinction depth (Dext) and is limited by the extinction water content (θext) if θ is less than or equal to θext. If the water table is above the extinction depth and the ET demand is not met by the unsaturated zone, then ET is removed directly from groundwater. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Groundwater ET can be represented either using the linear function of Harbaugh (2005) which assumes a linear decrease in ET with depth (equation 5.4) or the square-wave function that assumes constant ET rate over the extinction depth (equation 5.4 to 5.6) (Hughes et al. 2017) . [SOURCE: Page_99]

98 [SOURCE: Page_99]

ℎ − (𝑆𝑈𝑅𝐹 − 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 )                               5.3 𝐸𝑇𝑔 = 𝑃𝐸𝑇𝑟𝑒𝑠 (                      ) 𝐴𝑛 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_100]

where 𝐸𝑇𝑔 is the groundwater evapotranspiration, 𝑃𝐸𝑇𝑟𝑒𝑠 is the residual PET not satisfied in the unsaturated zone, h is the water elevation (head) in the cell, SURF is the ET surface elevation, 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 is the ET extinction depth and 𝐴𝑛 is the horizontal surface area of the cell n from which the loss occurs, [SOURCE: Page_100]

𝐸𝑇𝑔 = 𝑆𝑔 𝑃𝐸𝑇𝑟𝑒𝑠 𝐴𝑛                                      5.4 [SOURCE: Page_100]

where 𝑆𝑔 is a smoothing function that scales the residual PET calculated as: 𝑆𝑔 = −2𝛽 3 + 3𝛽 2                                       5.5 [SOURCE: Page_100]

and 𝛽 is a relative height of the water above 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 calculated as: ℎ − (𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 − 0.15𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 )                               5.6 𝛽= 0.15𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 [SOURCE: Page_100]

The option to simulate groundwater ET using the linear function was used in this study. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Figure 5-1: Schematization of the processes within the unsaturated zone and the unconfined aquifer: 𝑃 , precipitation; 𝑃𝑒 , effective precipitation; 𝐸𝑖 , interception rate; Ro, rejected [SOURCE: Page_100]

99 [SOURCE: Page_100]

infiltration; 𝑞𝑎 , infiltration rate, 𝑞𝑔 , recharge rate, 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 , extinction depth, 𝐸𝑇𝑢𝑧 ; unsaturated zone evapotranspiration; 𝐸𝑇𝑔 , groundwater evapotranspiration. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Flow from the unsaturated zone as recharge to the water table (𝑞𝑔 ) is dependent on ground- water head and the quantity of water stored in the unsaturated zone above the residual water content ( 𝜃 − 𝜃𝑟 ) (Langevin et al. 2017). However, since ground water recharge is dependent on the amount of storage and the downward flux rate in the unsaturated zone, there is no general equation that defines the relation between recharge and groundwater head. If the water table rises into the unsaturated zone then recharge is calculated as the sum of water flowing through the unsaturated zone and the volume of water stored in the unsaturated zone submerged by the rising water table (Niswonger et al. 2006; Langevin et al. 2017). [SOURCE: Page_101]

The UZF Package also allows for ground-water discharge to the land surface (exfiltration) whenever the water table in a cell is higher than the land-surface elevation. Exfiltration is calculated as a linear function of groundwater head. Rejected infiltration and exfiltration to the land surface can be routed over land and be instantaneously added to features of other advanced stress packages (Stream Flow Routing (SFR), Lake (LAK) , Multi-Aquifer Well (MAW) and UZF Packages) through the Mover Package (Langevin et al. 2017) not used in this study. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Spatially variable 𝐷𝑒𝑥𝑡 was defined based on different vegetation and soil types, following ESA GlobCover 2009 land cover map (Figure 2.3 b) (Bontemps et al. 2011) and soil map (Figure 2.4) (Jones et al. 2013). The values for each landcover and soil class ((Table 5-1)) were adopted from Shah et al. (2007) and were adjusted during model calibration. The soil parameters, θs, θr and Ksat were defined with the help of Africa soil map (Jones et al. 2013) and for each soil class, the values were assigned based on the study by Carsel and Parrish (1988) (Table 5-2) and θi and θext were defined as equal to θr. A default value of 3.5 was maintained for the Brooks Corey epsilon. Ksat was assumed to be equal to Kz of the saturated zone. Table 5-1: Extinction depths for different soil and land cover types [SOURCE: Page_101]

5.2.4. Parameterization of the Groundwater Flow Model – MODFLOW 6 The Newton-Raphson formulation with the under-relaxation option was used for its capability to handle wetting and drying difficulties due to non-linearity of unconfined groundwater flow equations (Niswonger et al. 2011; Langevin et al. 2017). The under-relaxation option was used because it provides stable solutions for Newton-Raphson method (Cooley 1983) and the delta- bar-delta (DBD) (Smith 1993) was used as the under relaxation scheme. The delta-bar-delta scheme is efficient in solving problems that exhibit oscillatory behaviour in the nonlinear iterations and is more robust than the other alternatives (Hughes et al. 2017) which include the method of Cooley (1983) and a simple method equivalent to the method used in the MODFLOW-2005 Preconditioned Conjugate Gradient Package (Hill 1990). The model complexity was defined as ‘complex’ and the values recommended for complex solutions (USGS 2018) were adopted for both linear and non-linear variables. Parameterization of the Node Property Package was done based on lithology of each hydrostratigraphic unit and values of horizontal hydraulic conductivity, specific storage and specific yield were adjusted during model calibration following a range of values outlined in section 3.3.6. Vertical hydraulic conductivities were estimated to be 10 orders of magnitude less than the corresponding horizontal hydraulic conductivity i.e., Kz = Kh /10. [SOURCE: Page_102]

5.2.5. Groundwater abstraction Groundwater abstraction in Namibia was specified per production borehole based on values estimated by UNESCO (2016) where the estimates were classified as domestic supply, livestock watering and irrigation. Apart from the supply to village centres by the municipality, there was no information on the aquifer tapped by the production boreholes. It was therefore assumed that abstraction for irrigation was from Auob Aquifer while for livestock watering was from Kalahari Aquifer. The estimates were provided as total annual abstraction in cubic metres per year and were converted to cubic metres per day with assumption that the same amount was abstracted every day for the whole simulation period. Abstraction from Ncojane wellfield was assumed to be equal to the amount recommended by WRC (2008) and was specified for each borehole from 2008 when the wellfield was completed to the end of simulation. The abstraction data for the South African part of STAS was obtained from the National Groundwater Archive. However, since only a few boreholes had the recorded abstraction data, the values estimated by ORASECOM (2009) was distributed evenly in time across all the boreholes. [SOURCE: Page_102]

### 5.2.6. State variables for model calibration

Hydraulic heads The groundwater monitoring network in STAS consists of a total of 46 piezometers (14 in Kalahari, 22 in Auob and 10 Nossob) with either daily water level measurements averaged from hourly measurements or monthly recordings (Table 2-4). However, some are located within the same grid which reduced the number of piezometers used to 33. The groundwater levels were converted to piezometric heads and used to constrain the model. However, the groundwater monitoring network is not well distributed (Figure 2.6) with most of the piezometers located in Namibia and the available time series data has gaps. GRACE TWS anomaly was thus used as an additional calibration target to compensate for the insufficient head measurements. [SOURCE: Page_102]

101 [SOURCE: Page_102]

GRACE TWS The GRACE mission, a joint effort between NASA (USA) and DLR (Germany) and NASA (USA) and the German Research Center for Geosciences (GFZ) for GRACE-FO, consists of two identical satellites orbiting in tandem at a height of about 500 km, with an along-track separation distance of about 220 km and an inclination of 89.5 (Tapley et al. 2004). The twin satellites' orbital velocity is affected by changes in the gravitational field brought on by changes in the mass beneath the satellites. This variation in orbital motion is measured by K-band microwave ranging device onboard the satellites. Since water is a significant contribution to this mass shift, the GRACE gravity anomaly measurement can be processed and transformed into a terrestrial water storage anomaly (TWSA) product (Wouters et al. 2014). [SOURCE: Page_103]

GRACE derived TWSA datasets are available publicly as Level-1b data (GRACE raw data), Level-2 (spherical harmonic coefficients), and Level-3 (TWSA monthly grids computed from either level-1b data using the mass concentration (mascon) solution or based on level-2 data. Mascon solutions provide advantages relative to spherical harmonics (SHCs) because: (1) they rely less on empirical scale factors, which can have significant uncertainty, and therefore should allow for improved spatial resolution and accuracy when studying trends in smaller scale hydrological basins (Watkins et al. 2015); (2) their basis functions regarding the synthesis of SHCs are represented locally rather than globally, which allows geophysical data constraints hence reducing empirical postprocessing requirements (Scanlon et al. 2016; Ferreira et al. 2023); (3) they reduce leakage from land to ocean hence increasing signal amplitude (Scanlon et al. 2016). At the moment, there are three research groups in the United States, namely the Centre for Space Research (CSR) (Save et al. 2016), Goddard Space Flight Center (GSFC) (Loomis et al. 2019), and Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) (Watkins et al. 2015) and one in Australia at the Australian National University (AUN) (Allgeyer et al. 2022), providing TWSA products for the public domain based on Mascon solutions. [SOURCE: Page_103]

The GRACE mascon solutions from CSR, GSFC, and JPL processing centres were downloaded from https://www^2.csr.utexas.edu/grace/, https://earth.gsfc.nasa.gov/geo/data/grace-mascons and GRACE Tellus (nasa.gov) , respectively. The data was obtained for the period between April 2002 and August 2016. CSR has a spatial resolution of 0.250 while GSFC and JPL have a spatial resolution of 0.50. The long-term average (2004 to 2009) was removed from each GRACE grid for the three products and the gaps in the data were taken into account to make GRACE data comparable with results from the model simulations. Figure 5.2 shows the temporal variability of area averaged change in storage from the three solutions. While there is a quantitative difference among the products with GSFC having the highest peaks and JPL the lowest, they all depict a similar trend which apart from the first three years is similar to that of rainfall. [SOURCE: Page_103]

102 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Figure 5-2: Area-averaged time-series of TWS for products from different processing centers (CSR, JPL, and GFZ). [SOURCE: Page_104]

5.2.7. Sensitivity analysis and model calibration Model calibration was done using the trial-and-error method in order to allow incorporation of subjective knowledge and to gain understanding of model behaviour during the calibration process that can be difficult using automated calibration (Anderson et al. 2015). A two-step calibration process was adopted in order to evaluate the impact of incorporating GRACE TWS as an additional model constraint, as summarised in (Figure 5.3). The model was, thus, first calibrated only on the field measured observations of groundwater heads until satisfactory objective functions were achieved. The calibration on heads was done by adjusting horizontal hydraulic conductivity (Kh), specific yield (Sy) and specific storage (Ss) through zonation per layer. The unsaturated zone parameters, apart from Ksat, were adjusted by creating zones of soil and landcover classes. The Ksat was assumed to be equal to the vertical hydraulic conductivity (Kz) of the first layer and together with conductance of the GHB and Drain boundary cells, they were adjusted as dependent on the Kh of the matching zones. Model performance relative to groundwater heads was evaluated using mean error (ME) (equation 5.7) and root-mean-square-error (RMSE) (equation 5.8) objective functions at each time step. Considering the scale and the accuracy of field measurements, the target objective functions at daily scale were ME < ±1.0 m, and RMSE < 1.0 m for groundwater levels. [SOURCE: Page_104]

1  n                                                   5.7 ME =     ∑ (hobs − hsim )i n  i=1 [SOURCE: Page_104]

n 5.8 1                 2 RMSE = √[ ∑ (hobs − hsim )i ] n i=1 [SOURCE: Page_104]

Sensitivity analysis was performed before calibration on GRACE TWS to gain better understanding of the influence of various parameters on both unsaturated zone (Pa, ETuz) and [SOURCE: Page_104]

103 [SOURCE: Page_104]

groundwater fluxes (ETg, Rg,) and change in storage. The unsaturated zone parameters tested include the saturated vertical hydraulic conductivity (Ksat), saturated water content (θs), extinction water content (θext) and extinction depth (Dext) while the MODFLOW$_6$ parameters include horizontal hydraulic conductivity (Kh) and specific yield (Sy) of the top Aquifer. θs, θext Sy and Dext were tested by applying multipliers of 0.7 and 1.3 to their calibrated values, while Ksat and Kh were tested using multipliers of 0.1 and 10. [SOURCE: Page_105]

The model was then re-calibrated while aiming to match both simulated heads and change in storage to observed heads and GRACE derived change in storage respectively. Correlation coefficient (r) was used to evaluate calibration on change in storage. [SOURCE: Page_105]

𝑜𝑏𝑠 𝐶𝑜𝑣(∆𝑆𝑠𝑠      𝑠𝑖𝑚 , ∆𝑆𝑠𝑠  )                                    5.9 𝑟=       𝑜𝑏𝑠      𝑠𝑖𝑚 𝜎∆𝑆𝑠𝑠  𝑥 𝜎∆𝑆𝑠𝑠 [SOURCE: Page_105]

5.2.8. Water balance analysis The IHM simulated water budget was analysed following equations 5.10 to 5.15. For the entire model domain i.e., unsaturated and saturated zones (equation 5.5), the main water input is actual infiltration (Pa) with lateral inflow (qin) through the north west inflow boundary as a minor input while the outputs are through rejected infiltration (Ro), sub-surface evapotranspiration (ETss) which consists of unsaturated zone evapotranspiration (ETuz) and groundwater evapotranspiration (ETg) (equation 5.11), lateral outflow (qout) through the north eastern outflow boundary, groundwater abstraction (qabs), groundwater exfiltration (Exfg) and ∆Sss is the total change in sub-surface storage which is a summation of change in storage in the unsaturated (∆Suz) and saturated zones (∆Sg) (equation 5.12). The surface and unsaturated zone lateral inflow and outflow are assumed to be negligible and therefore qin and qout consist of lateral groundwater inflow and outflow respectively. [SOURCE: Page_106]

𝑃𝑎 + 𝑞𝑖𝑛 = 𝑅𝑜 + 𝐸𝑇𝑠𝑠 + 𝑞 + 𝑞𝑎𝑏𝑠 + 𝐸𝑥𝑓𝑔 ± ∆𝑆                             5.10 [SOURCE: Page_106]

105 [SOURCE: Page_106]

𝐸𝑇𝑆𝑆 = 𝐸𝑇𝑔 + 𝐸𝑇𝑢𝑧                                         5.11 [SOURCE: Page_107]

∆𝑆𝑠𝑠 = ∆𝑆𝑔 + ∆𝑆𝑢𝑧                                         5.12 [SOURCE: Page_107]

The unsaturated zone water balance can be evaluated as [SOURCE: Page_107]

𝑃𝑎 = 𝑅𝑜 + 𝐸𝑇𝑢𝑧 + 𝑅𝑔 ± ∆𝑆𝑢𝑧                                    5.13 [SOURCE: Page_107]

where 𝑅𝑔 is the amount of water released from the unsaturated zone into the groundwater flow system as gross recharge and 𝑅𝑜 is rejected infiltration [SOURCE: Page_107]

The saturated zone water balance can be expressed as [SOURCE: Page_107]

𝑅𝑔 + 𝑞𝑖𝑛 = 𝑞𝑎𝑏𝑠 + 𝑞𝑜𝑢𝑡 + 𝑞𝑑𝑟𝑛 + 𝐸𝑇𝑔 + 𝐸𝑥𝑓𝑔𝑤 ± ∆𝑆𝑔                           5.14 [SOURCE: Page_107]

The net recharge (𝑅𝑛 ) is defined as follows [SOURCE: Page_107]

𝑅𝑛 = 𝑅𝑔 − (𝐸𝑇𝑔 + 𝐸𝑥𝑓𝑔𝑤 )                                     5.15 [SOURCE: Page_107]

where Rn is net recharge [SOURCE: Page_107]

### 5.3.    Results

5.3.1. Evaluation of model calibrated on heads Table 5-3 shows the calibrated UZF and MODFLOW$_6$ parameters while Figure 5.4 shows the comparison between simulated and observed groundwater heads for 33 piezometers over 14- year calibration period. Generally, there was a good agreement on the trends between simulated and observed groundwater levels in all the layers with the RMSE for individual wells ranging between 0.1 m and 2.47, and 31 out of the 33 wells having a RMSE of less than 1 m. Discrepancies in simulated groundwater heads are likely to be explained by: (i) averaging of the simulated heads within the 25 km^2 model grid; (ii) difference between field measurements of elevation and the DEM used to set the model top, which affects groundwater simulated in the model relative to the altitude at the well; (iii) unrepresented heterogeneity within and around the model grid; (iv) errors in model conceptualization; (v) errors in model parameterization; (vi) uncertainties in field observations of groundwater levels, and (vii) unaccounted for errors in water abstraction data. For instance, in WW39845 (Figure 5.4) the error was likely due to errors in model conceptualization or unrepresented heterogeneity since field measurements of elevation and elevation derived from DEM were almost similar in this particular well while the little spike in WW40963 could be due to errors in field measurements. The variability in most of the wells observing Auob such as WW39840, WW39843, WW39850, WW39857, WW40961 and WW40962 are likely due to unaccounted for pumping as the model was not able to reproduce the temporal variability patterns in this aquifer. The model calibration was regarded satisfactory taking into account the model's scale and the complexity of the hydrogeological system within the research area. [SOURCE: Page_107]

5.3.2. Sensitivity analysis Sensitivity analysis showed that the analysed fluxes were most sensitive to unsaturated zone (UZF) parameters with evapotranspiration from the unsaturated zone (ETuz) and gross recharge (Rg) being the most sensitive (Figure 5.5). Infiltration and rejected infiltration were only sensitive to the saturated vertical hydraulic conductivity (Ksat) where an increase in the parameter by a factor of 10 led to reduction in rejected infiltration (Ro) while decreasing the parameter by a factor of 0.1 increased Ro by several magnitudes. This is because infiltration rate is limited to Ksat (Hughes et al. 2017), such that if the applied infiltration rate is higher than Ksat, some of the infiltrating water may be rejected and if the Ksat is equal or higher than the applied infiltration rate, actual infiltration is equal to specified infiltration. [SOURCE: Page_110]

0.1 Ksat led to a decline in both unsaturated zone evapotranspiration (ETuz) and gross recharge (Rg) with an increase in the timespan over which ETuz took place by ~2 months while 10 Ksat led to an increase in the fluxes and reduced the time span over which ETuz took place by ~2 months. This is because Ksat, which is the ease with which water is transmitted through soil is governed by pore size distribution and the tortuosity of flow paths. Thus, low Ksat is a characteristic of fine textured soils with higher retention while high Ksat is a characteristic of coarse textured soils with limited water retention. Groundwater evapotranspiration (ETg) had minimal response to Ksat. This led to an increase in change in unsaturated zone storage (∆Suz) for 10Ksat with higher peaks and lower troughs, and an increase in change in groundwater storage (∆Sg) due to increased Rg , while 0.1Ksat led to a decline in both ∆Suz and ∆Sg. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Reducing saturated water content (θs) by a factor of 0.7 resulted into an increase in ETuz during the climbing limb up to the peak of the wet season, and a reduction during the falling limb as well as reduction of the timespan over which it took place by ~ 1 month while the inverse was observed for 1.3 θs (Figure 5.5b). 0.7 θs led to an increase in Rg taking place even during the dry season while 1.3 θs led to a decline in Rg. ETg had minimal response to θs with an overall increase in the flux for 0.7 θs and decline for 1.3 θs. This is because low θs is a characteristic of coarse textured soils such as sands and loamy sands whose large pores have limited ability to retain water. Hence, these soils lose water faster through drainage and evaporation. On the other hand, fine textured soils have greater θs and may hold more water, leading to reduced drainage and evaporation. This led to an increase in negative ∆Suz (lower troughs) and in increase in ∆Sg due to increased Rg for 0.7 θs and slight increase in positive ∆Suz (higher peaks) and a decline in ∆Sg for 1.3 θs due lower Rg. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Increasing θext by a factor of 1.3 resulted into a decrease in ETuz, an increase in Rg by several magnitudes and an increase in ETg (Figure 5.5c). This is because, high θext means that less water can be removed from the unsaturated zone through evapotranspiration. This increases the amount of water available in the unsaturated zone above the residual water content and hence more water is released to the water table as Rg. This leads to a rise in the water table and if ETuz does not meet the ET demand and the water table is within Dext, more water is removed from the groundwater, hence high ETg. 1.3θext caused a slight change in ∆Suz and an increase in ∆Sg by several magnitudes due to increase in Rg. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Evapotranspiration extinction depth (Detx) had a similar influence on the fluxes as θs (Figure 5.5d), where reducing the parameter by a factor of 0.7 resulted into an increase in ETuz during the climbing limb and a reduction during the falling limb as well as a reduction of the timespan over which it took place by ~1 month while the inverse was observed when the parameter was [SOURCE: Page_110]

109 [SOURCE: Page_110]

increased by a factor of 1.3. This resulted into an overall decline in ETuz for 0.7Detx and an overall increase in the flux for 1.3Detx. On the other hand, 0.7 Detx led to an increase in Rg while 1.3Detx led to a decline in the Rg. This is because a decline in ETuz avails more water in the unsaturated zone above the residual water content hence more is released into the water table as Rg. 0.7 Detx led to a decline in ETg due to the reduction in the depth below the surface in which ET can take place while the inverse was observed for 1.3 Detx. This led to an increase in negative ∆Suz (lower troughs) and in increase in ∆Sg due to increased Rg for 0.7 Detx and slight increase in positive ∆Suz (higher peaks) and a decline in ∆Sg for 1.3Detx due lower Rg. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Sensitivity analysis of specific yield (Sy) (Figure 5.5e) shows that ETuz was not sensitive to the parameter while groundwater fluxes were highly sensitive. Unlike the other parameters tested, changes in Sy were not reflected by proportional changes in Rg and there was no clear trend with peak Rg varying for 0.7Sy, medium Sy and 1.3Sy from one recharge event to another. On the other hand, ETg had an inversely proportional response to Sy where 0.7Sy led to an increase in ETg, while 1.3Sy led to a decline in the flux. This in turn resulted to no effect on ∆Suz for both 0.7Sy and 1.3Sy and an increase in ETg for 0.7Sy and a decrease for 1.3Sy. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Kh had very minimal to no influence on the fluxes but were most played a major role in controlling time series trend of the hydraulic heads (not shown in Figure 5.5), followed by Sy. Only monitoring wells located in the recharge zone were sensitive to UZF parameters with an increase in heads as a result of increasing recharge. [SOURCE: Page_111]

5.3.3. Evaluation of model calibrated on both hydraulic heads and ∆S Monthly simulated change in storage from the model calibrated on hydraulic heads was computed for the same dates as GRACE TWS in order to compare them before and during the simultaneous calibration on both hydraulic heads and GRACE TWS. As shown in Figure 5.6 a, while both datasets matched the rainfall peaks during wet seasons, with a lag in some months, their phase and amplitudes do not match, hence no correlation. The trial-and-error calibration process was, thus, conducted while examining the groundwater levels, change in storage in the saturated and unsaturated zones and the main subsurface fluxes i.e. infiltration (Pa), unsaturated zone evapotranspiration (ETuz), gross recharge (Rg) and groundwater evapotranspiration (ETg). Through the trial-and-error calibration process, it was found that a lag between Pa and ETuz, the main inflow and outflow fluxes of the unsaturated zone respectively, was the cause of the mismatch between the phases of the IHM simulated change in storage and GRACE TWS. Based on the sensitivity analysis (Figure 5.5) results, saturated hydraulic conductivity (Ksat), saturated water content (θs), residual water content, (θr) and evapotranspiration extinction depth (Dext) were adjusted accordingly. This resulted into a decline in ETss an increase in Rg and Rn and hence increase in ∆S. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Simultaneous calibration of the IHM on both groundwater levels and GRACE TWS resulted into generally lower saturated water content (θs) and evapotranspiration extinction depth (Dext) and a higher residual (θr) and extinction water content (θext), saturated vertical hydraulic conductivity (Ksat) and Brooks Corey Epsilon (ε), while saturated zone parameters mostly remained the same (Table 5-3). This is because, based on the sensitivity analysis results (Figure 5-5), unlike the unsaturated zone parameters, the saturated parameters had very low impact on the simulated change in storage. [SOURCE: Page_111]

110 [SOURCE: Page_111]

As can be seen in Figure 5.6 a and b, there was a notable increase in ∆Sg from the model calibrated on both heads and ∆S as from 2011 which is the wettest hydrological year, and consequently on the simulated groundwater levels in the Kalahari piezometers located in the western part and upper reaches of fossil rivers (Figure 5-4). This is because, GRACE TWS depicts an increasing trend throughout the simulation period (Figure 5-2). Additionally, while the IHM simulated ∆Suz exhibits higher frequency signal before calibration on ∆S (Figure 5.6 a), the troughs in simulated ∆Suz and hence ∆S (Figure 5.6b and c) were smoothened after calibration on ∆S. There was also a reduction in the time lag between IHM simulated ∆S and TWS, resulting into an increase in r from 0 to 0.19 and 0.21 for ∆Suz and ∆S respectively. However, the magnitudes of IHM ∆S are more subdued than GRACE TWS. A better correlation between TWS and IHM ∆S could not be achieved because IHM ∆S is dependent on the rainfall data used which had a correlation coefficient of 0.34 with the ensemble of GRACE TWSA data from JPL, CSR and GSFC. A better correlation could probably be achieved with rainfall data with a better correlation with TWS. Moreover, GRACE data has gaps which are dominant from around 2011. [SOURCE: Page_112]

111 [SOURCE: Page_112]

Figure 5-4: Sensitivity analysis of rejected infiltration (Ro), unsaturated zone evapotranspiration (ETuz), gross recharge (Rg), groundwater evapotranspiration (ETg), change in unsaturated zone storage (∆Suz) and change in groundwater storage (∆Sg) in response to changes in (a) saturated hydraulic conductivity (Ksat); b) saturated water content (θs); (c) evapotranspiration extinction water content (θext) (d) evapotranspiration extinction depth (Dext) and (e) specific yield (Sy) [SOURCE: Page_113]

112 [SOURCE: Page_113]

Figure 5-6: Comparison of monthly TWSA with IHM simulated a) ∆Sg and ∆Suz before calibration on TWSA; b) ∆Sg and ∆Suz after calibration on TWSA; c) total ∆S before and after calibration on TWSA and average monthly precipitation (blue column bars) for STAS for 2003 to 2016 hydrological years. [SOURCE: Page_114]

5.3.4. Water balance analysis The 14-year mean annual water budget of the entire basin for both models is presented in Figure 5.7. The simulated inflow into the model domain consisting of Pa = 255.6 mm yr-1 (~94% of P) and qin = 0.6 mm yr-1 was the same for both models. Ro and ETss simulated by the model calibrated on heads were 8.8 mm yr-1 and 247 mm yr-1 and were reduced to 0.05 mm yr-1 and 219.8 mm yr-1 by the model calibrated on both heads and change in storage while Rg was 1.01 mm yr-1 and increased to 8.25 mm yr-1 while Exfg was the same for both models at 0.01 mm yr- 1 [SOURCE: Page_114]

The input into the unsaturated zone consists only of Pa = 255.6 mm yr-1 (~94% of P), while the output consists of ETuz = 245.2 mm yr-1 (97.9% of Pa) and 217.9 (97.9% of Pa) and Rg =1.0 mm yr-1 and 8.25 mm yr-1 from the model calibrated on heads and both heads and storage respectively. This resulted into a ∆Suz of 0.84 mm yr-1 and 29.62 mm yr-1 for the model calibrated on heads and both heads and storage respectively. Dominance of ETuz over Rg is due to a thick Kalahari unsaturated zone made up primarily of coarse grained sandy soils and large vapour pressure deficit due to high evapotranspiration demand, which favours ETuz and limits Rg to particularly wet seasons with heavy rain showers (Lekula and Lubczynski 2019). [SOURCE: Page_114]

The input into the saturated zone consists of Rg =1.0 mm yr-1 and 8.25 mm yr-1 for the model calibrated on heads and both heads and storage respectively and qin = 0.6 mm yr-1 for both while outflow consists of ETg = 1.83 mm yr-1 and 1.89 mm yr-1and qdrn = 0.02 mm yr-1 and 0.1 mm yr- 1 from the model calibrated on heads and on both heads and storage respectively and Exfg = 0.01 mm yr-1, qout = 0.09 mm yr-1, qabs = 0.08 mm yr-1 for both models. This resulted into Rn = - 0.83 mm yr-1 and 6.35 mm yr-1 and ∆Sg =0.44 mm yr-1and 6.6 mm yr-1 for the model calibrated on heads and on both heads and storage respectively. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Reduction of ETuz and increase in Rg in the model calibrated on both heads and storage is because to match the IHM ∆S to GRACE TWS, there should be no lag between Pa and ETuz. The possible parameter adjustments that could help achieve this based on sensitivity analysis results include increasing Ksat, θr and θext and reducing θs and Dext. Increasing Ksat increases drainage hence reducing the amount of rejected infiltration, while increasing θext reduces the [SOURCE: Page_114]

113 [SOURCE: Page_114]

amount of water available for evapotranspiration from the unsaturated zone. Decreasing θ s reduces the soil’s ability to hold water while decreasing Dext reduces the depth over which ET can take place hence a reduction in ET. All these are characteristics of coarse textured soils and they resulted into a decrease in ETuz and favoured recharge into the water table. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Figure 5-7:14 year mean precipitation (P), Interception (Ei), Effective Precipitation (Pe) and simulated fluxes from the model calibrated on hydraulic heads only (blue bars) and one calibrated on both hydraulic heads and GRACE TWS (green bars). Actual infiltration (Pa), rejected infiltration (Ro), subsurface ET (ETss), unsaturated zone ET (ETuz), groundwater ET (ETg), groundwater exfiltration (Exfg), gross recharge (Rg), net recharge (Rn), lateral groundwater inflow (qin), lateral groundwater outflow (qout), groundwater outflow through the pans (qdrn), groundwater abstraction(qabs), change in subsurface storage (∆S), change in unsaturated zone storage (∆Suz), change in groundwater storage (∆Sg) [SOURCE: Page_115]

5.3.5. Daily and annual variability of water fluxes The difference between the temporal variability of simulated fluxes from both models is quite large especially from 2010 hydrological year which is most noticeable for both Rg and Rn at both daily and annual scales (Figure 5.8 and Figure 5.9 a). For ETuz, the most noticeable difference at daily scale is the lower lag between Pa and ETuz from the model calibrated on both heads and change in storage (Figure 5.8) while at annual scale it is clear that ETuz is lower for the model calibrated on both heads and change in storage throughout the simulation period (Figure 5.9 a). ETg is almost similar for both models while Exfg is negligible for both because they both depend on the depth to water table which is also why they do not vary much between dry and wet years. [SOURCE: Page_115]

The total subsurface change in storage from both models (Figure 5.9 b) was dominated by storage anomalies in the unsaturated zone throughout the simulation period. For the model calibrated on heads, ∆Sg was negligible and exhibited a rather stable trend throughout the simulation period while the one from the model calibrated on both heads and storage was negligible from 2003 to 2009 hydrological years and increased from 2010 hydrological year. On the other hand, ∆Suz followed the rainfall trend and was consistently higher for the model calibrated on both heads and ∆S throughout the simulation period. The difference in ∆Sg was due to increased Rg and relatively similar ETg while for ∆Suz it was due to the reduced Ro and ETuz. [SOURCE: Page_115]

114 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Figure 5-7: Daily variability of different water components over the 14-year simulation for entire STAS: Pa actual infiltration, ETuz unsaturated zone evapotranspiration, Rg gross recharge, ETg groundwater evapotranspiration, Rn net recharge [SOURCE: Page_116]

Figure 5-8: Annual variability of a) simulated fluxes and b) simulated change in storage over the 14-year simulation for entire STAS. The continuous and dotted lines represent fluxes simulated from the model calibrated on heads only and one calibrated on both head [SOURCE: Page_116]

5.3.6. Spatial variability of groundwater fluxes Rg simulated by the model calibrated on heads only occurs on the upper reaches of the fossil rivers and some parts of the western part of STAS where the water level is relatively shallow in both the driest (2007) and wettest (2011) hydrological years during the simulation period. This area coincides with the potential recharge zones identified by Tweed (2021) (Figure 3-7) and is characterised by presence of relief depressions which hold water after rainfall events hence creating favourable conditions for Rg. This is in agreement with previous studies such as JICA [SOURCE: Page_116]

115 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Figure 5-9: Spatial variability of (a) gross recharge Rg, (b) groundwater evapotranspiration ETg, (c) groundwater exfiltration Exfg and (d) net recharge Rn for the driest (2007) and wettest (2011) hydrological years during the simulation period before and after calibration on GRACE TWS respectively [SOURCE: Page_117]

(2002) who noted that younger water occurs in the north-western part of the basin and along upper reaches of Auob and Nossob rivers, confirming the importance of ‘floodwater’ recharge in the basin. ETg is restricted to the upper reaches of the fossil rivers which have been [SOURCE: Page_117]

116 [SOURCE: Page_117]

encroached by deep rooted alien prosopis trees (Prosopis spp) and the south-western part where the Dwyka Group outcrops and large evaporation pans such as Hakskeen Pan are located and the water table is relatively shallow at less than 10 m below the ground surface, both before and after calibration on GRACE TWS. The spatial extent of ETg in the south-western part is smaller after calibration on GRACE TWS in both 2007 and 2011 while Rg takes place at a much larger scale including the north-eastern part of STAS, after calibration on GRACE TWS in both years. Exfg is negligible as it occurs at very few grids along the upper reaches of Nossob River and was zero in 2007 before calibration on GRACE TWS. [SOURCE: Page_118]
