### 5.4.    Discussion

5.4.1. Use of GRACE TWS to constrain the IHM of STAS IHMs are data demanding with many parameters to be optimized and thus one of their limitations is nonuniqueness where many different combinations of model inputs and parameters produce reasonable model (Anderson et al. 2015). Model parameters are usually spatially distributed, except for small scale models where homogeneity can be assumed. The scale dependency of model parameters makes it inappropriate to constrain groundwater models and IHMs using point measurements as there could be an infinite number of possible solutions (Sun et al. 2012). Additionally, in arid and semi-arid regions where the water table is deep, most of hydrological processes take place in the thick unsaturated zone (Lekula and Lubczynski 2019), and therefore, use of point measurements of groundwater levels may not be sufficient to constrain IHMs. Thus, additional constraints, including state variables that take into account the unsaturated zone, are required to reduce uncertainty of IHM predictions. [SOURCE: Page_118]

The main objective of this study was to apply a multi-objective approach to calibrate an IHM of STAS using GRACE TWS as an additional calibration target in order to compensate for the insufficient groundwater level measurements. To achieve this, a two-step calibration process was adopted i.e., the IHM was first calibrated on hydraulic heads and then on both hydraulic heads and GRACE TWS simultaneously, and the two models were compared in order to assess the impact of incorporating GRACE TWS as an additional calibration target. Since the water table in most part of STAS is quite deep, only piezometers located along the upper reaches of the fossil rivers, the western and south-western parts of STAS, where the water table is relatively shallow, were sensitive to UZF parameters. [SOURCE: Page_118]

The Integrated Hydrological Model (IHM) simulations indicated that, as anticipated, the storage anomaly within the unsaturated zone constitutes the majority of the total change in water storage in the Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System (STAS). This phenomenon can be attributed to several factors inherent to this arid region, including a thick unsaturated zone hence substantial storage capacity for infiltrated water; high evapotranspiration demands which leads to significant evaporation of the infiltrated water as it traverses through the unsaturated zone, before it reaches the water table; low soil moisture retention capacity; and infrequent recharge events resulting from limited rainfall. As a result, the UZF parameters of the IHM play a major role of controlling the sub-surface water fluxes especially in arid and semi-arid areas such as STAS. However, since most studies do not incorporate state variables for the unsaturated zone, optimizing these parameters is quite challenging and therefore some studies (e.g., Hassan et al. 2014; Lekula and Lubczynski 2019; Daoud et al. 2022) obtained the values for these parameters from literature and were not optimised during calibration. [SOURCE: Page_118]

117 [SOURCE: Page_118]

The inclusion of GRACE TWS as an additional calibration target in the Integrated Hydrological Model (IHM) for STAS revealed that neglecting the optimization of UZF parameters can lead to significant underestimations of gross and net recharge. This aligns with findings by Bai et al. (2018), who demonstrated that incorporating GRACE data enhances parameter calibration and improves the accuracy of hydrological simulations. However, it was also observed that using GRACE TWS alone to constrain an IHM may pose a risk of over-estimating both gross and net recharge since to obtain a good match between IHM simulated change in storage and GRACE TWS, there should be no lag between infiltration and evapotranspiration from the unsaturated zone which form the main input and output into the unsaturated zone respectively. Achieving this required increasing the saturated hydraulic conductivity, residual and extinction water content, and decreasing the saturated water content and evapotranspiration extinction depth, all of which favour release of more water from the unsaturated zone to the water table as gross recharge. This in turn resulted to a rise in simulated groundwater levels especially in the recharge zones. This aligns with findings by Su et al. (2022), who demonstrated that groundwater storage anomalies based only on hydrological models and GRACE data may have greater uncertainties which may be difficult to verify especially in regions where groundwater level measurements are limited. It is therefore important to use GRACE TWS together with other state variables such as groundwater measurements or actual evapotranspiration to constrain the IHM (Bai et al. 2018). [SOURCE: Page_119]

Both GRACE-derived TWS anomaly and model-simulated change in sub-surface storage showed the climatic variability in STAS as seen in rainfall data, but the peaks of IHM ∆S were more subdued. The results of this study also show that gross recharge (Rg) was highly dependent on rainfall and depth to water table occurring mainly in areas where the water table is less than 30 m below the ground level. On the other hand, groundwater evapotranspiration was generally low throughout the simulation period, occurring mainly along the upper reaches of the fossil river channels and the south-western part of STAS where the Dwyka Group outcrops and the water table is very shallow. Groundwater exfiltration was very low occurring in very few grids along the upper reaches of Nossob River. This study also demonstrates the applicability of a well calibrated IHM to monitor the trend in variations of groundwater storage in arid and semi- arid areas, hence, their applicability in assessing impact of climate change on sustainability of groundwater resources of STAS. The results of this study will aid in informed decision making and outlining of aquifer management strategies by stakeholder countries and eventually prevent any conflicts over the water resources. [SOURCE: Page_119]

5.4.2. Recommendations and future perspective Due to high model computational demand which is time consuming, a coarse grid of 25 km^2 was used in this study which made it impossible to accurately simulate the role of linear features such as river channels and faults which play a major in recharge and discharge mechanisms of STAS. It is therefore recommended to use finer grids or unstructured grids, in future, to better represent these features. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Sensitivity analysis of the calibrated model showed that the partitioning of effective precipitation into various unsaturated zone and groundwater fluxes is highly dependent on model parameters. This means that model parameterization is a major source of uncertainty. Investigation and mapping of the soil (θr, θs and Ksat) and landcover (Dext) parameters at basin scale, as well as pumping tests to determine aquifer properties would reduce these uncertainties which in turn would reduce the uncertainty of the model output. Additionally, the model showed [SOURCE: Page_119]

118 [SOURCE: Page_119]

that both gross and net recharge are highly dependent on precipitation. While several remote sensing products were evaluated in order to select the most appropriate for this study, the evaluation was based on limited in situ measurements with none available within the study area. Running the calibrated model with various combinations of precipitation and PET could give an idea of how the discrepancies in various remote sensing products translate in the model, in terms of the simulated recharge, water budget and groundwater levels. Actual evapotranspiration could also be used as additional model constraints in order to improve confidence of the calibrated model. [SOURCE: Page_120]

5.5. Conclusion Arid and semi-arid regions such as STAS are typically characterised by a thick unsaturated zone and hence are prone to complex hydrologic dynamics which affect the rate and amount of groundwater recharge. This requires that all the important hydrologic processes be simulated simultaneously in an integrated framework for more accurate estimation of net recharge. IHMs require spatial-temporally distributed input data such as precipitation, PET and interception and other ancillary data such as soil physical properties and landcover used to define model parameters. Typically, such data on the ground are scarce or unavailable, particularly in remote areas of developing countries such as STAS. This study presents an application of RS data in the setting of an IHM of a multi-layered aquifer system characterised by a thick unsaturated zone to provide the quantitative assessment of subsurface water balance components and change in storage. It was noted that: [SOURCE: Page_120]

1. Unlike the standalone MODFLOW model, the UZF-MODFLOW$_6$ IHM simulates the fluxes exchanged between the land surface model and groundwater, hence yielding more information on the water budget of the aquifer system 2. Incorporation of GRACE TWS as an additional calibration target for the IHM helped constrain UZF parameters which improved the simulated subsurface water fluxes. 3. For IHM simulated storage change to match GRACE TWS, there should be no lag between infiltration and evapotranspiration in the unsaturated zone. GRACE data therefore indicates that most effective infiltration is quickly returned to the atmosphere just after it enters the unsaturated zone. 4. The unsaturated zone accounts for the majority of change in subsurface storage in STAS, as it stores infiltrated water that may evaporate before reaching the water table. This may be the case in other aquifer systems with comparable properties. [SOURCE: Page_120]

119 [SOURCE: Page_120]

6.      Synthesis: Application of remote sensing and IHM for assessment of groundwater resources in arid and data scarce Stampriet Transboundary Aquifer System [SOURCE: Page_121]

6.1. Introduction Arid and semi-arid areas like STAS are mostly characterised by high evapotranspiration, low to medium rainfall, a thick unsaturated zone and little to no surface water and groundwater is the main if not the sole source of water. Over-exploitation of groundwater in these areas can cause declining groundwater levels resulting into desertification. As a result, sustainable groundwater resource management must take into account both human and natural water consumption. The assessment of groundwater balance serves as the foundation for developing a sustainable groundwater usage strategy. In areas where the water table is deep, the thick unsaturated zone plays an important role in water balance and groundwater flux dynamics because it stores a significant amount of infiltrated water most of which is lost by evapotranspiration and does not reach the water table. The thick unsaturated zone also influences the time lag of actual recharge. Groundwater resource assessments in these locations must take into account the effects of the unsaturated zone, therefore analytical methods such as the water table fluctuation (WTF) method or classic standalone groundwater models are deemed insufficient tools. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Distributed integrated hydrological models (IHMs) are the most effective tools for studying and analyzing water balance dynamics and predicting aquifer responses to groundwater abstraction and climate, and/or land use changes in such areas (Keese et al. 2005), as they include all features characterizing the system. However, IHMs are usually data intensive and this kind of data is not always available especially in developing countries. In the recent decades remote sensing has gained popularity as a handy tool for water resources assessment and monitoring. For instance, remote sensing is a robust tool for monitoring and evaluating different phenomena one earth such as rainfall, evapotranspiration, land use and land cover changes, terrestrial water storage among others. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Additionally, ability of IHMs to reproduce aquifer processes is highly dependent on the reliability and accuracy of hydrogeological conceptual model. In this respect, three aspects were important in the assessment of water resources of STAS. First, a hydrogeological conceptual model (Chapter 3) was developed based on the topographic, geological and hydrogeological data. Secondly, since rainfall is the most important driving force in IHM, thirteen gridded precipitation products (eleven satellite based and one re-analysis) were evaluated against rain gauge measurements in order to select the most suitable for use in the IHM of STAS (Chapter 4). Thirdly, an IHM of STAS was constructed mainly using remote sensing data and calibrated on field measurements of groundwater levels and GRACE TWS (Chapter 5). [SOURCE: Page_121]

6.2. Hydrogeological conceptual model The hydrogeology of the Namibian part of STAS is quite well understood from an intensive study carried out by JICA (2002). This formed the foundation of the hydrogeological conceptualization in this study which sought to investigate how far the aquifer system extends into Botswana and South Africa and to establish the links with the neighbouring Central Kalahari Basin (Lekula et al. 2018a) . Hydrogeological conceptualization of the whole Kalahari Karoo Basin was carried out, with more focus on the Aranos, South-West Botswana and Kalahari Gemsbok sub-basins. [SOURCE: Page_121]

120 [SOURCE: Page_121]

One important feature of a HCM is a hydrostratigraphic model detailing the geometry and spatial extent of hydrostratigraphic units. The hydrostratigraphic model of STAS was developed using Rockworks (2020) 3-D geological modelling code together with ArcGIS iteratively. The hydrostratigraphic units were defined using geological and hydrogeological data such as lithological logs and information on the hydrogeological importance of respective stratigraphic units following JICA (2002) in Aranos sub-basin in Namibia, WRC (2008), DGS (2000) and ORASECOM (2022) in South-West Botswana sub-basin and van Wyk (1987a, b), Levin (1980) and Rose and Van Wyk (2006) in Kalahari Gemsbok sub-basin in South Africa. Hydrostratigraphic classification and harmonization was done across the three sub-basins as presented in Table 2-1 and in 3-D schematization of the HCM (Figure 3-9) consisting of six hydrostratigraphic units including from the top Kalahari Layer, Inter-Karoo Aquitard, Auob Aquifer, Mukorob Aquitard, Nossob Aquifer and Dwyka Aquifer and none is spatially continuous. Based on the available piezometric data inside and outside the Kalahari Karoo basin two groundwater flow systems were identified i.e., north-west to north-east into CKB and north- west to south-east into South Africa. [SOURCE: Page_122]

6.3. Remote sensing for assessment of groundwater resources of STAS Rainfall is the most important input of an IHM. Ground based measurements of rainfall are usually point measurements taken by rain gauges, which are then spatialised through interpolation. However, there are very few rain gauges in STAS with a lot of gaps in the data. Several gridded precipitation products (GPPs) based on either satellite data, re-analysis of climatic data or a combination of both, with some integrated with rain gauge station data have been developed in the recent past. However, the GPPs have varying degrees of accuracy in different places and, therefore, need to be evaluated, in order to select the most optimal one for a particular use. Due to erratic nature of the rainfall pattern in this region, data with relatively high spatial resolution is required as input to the distributed IHM at daily scale [SOURCE: Page_122]

Thirteen GPPs spanning from 2001 or earlier with spatial resolution ≥ 0.250 and daily temporal resolution or higher were thus downloaded for the whole of Namibia and Botswana for evaluation in order to select the best performing one for use in the IHM. The selected GPPs include TMPA, IMERG, CHIRP, CHIRPS, MSWEP with and without gauge, CMORPH-DLY, CMORPH-30min, RFE, ARC, PERSIANN-CCS-CDR, TAMSAT, ERA$_5$-Land. The idea to evaluate the GPPs for such a large area was motivated by the lack of rain gauges with sufficient data in STAS. Apart from the northern parts of both countries which are quite humid, the rest of the region is classified as arid and semi-arid and therefore, a GPP that performs well in the whole region would be considered the best for STAS. The GPPs were evaluated against 37 daily rain gauge records for a period of four hydrological years i.e., September 2015 to August 2019 for which rain gauge data was available. The number of rain gauges was quite few for such a large region but they were well distributed spatially and therefore were considered to be representative of the region. [SOURCE: Page_122]

The GPPs were evaluated based on categorical and continuous metrics and at daily and seasonal scale. The bias was also decomposed into respective error components i.e., hit, miss and false biases and the mean squared error was decomposed into systematic and random errors, to identify the most dominant error in each GPP. In that assessment, it was found that MSWEP performed best based on all the criterion used while ERA$_5$-Land performed quite well in terms of rainfall detection capability with a high probability of detection and low miss bias but [SOURCE: Page_122]

121 [SOURCE: Page_122]

performed the poorest in all the other criteria as it over-estimated correctly detected rainfall as shown by the large hit bias and had quite large false bias. CHIRPS performed quite poorly in terms of rainfall detection capability but performed second best after MSWEP quantitatively, as shown by the continuous metrics. [SOURCE: Page_123]

The findings of this study revealed that, while GPPs can be utilized as complementary rainfall data sources, particularly in data sparse places such as STAS, they must be reviewed before use, and the optimum metrics are case-specific. For instance, CHIRPS and MSWEP would be appropriate for some applications such as hydrological water balance assessment which require precipitation estimates to be accurate, as depicted by RMSE and the total bias (TB) which are an indication of their ability to give accurate precipitation estimates quantitatively. On the other hand, for certain calendar-related applications, such as agricultural activities, the timing of rainfall episodes is more important than precise estimation. The Critical Success Index (CSI) and Pearson correlation coefficient (r) indicate that MSWEP and CMORPH are better suited for this application. MSWEP is most suited for applications such as drought detection and flood monitoring, where the timing of rainfall occurrences and the accuracy of forecasts are critical for establishing early warning systems and disaster management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_123]

PET is required as the second driving force to set an upper limit of evapotranspiration in order to allow the IHM to compute the actual evapotranspiration. IHMs are less sensitive to PET than to rainfall, hence the performance of RS PET products was not examined in this study. Despite the fact that PET is less spatially variable than rainfall (Lekula and Lubczynski 2019), high- resolution data are necessary for the IHM to ensure proper spatial representation. Since there is no sufficient in situ data within the study area to calculate PET, various remote sensing PET products including (i) CRU PET (Harris et al. 2020) and (ii) PT-JPL (Fisher 2018) at spatial resolution of 0.50 at monthly scale; (iii) FEWSNET PET at 0.10 and daily scale; (iv) MOD16 (Running et al. 2019) at 500 m every 8 days; (v) GLEAM (Martens et al. 2017) at 0.250 and daily scale; and hPET (Singer et al. 2021) at 0.10 every hour, were assessed in order to select the most suitable product to use in the IHM. The IHM requires daily data, hence only FEWSNET, GLEAM, and hPET were applicable in this study, to eliminate the uncertainties associated with downscaling. The hPET product, with the highest spatial (0.10) and temporal resolution (hourly), was chosen. [SOURCE: Page_123]

The interception rate estimated by Kagusi et al. (2023) for STAS was used to calculate estimate effective precipitation and effective PET which are the actual driving forces of the IHM.Other RS datasets used in this study include ancillary data such as: (i) digital elevation model used to define the top of the first layer for both HCM and IHM; (ii) land use and land cover maps used to define evapotranspiration extinction depths; and (iii) soil map used to define UZF parameters such as initial, residual, extinction and saturated water contents as well saturated vertical hydraulic conductivity [SOURCE: Page_123]

6.4.   Application of IHM and remote sensing data for assessment of groundwater resources of STAS and its implication on groundwater management Application of IHM to investigate the water resources of data scarce STAS made it possible to utilize remote sensing data, both as input and calibration target. This is because, in contrast to the traditional standalone groundwater models which require Rg and ETg as the driving forces, IHMs require effective rainfall (Pe) and effective potential evapotranspiration (PETe) as the [SOURCE: Page_123]

122 [SOURCE: Page_123]

driving forces, which are well quantifiable through remote sensing while Rg and ETg are quantified internally based on the model parameterization. [SOURCE: Page_124]

IHM simulations revealed that the majority of subsurface storage change in the STAS is attributed to the unsaturated zone, as expected in arid regions with a thick unsaturated layer. Both GRACE-derived TWS anomalies and model-simulated changes in subsurface storage reflected the region’s climatic variability, closely following rainfall patterns, although the IHM-simulated storage changes were more subdued compared to the GRACE TWS peaks. The study also highlighted that gross recharge (Rg) was heavily dependent on rainfall and depth to the water table, with recharge primarily occurring in areas where the water table was less than 30 meters below ground level. Groundwater evapotranspiration was generally low during the simulation period, confined to the western part of STAS and the upper reaches of the fossil river channels where the water table is relatively shallow as well as the southwestern part of STAS, particularly where the Dwyka Group (fractured aquifer) outcrops. Groundwater exfiltration was minimal, occurring only in a few grids along the upper reaches of the Nossob River. [SOURCE: Page_124]

This study demonstrated that UZF (Unsaturated Zone Flow) parameters significantly influence subsurface water fluxes, especially in basins with a thick unsaturated zone, such as the STAS. Typically, UZF parameters are adopted from the literature and are not optimized during calibration, as most Integrated Hydrological Models (IHMs) are calibrated solely based on groundwater levels and stream discharge, where available. This makes optimizing UZF parameters challenging due to the lack of observations to properly account for the unsaturated zone’s fluxes. The incorporation of GRACE Total Water Storage (TWS) as an additional calibration target in the STAS IHM revealed that failing to optimize UZF parameters can lead to an underestimation of both gross and net recharge. However, relying solely on GRACE TWS for calibration introduces the risk of overestimating recharge. This occurs because, for the IHM- simulated change in storage to align with GRACE TWS observations, there must be no lag between actual infiltration and evapotranspiration in the unsaturated zone—its main input and output. [SOURCE: Page_124]

To achieve this alignment, the model required adjustments to UZF parameters, such as increasing the saturated hydraulic conductivity, residual water content, and extinction water content, while decreasing the saturated water content and evapotranspiration extinction depth. These changes promoted a faster release of water from the unsaturated zone to the water table, increasing simulated gross recharge and raising groundwater levels, particularly in recharge zones. Consequently, GRACE TWS should be used alongside other state variables, such as groundwater level measurements and actual evapotranspiration data, to better constrain IHMs, particularly in regions where storage changes in the unsaturated zone account for the majority of subsurface water dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_124]

The findings demonstrate that a well-calibrated IHM can effectively monitor trends in groundwater storage changes in arid and semi-arid regions, making it a valuable tool for assessing the impacts of climate change on groundwater sustainability in STAS. These results can support member countries in making informed decisions, shaping aquifer management strategies, and ultimately preventing potential water resource conflicts. [SOURCE: Page_124]

123 [SOURCE: Page_124]

6.5. Uncertainty and limitations, recommendations and future work Due to high model computational demand, a coarse grid of 25 km^2 was used in this study which made it impossible to accurately simulate the role of linear features such as river channels and faults which play a major role in recharge and discharge mechanisms of STAS. It is therefore recommended to use finer grids or unstructured grids, in future, to better represent these features. [SOURCE: Page_125]

The sensitivity analysis of the calibrated model revealed that the partitioning of effective precipitation into various unsaturated zone and groundwater fluxes is highly sensitive to model parameters, indicating that parameterization is a significant source of uncertainty. Reducing these uncertainties requires detailed investigation and mapping of key soil properties, such as residual moisture content (θr), saturated moisture content (θs), and saturated hydraulic conductivity (Ksat), as well as land cover parameters like extinction depth, across the basin. Additionally, conducting pumping tests to better determine aquifer properties would further enhance the accuracy of the model. The model also demonstrated that both gross and net recharge are strongly influenced by precipitation variability. [SOURCE: Page_125]

While several remote sensing datasets were evaluated to select the most suitable for this study, the selection was based on limited in-situ measurements, with no direct observations available within the study area. Running the calibrated model using various combinations of precipitation and potential evapotranspiration (PET) data would provide insight into how discrepancies in remote sensing products affect the simulated recharge, water budget, and groundwater levels. Installing rain gauges throughout the STAS region would not only validate gridded rainfall products but also improve their accuracy. Moreover, increasing the number of daily piezometric observations, particularly in Botswana and the South African parts of the study area, would better constrain the model, improving the precision of the results. Incorporating additional data, such as actual evapotranspiration and soil moisture, as model constraints could further increase the confidence in the calibrated model. [SOURCE: Page_125]

124 [SOURCE: Page_125]
